ela|1|l1-1|k-2|Words Are Made of Sounds — Every word is made of small sounds put together.
ela|1|l1-2|k-2|The First Sound — The first sound is the sound you hear at the start of a word.
ela|1|l1-3|k-2|Last Sound, Middle Sound — Words have a last sound and a middle sound too.
ela|1|l1-4|k-2|Words That Rhyme — Words rhyme when their ends sound the same.
ela|1|l1-5|k-2|Clap the Parts — You can clap the beats in a word to count its parts.
ela|1|l1-6|k-2|Blend the Sounds — Put sounds together in a row and they make a word.
ela|1|l1-7|k-2|Take a Word Apart — You can break a word into its sounds, one at a time.
ela|1|l1-8|k-2|Change One Sound — Change one sound in a word and you get a new word.
ela|1|l2-1|k-2|A Name and a Sound — Every letter has a name and a sound, and the sound is what you read.
ela|1|l2-2|k-2|Big Letters, Small Letters — Each letter comes in a capital and a small form, and both say the same sound.
ela|1|l2-3|k-2|Vowels and Consonants — Five letters are vowels, and every word needs at least one.
ela|1|l2-4|k-2|Short Vowels — In a short word like cat, the vowel says its short sound.
ela|1|l2-5|k-2|Two Letters, One Sound — Some letter pairs, like sh, ch and th, make one new sound together.
ela|1|l2-6|k-2|The Silent e — An e at the end of a word makes the vowel say its name.
ela|1|l2-7|k-2|Read Left to Right — Read the letters in order from left to right, and you get the right word.
ela|1|l2-8|k-2|Write the Letters in Order — The alphabet has 26 letters in a set order, and you can write each one.
ela|10|l22-1|6-8|Telling and Showing — Writers reveal character two ways: by telling you directly, or by showing actions, words, looks and thoughts and letting you decide.
ela|10|l22-2|6-8|What a Character Wants — Motivation is the reason behind a character's actions, and finding it explains choices that look strange from outside.
ela|10|l22-3|6-8|Hearing Character in Dialogue — How a character talks, including word choice, what they avoid saying, and how they treat others, reveals who they are.
ela|10|l22-4|6-8|Static and Dynamic Characters — A dynamic character changes inside during the story; a static character stays the same, and both kinds have jobs to do.
ela|10|l22-5|6-8|Conflict Inside — Internal conflict is a struggle within one character between two things they want, fear, or believe.
ela|10|l22-6|6-8|Conflict Outside — External conflict is a struggle between a character and something outside them: another person, society, nature, or fate.
ela|10|l22-7|6-8|First Person — In first-person point of view, a character tells the story as "I," so you get their thoughts but only what they know and choose to say.
ela|10|l22-8|6-8|Third Person: Limited and Omniscient — Third-person narrators use he, she and they; a limited narrator stays inside one character's mind, while an omniscient narrator can enter any mind.
ela|10|l22-9|6-8|Narrators You Cannot Trust — An unreliable narrator tells the story in a way the reader should doubt, and the clues to that doubt are part of the story's meaning.
ela|10|l22-10|6-8|Inference With Evidence — An inference about a character is a claim; to make it count, cite the exact words from the text that support it and explain the link.
ela|10|l22-11|6-8|Following One Character Through a Book — To understand a character in a whole novel, track them at the start, at turning points, and at the end, and compare the evidence.
ela|10|l23-1|6-8|Five Parts of a Plot — Most plots move through exposition, rising action, climax, falling action, and resolution, and naming those parts helps you see how a story is built.
ela|10|l23-2|6-8|Why the Climax Comes Late — Writers place the climax near the end and build to it so that the resolution feels earned, and the placement shapes how a reader feels.
ela|10|l23-3|6-8|Subplots — A subplot is a smaller story running beside the main one, and it usually reflects or pushes the main plot.
ela|10|l23-4|6-8|Flashback and Foreshadowing — Flashback jumps backward to show earlier events; foreshadowing plants hints about events to come.
ela|10|l23-5|6-8|Setting That Does Work — Setting is when and where a story happens, and in good writing it shapes characters, creates conflict, and builds mood.
ela|10|l23-6|6-8|Theme, Topic and Moral — A topic is what a story is about in a word; a theme is what it says about that topic in a sentence; a moral is a lesson it orders you to follow.
ela|10|l23-7|6-8|How a Theme Grows — Theme is not stated once; it develops across a whole text through repeated situations, changing characters, and the ending.
ela|10|l23-8|6-8|Symbols and Motifs — A symbol is a thing in a story that stands for a larger idea; a motif is a repeated element that builds meaning; both need evidence, not guesswork.
ela|10|l23-9|6-8|The Book and the Movie — Comparing a novel to its film shows what each form can and cannot do, and every change a filmmaker makes is a choice worth explaining.
ela|10|l23-10|6-8|Writing About Theme — A strong theme paragraph states the theme as a sentence, supports it with specific evidence from the text, and explains how the evidence shows the theme.
ela|10|l24-1|6-8|Building Reading Stamina — Reading a long book is a skill you build with steady time, a plan, and honest strategies for the pages that are hard.
ela|10|l24-2|6-8|Marking the Page — Annotating means writing brief notes, marks, and questions on or beside a text so that it becomes a conversation instead of a wall of words.
ela|10|l24-3|6-8|Keeping a Reading Journal — A reading journal records what you noticed, asked, and predicted as you read, so your first reading already contains the second.
ela|10|l24-4|6-8|Asking the Text Questions — Strong readers interrogate a text with questions about what the writer chose and why, not only what happened.
ela|10|l24-5|6-8|Realistic and Historical Fiction — Realistic fiction invents people and events that could happen now; historical fiction invents them inside a real past, and each has rules readers expect.
ela|10|l24-6|6-8|Fantasy and Mystery — Fantasy builds a world with its own consistent rules; mystery sets a puzzle and plays fair with the clues, and readers judge each by its own conventions.
ela|10|l24-7|6-8|Memoir: A True Story Shaped — A memoir is a true account of part of the author's own life, shaped like a story, and readers judge it by both its honesty and its craft.
ela|10|l24-8|6-8|O. Henry and the Turn — O. Henry's stories build to a surprise ending, and reading one closely shows how a writer plants the turn while keeping you looking elsewhere.
ela|10|l24-9|6-8|Jack London and the Cold — In "To Build a Fire," London uses a limited narrator and precise physical detail to turn a walk in the cold into a study of judgment and pride.
ela|10|l24-10|6-8|Kate Chopin and the Hour — Chopin's "The Story of an Hour" packs a whole life into one hour and one room, and its irony depends on the reader knowing what the characters do not.
ela|10|l24-11|6-8|Giving a Book Talk — A book talk is a short, planned spoken pitch that makes listeners want to read a book, built from a hook, a taste, and a reason, without spoiling the ending.
ela|10|l24-12|6-8|Books You Can Read and Books That Stretch You — Strong readers read a mix of books they can read easily and books that stretch them, and they use simple tests to tell the difference before committing.
ela|11|l25-1|6-8|Topic Is Not the Same as Idea — A topic is what a text is about; the central idea is what the text says about that topic, and it is always a full sentence.
ela|11|l25-2|6-8|How Details Build an Idea — Writers develop a central idea with details such as facts, examples, numbers and quotations, and each detail should push the idea forward.
ela|11|l25-3|6-8|The Objective Summary — An objective summary restates a text's central idea and key details in your own words, without adding your opinion.
ela|11|l25-4|6-8|Four Ways to Organize — Nonfiction writers organize text in patterns such as time order, cause and effect, compare and contrast, and problem and solution, and signal words reveal the pattern.
ela|11|l25-5|6-8|Why This Order? — Analyzing structure means asking why a writer put a paragraph or section where it is, and what the text would lose if it moved.
ela|11|l25-6|6-8|Word Choice and Tone — Tone is the writer's attitude toward the subject, and you hear it in the exact words the writer chose.
ela|11|l25-7|6-8|Two Authors, One Topic — When two authors write about the same topic, compare what each includes, leaves out, and emphasizes, and you will understand the topic better than either author alone.
ela|11|l25-8|6-8|Letters, Speeches and Diaries — A primary source is a text made by someone who was there, and it is powerful evidence as long as you remember who wrote it, for whom, and why.
ela|11|l25-9|6-8|Graphs and Pictures With the Words — Graphs, maps and photos carry information the words do not, and reading them well means checking labels, scale and what was left out.
ela|11|l25-10|6-8|Fact or Opinion? — A fact can be checked and shown true or false; an opinion expresses a belief or feeling that cannot be proved, though it can be argued.
ela|11|l25-11|6-8|Reasoned Judgment — A reasoned judgment is a conclusion built from facts and clear reasoning, stronger than a plain opinion but still open to argument.
ela|11|l26-1|6-8|A Claim You Can Defend — A claim is a debatable statement that an argument sets out to prove, and a strong claim is clear, specific and possible to disagree with.
ela|11|l26-2|6-8|Evidence Is What You Can Point To — Evidence is the facts, data, examples and expert statements that support a claim, and it must come from somewhere a reader could check.
ela|11|l26-3|6-8|Reasoning Connects the Two — Reasoning is the explanation of why a piece of evidence supports the claim, and without it, evidence just sits there.
ela|11|l26-4|6-8|Tracing an Argument — To trace an argument, find the claim, list each piece of evidence, and check the reasoning that links them; then you can judge whether the argument holds.
ela|11|l26-5|6-8|Relevant and Sufficient — Evidence must be relevant, meaning it connects to the claim, and sufficient, meaning there is enough of it to carry the claim's weight.
ela|11|l26-6|6-8|Counterclaims and Rebuttal — A strong argument states the other side's best point fairly and then answers it; that answer is the rebuttal.
ela|11|l26-7|6-8|Three Fallacies to Spot — A fallacy is a mistake in reasoning that makes an argument sound good without really supporting it; bandwagon, ad hominem and false choice are three of the most common.
ela|11|l26-8|6-8|The Shape of the Essay — An argumentative essay has an introduction with a claim, body paragraphs that each give evidence and reasoning, a counterclaim paragraph with a rebuttal, and a conclusion.
ela|11|l26-9|6-8|Transitions and a Formal Voice — Transitions show the reader how ideas connect, and a formal style keeps the focus on the argument instead of on the writer's feelings.
ela|11|l26-10|6-8|How a Classroom Debate Runs — A debate is an argument with rules: two sides, timed turns, a stated resolution, and judges who score evidence and reasoning rather than volume.
ela|11|l26-11|6-8|Listening and Answering — In a debate the rebuttal turn is won by listening closely, writing down the other side's exact points, and answering them one by one with evidence.
ela|11|l27-1|6-8|Who Made This, and Why? — Before trusting a source, find out who made it and what they wanted from you, because author and purpose shape everything in it.
ela|11|l27-2|6-8|Date and Evidence — A good source is recent enough for its topic and shows where its own facts came from; check both before you rely on it.
ela|11|l27-3|6-8|Reading Sideways — Lateral reading means leaving a source to check what other, independent sources say about it, which is faster and more reliable than judging a page by its own looks.
ela|11|l27-4|6-8|Bias and Point of View — Every source comes from a perspective, and bias is when that perspective bends what is reported; spotting it means asking what was left out and how it was worded.
ela|11|l27-5|6-8|What a Headline and a Photo Do — Headlines and images are chosen to grab you before you read, so check whether they match what the article actually says and where the picture really came from.
ela|11|l27-6|6-8|Paraphrase or Plagiarism? — Paraphrasing means restating a source's idea fully in your own words and structure and still giving credit; copying words or just swapping a few is plagiarism.
ela|11|l27-7|6-8|Quoting Well — Quote when the exact words matter, keep quotations short, put them in quotation marks, and add an in-text citation so a reader can find the source.
ela|11|l27-8|6-8|The Works Cited Page — A works cited page lists every source you used, in alphabetical order, in a set format, so any reader can find what you found.
ela|11|l27-9|6-8|A Question and Good Notes — Research starts with a question that is narrow enough to answer, and note cards that keep the source, the fact and your own thinking separate.
ela|11|l27-10|6-8|Sharing What You Found — Presenting research means turning your findings into a clear claim with a few strong pieces of evidence, shown on slides or a poster that support your talk instead of replacing it.
ela|12|l28-1|6-8|Stems, Prefixes and Suffixes — Many long English words are built from a stem that carries the core meaning, plus a prefix in front or a suffix behind.
ela|12|l28-2|6-8|Two Old Languages in English — English borrowed stems from Latin and from Greek, and knowing which language a stem comes from helps you spot where it shows up.
ela|12|l28-3|6-8|bio, geo and graph — Bio means life, geo means earth and graph means write or draw; together they build dozens of words in science and beyond.
ela|12|l28-4|6-8|log, chron and phon — Log means word or study, chron means time and phon means sound; they name subjects, clocks and everything that has to do with sound.
ela|12|l28-5|6-8|spec and port — Spec means look and port means carry; both are Latin stems that appear in hundreds of everyday words.
ela|12|l28-6|6-8|rupt, ject and dict — Rupt means break, ject means throw and dict means say; each is a Latin stem for a strong action.
ela|12|l28-7|6-8|cred, bene and mal — Cred means believe, bene means good or well, and mal means bad; these stems tell you whether a word is about trust, help or harm.
ela|12|l28-8|6-8|anti, auto and tele — Anti means against, auto means self and tele means far; these three Greek prefixes appear on the front of many modern words.
ela|12|l28-9|6-8|micro, poly and hydro — Micro means small, poly means many and hydro means water; they fill science words in biology, chemistry, geography and math.
ela|12|l28-10|6-8|Word Families Across Subjects — A single stem links words in science, math, history and art, so learning a word in one class helps you read another.
ela|12|l28-11|6-8|Unlocking a Test Word — When a strange word appears on a test or in a textbook, take it apart, build a meaning from the parts, then check it against the sentence.
ela|12|l29-1|6-8|Denotation and Connotation — A word's denotation is its dictionary meaning, and its connotation is the feeling or association that rides along with it.
ela|12|l29-2|6-8|Word Choice and Tone — Tone is the writer's attitude toward the subject, and it is built largely from the connotations of the words the writer chooses.
ela|12|l29-3|6-8|A Thesaurus, Used Wisely — A thesaurus lists words with similar meanings, but its words are not interchangeable, so check the connotation and the sentence before you swap.
ela|12|l29-4|6-8|Simile — A simile compares two unlike things using like or as, and the point of the comparison is to make one thing clearer or more vivid.
ela|12|l29-5|6-8|Metaphor — A metaphor says one thing is another, without like or as, so the reader carries the qualities of one over onto the other.
ela|12|l29-6|6-8|Personification — Personification gives human actions, feelings or qualities to something that is not human, so the reader relates to it as if it were alive.
ela|12|l29-7|6-8|Hyperbole — Hyperbole is deliberate exaggeration for effect; the reader is not supposed to believe it literally but to feel the size of the feeling.
ela|12|l29-8|6-8|Allusion — An allusion is a brief reference to a well-known story, person, place or event that the reader is expected to recognize.
ela|12|l29-9|6-8|Idiom — An idiom is a fixed expression whose meaning cannot be figured out from its individual words, so it must be learned as a whole.
ela|12|l29-10|6-8|Analogies — An analogy explains something unfamiliar by comparing it to something familiar, and on tests it appears as a pair of word pairs with the same relationship.
ela|12|l29-11|6-8|Figures in Poems and Ads — Poets and advertisers both use figurative language, and interpreting a figure means finding the comparison and asking what feeling or idea it delivers.
ela|12|l29-12|6-8|Precise Language in Your Writing — Strong writing uses exact words and fresh figures where they help, and cuts vague words, worn-out figures and exaggeration that readers will not believe.
ela|13|l30-1|6-8|Phrases and Clauses — A clause has a subject and a verb; a phrase is a group of words that does not.
ela|13|l30-2|6-8|Independent and Dependent Clauses — An independent clause can stand alone as a sentence; a dependent clause has a subject and verb but needs a main clause to lean on.
ela|13|l30-3|6-8|Simple and Compound Sentences — A simple sentence is one independent clause; a compound sentence joins two independent clauses with a comma and a coordinating conjunction, or with a semicolon.
ela|13|l30-4|6-8|Complex Sentences — A complex sentence joins an independent clause with at least one dependent clause; put a comma after the dependent clause when it comes first.
ela|13|l30-5|6-8|Compound-Complex Sentences — A compound-complex sentence has at least two independent clauses and at least one dependent clause; count the clauses to name any sentence.
ela|13|l30-6|6-8|Combining for Variety — Combining short sentences with conjunctions, dependent clauses and phrases makes writing flow; varying length tells the reader which idea is big.
ela|13|l30-7|6-8|Fragments — A fragment is a piece of a sentence punctuated as if it were whole; fix it by attaching it to a neighbor or adding what is missing.
ela|13|l30-8|6-8|Run-ons and Comma Splices — A run-on jams two independent clauses together with nothing or only a comma; fix it with a period, a semicolon, a conjunction, or by making one clause dependent.
ela|13|l30-9|6-8|Active and Passive Voice — In active voice the subject does the action; in passive voice the subject receives it. Prefer active unless the doer is unknown or unimportant.
ela|13|l30-10|6-8|Verb Moods — A verb's mood signals whether the sentence states, commands, asks, imagines a condition, or expresses a wish or demand.
ela|13|l30-11|6-8|Pronoun Case and Agreement — Choose I or me, he or him, by the pronoun's job in the sentence, and make every pronoun match the noun it stands for.
ela|13|l30-12|6-8|Dangling Modifiers — A modifier must sit next to the word it describes; a dangling modifier describes something not in the sentence, and a misplaced one attaches to the wrong word.
ela|13|l31-1|6-8|Series and Introductory Commas — Use commas to separate items in a series and to set off an introductory word, phrase or clause from the main sentence.
ela|13|l31-2|6-8|Extra Information Commas — Set off with commas any clause or phrase that only adds information; leave out the commas when the words are needed to tell which one.
ela|13|l31-3|6-8|Commas Between Adjectives — Put a comma between two adjectives when each describes the noun equally and could be joined by and; do not use one when the first adjective describes the second plus the noun.
ela|13|l31-4|6-8|Semicolons and Colons — A semicolon joins two closely related independent clauses; a colon follows a complete sentence to introduce a list, an explanation or a quotation.
ela|13|l31-5|6-8|Dashes and Parentheses — Dashes and parentheses both set off an interruption; a dash makes it loud and sudden, while parentheses make it quiet and aside.
ela|13|l31-6|6-8|Apostrophes — An apostrophe shows ownership or a missing letter; it never makes a word plural.
ela|13|l31-7|6-8|Quotation Marks — Quotation marks wrap a speaker's exact words and the titles of short works; commas and periods go inside the closing mark, and a new speaker starts a new paragraph.
ela|13|l31-8|6-8|Capital Letters — Capitalize the first word of a sentence, the pronoun I, and proper nouns, the names of specific people, places and things; leave common nouns lowercase.
ela|13|l31-9|6-8|Words That Sound Alike — Words that sound alike, such as affect and effect or than and then, are chosen by meaning and job, not by sound; a short test settles each one.
ela|13|l31-10|6-8|Spelling Patterns — Most English spelling follows patterns, such as dropping a silent e or doubling a final consonant before -ing; learn the pattern, then memorize the few words that break it.
ela|13|l31-11|6-8|Style Guides and Meaning — When rules are contested, a style guide settles them for a publication; whatever guide you follow, every mark you place changes what a reader takes from the sentence.
ela|14|l32-1|6-8|A Claim You Can Argue — A thesis is one sentence that takes a position a reasonable person could disagree with, and the whole essay exists to support it.
ela|14|l32-2|6-8|Making a Thesis Specific — A strong thesis names a precise position and hints at the reasons, so the reader knows exactly what the essay promises to prove.
ela|14|l32-3|6-8|The Introduction — An introduction earns the reader's attention, gives the background they need, and ends with the thesis.
ela|14|l32-4|6-8|Body Paragraphs and Topic Sentences — Each body paragraph makes one point that supports the thesis, states it in a topic sentence, and then proves it.
ela|14|l32-5|6-8|The Conclusion — A conclusion restates the argument in fresh words and shows the reader why it matters beyond the essay.
ela|14|l32-6|6-8|Bringing in Evidence and Quotations — Evidence must be introduced, quoted or summarized accurately, and credited, so the reader knows where it came from and why it is there.
ela|14|l32-7|6-8|Explaining Evidence — Evidence does not speak for itself; after every piece of evidence, the writer must explain how it proves the point.
ela|14|l32-8|6-8|Formal Style and Objective Tone — Argument and informative essays use a formal style and an objective tone so the reader focuses on the evidence, not the writer's feelings.
ela|14|l32-9|6-8|Compare, Contrast, Cause and Effect — Informative essays explain rather than argue, and two of their most useful shapes are compare-and-contrast and cause-and-effect.
ela|14|l32-10|6-8|Defining a Term — A definition essay explains what a word or idea really means by placing it in a class, separating it from its neighbors, and showing examples.
ela|14|l32-11|6-8|Real Revision — Revision means re-seeing the whole essay and changing what it says and how it is built, not just fixing commas.
ela|14|l32-12|6-8|Writing Under a Time Limit — When the clock is running, spend the first minutes planning a thesis and three reasons, and save the last minutes to check what you wrote.
ela|14|l33-1|6-8|Openings That Pull — A strong narrative opening drops the reader into a specific moment and raises a question they want answered.
ela|14|l33-2|6-8|Dialogue That Sounds Real — Good dialogue sounds like real speech, is punctuated correctly, and does work: it shows character, moves the story, or raises tension.
ela|14|l33-3|6-8|Speeding Up and Slowing Down — Pacing is how fast a story moves; short sentences and skipped time speed it up, while long sentences and close detail slow it down.
ela|14|l33-4|6-8|Senses and Precise Verbs — Sensory details and exact verbs let the reader experience a scene instead of being told about it.
ela|14|l33-5|6-8|Who Tells It — Choosing first or third person, and how much the narrator knows, changes what the reader can see and whom they trust.
ela|14|l33-6|6-8|Reflection and the Earned Ending — A narrative ends well when something has changed and the narrator briefly shows what it meant, without a tacked-on moral.
ela|14|l33-7|6-8|Writing a Personal Narrative — A personal narrative tells one true moment from your life in scene, with detail and reflection, and its truth is in the details, not the drama.
ela|14|l33-8|6-8|Writing a Fictional Narrative — Fiction needs a character who wants something, an obstacle, and a change, and the writer invents details as carefully as a memoirist remembers them.
ela|14|l33-9|6-8|Line Breaks and Images — A poem is made of lines, and where a line ends controls speed, emphasis and meaning; images give the reader something to see.
ela|14|l33-10|6-8|Sound in a Poem — Poems are built for the ear; rhythm, repeated sounds and rhyme make lines memorable and shape how they feel.
ela|14|l33-11|6-8|Haiku, Free Verse and the Sonnet — Poetic forms are sets of rules about lines, syllables and rhyme, and each form pushes the poet toward a different kind of attention.
ela|14|l33-12|6-8|Reading Aloud and the Anthology — Reading your work aloud reveals what the page hides, and gathering a class's work into an anthology makes writing public and real.
ela|15|l34-1|9-10|What the Text Says — Close reading starts with the plain facts of the text: who, what, where, and in what order, before any interpretation.
ela|15|l34-2|9-10|How the Text Says It — Once you know what a passage says, look at how it says it: word choice, sentence length, images, and what is repeated or left out.
ela|15|l34-3|9-10|Why the Writer Chose It — The last step of close reading asks why: what effect does a choice have on the reader, and what would be lost if the writer had done it differently?
ela|15|l34-4|9-10|Complex Characters — A complex character wants more than one thing, changes across the story, and cannot be summed up in one word.
ela|15|l34-5|9-10|Conflicting Motivations — Motivation is the reason a character acts; when two motivations pull in opposite directions, the story's conflict often lives inside one person.
ela|15|l34-6|9-10|Point of View — Who tells the story, and how much they know, shapes everything the reader is allowed to see.
ela|15|l34-7|9-10|Cultural Context — Knowing when and where a story was written helps you see what its characters could and could not do, and why the writer's choices were bold.
ela|15|l34-8|9-10|Theme Built From Details — A theme is an idea about life that a story develops; you find it by tracing how specific details keep pointing the same way.
ela|15|l34-9|9-10|Order and Pacing — Writers control the order in which events are told and how fast they move, and both choices shape what the reader feels.
ela|15|l34-10|9-10|Parallel Plots and Pattern — Some stories run two lines of action side by side, or repeat a pattern, so that one part comments on another.
ela|15|l34-11|9-10|Claim and Evidence — A literary analysis states a claim that could be argued, then supports it with specific evidence from the text.
ela|15|l34-12|9-10|Quoting and Explaining — Quote only the words you need, fit them into your own sentence, and always explain what they show.
ela|15|l35-1|9-10|Lines and Stanzas — A poem is built from lines and stanzas, and where a line breaks is a choice that changes how the words are read.
ela|15|l35-2|9-10|Meter and Rhythm — Meter is a poem's pattern of stressed and unstressed syllables, and the most common English pattern is the ten-syllable line called iambic pentameter.
ela|15|l35-3|9-10|Sound Devices — Poets repeat sounds on purpose, through rhyme, alliteration and assonance, so that the ear connects words the mind might not.
ela|15|l35-4|9-10|Imagery — Imagery is language that makes the reader see, hear, smell, taste or touch something, and it is the poem's main way of making feeling physical.
ela|15|l35-5|9-10|Figurative Language — Metaphor, simile and personification say one thing is another, or is like another, so that the reader understands both differently.
ela|15|l35-6|9-10|The Speaker Is Not the Poet — The voice in a poem is a speaker the poet created, and the two may agree, disagree or be worlds apart.
ela|15|l35-7|9-10|Hearing the Tone — Tone is the speaker's attitude toward the subject, and you hear it in word choice, rhythm and what is left unsaid.
ela|15|l35-8|9-10|The Turn — Many poems pivot at a certain line, changing tone, direction or meaning, and finding that turn is the key to reading the poem.
ela|15|l35-9|9-10|Structure Makes Meaning — The shape of a poem, its stanzas, repetitions and ending, is not a container for the meaning; it is part of the meaning.
ela|15|l35-10|9-10|Paraphrase First — Before you analyze a poem, put it into plain prose in your own words so that you know what it literally says.
ela|15|l35-11|9-10|Writing About a Poem — A strong paragraph about a poem makes one claim about how the poem works, quotes the exact words that show it, and explains the effect.
ela|15|l36-1|9-10|Acts and Scenes — A play is divided into acts and scenes, and the divisions mark changes in time, place and pressure that a reader should track.
ela|15|l36-2|9-10|Dialogue and Stage Directions — A play has no narrator; everything a reader learns comes from what characters say and from the stage directions that tell bodies what to do.
ela|15|l36-3|9-10|Soliloquy and Aside — In a soliloquy a character alone on stage speaks their real thoughts; in an aside a character speaks to the audience without other characters hearing.
ela|15|l36-4|9-10|Decoding the Language — Shakespeare's English is hard for four fixable reasons: old pronouns, shifted word meanings, reordered sentences and dense figures of speech.
ela|15|l36-5|9-10|Character Through Speech — Since a play has no narrator, a character's way of speaking, in prose or verse, plain or ornate, is the main evidence of who they are.
ela|15|l36-6|9-10|Dramatic Irony — Dramatic irony is when the audience knows something a character does not, and it turns ordinary lines into knives.
ela|15|l36-7|9-10|The Shape of Tragedy — A tragedy follows a person of standing whose own choice, driven by a flaw, leads to a downfall the audience sees coming and cannot stop.
ela|15|l36-8|9-10|The Point of No Return — Every tragedy has a moment after which nothing can be fixed, and reading closely around that moment shows what the play is about.
ela|15|l36-9|9-10|Performance Choices — A script leaves most decisions open, and every production makes choices about voice, movement, pause and setting that change the scene's meaning.
ela|15|l36-10|9-10|A Scene on Its Feet — Standing up and playing a scene reveals things about it that silent reading hides: who has power, where the bodies go, and what the silences hold.
ela|15|l36-11|9-10|Comparing Page and Stage — Comparing a scene as written with a scene as performed shows which meanings are in the text and which a production added, cut or changed.
ela|16|l37-1|9-10|Ethos: Why Should I Trust You? — Ethos is the trust an audience places in a speaker, and writers build it on purpose.
ela|16|l37-2|9-10|Pathos: Moving the Heart — Pathos is an appeal to the audience's emotions, and it works through pictures, stories and chosen words.
ela|16|l37-3|9-10|Logos: The Chain of Reasons — Logos is the appeal to reason: claims backed by evidence and linked by logic the audience can follow.
ela|16|l37-4|9-10|The Rhetorical Situation — Every act of persuasion happens in a situation: a speaker, an audience, a purpose and an occasion that shape every choice.
ela|16|l37-5|9-10|Structure That Serves a Purpose — The order of a speech is a choice, and a strong structure carries the audience from where they are to where the speaker wants them.
ela|16|l37-6|9-10|Word Choice Under Pressure — In a great speech, single words carry the argument, and swapping one for a near synonym changes what the speech does.
ela|16|l37-7|9-10|Repetition and Parallelism — Repeating a word or a grammatical shape makes an idea easier to hear, remember and feel.
ela|16|l37-8|9-10|Antithesis and the Rhetorical Question — Setting opposites side by side, or asking a question the audience must answer in their heads, pulls listeners into the argument.
ela|16|l37-9|9-10|Two Voices for Freedom — Douglass and Sojourner Truth used very different tools to argue for the same thing, and the difference came from their situations.
ela|16|l37-10|9-10|Founding Documents as Arguments — The Declaration of Independence is a structured argument, and later speakers borrowed its ethos by quoting it.
ela|16|l37-11|9-10|Writing a Rhetorical Analysis — A rhetorical analysis explains how a text persuades, not whether you agree with it, and it does so with quoted evidence.
ela|16|l38-1|9-10|Tone Is Not Mood — Tone is the writer's attitude toward the subject; mood is the feeling the text creates in the reader, and the two can differ.
ela|16|l38-2|9-10|Diction Builds Tone — Tone comes mostly from word choice: connotation, level of formality and how concrete or abstract the words are.
ela|16|l38-3|9-10|Syntax and Voice — Sentence length and shape, called syntax, give a writer's voice its rhythm and can change tone as much as words do.
ela|16|l38-4|9-10|Irony: Saying the Opposite — Verbal irony means the opposite of what the words say, and readers detect it through context and tone clues.
ela|16|l38-5|9-10|Satire: Laughing to Wound — Satire uses irony, exaggeration and mockery to expose a real fault, and it always has a target and a purpose.
ela|16|l38-6|9-10|Understatement and Overstatement — Saying less than the truth or far more than it are both tone tools, and both depend on the reader knowing the real size of the thing.
ela|16|l38-7|9-10|Formal and Informal Registers — A register is the level of language a situation calls for, and choosing the wrong one is a tone mistake even when every word is correct.
ela|16|l38-8|9-10|Tone in Letters and Editorials — Persuasive nonfiction, from an editorial to a letter, chooses a tone to fit its reader, and the tone is part of the argument.
ela|16|l38-9|9-10|When the Tone Shifts — Many texts change tone partway through, and the moment of the shift is usually where the meaning turns.
ela|16|l38-10|9-10|Reading Twain for Voice — Twain's voice comes from plain diction, talking syntax and a deadpan turn, and studying it shows how a voice is made of choices.
ela|16|l38-11|9-10|Developing a Voice of Your Own — Your writing voice is built from choices you already make in speech, and you strengthen it by writing plainly, reading aloud and cutting what is not yours.
ela|16|l39-1|9-10|Ads, Editorials and Speeches — Different kinds of persuasive text follow different rules, and a reader judges each by what its form can and cannot do.
ela|16|l39-2|9-10|How Good Is the Evidence? — Evidence is not all equal: ask where it came from, how much of it there is, how recent it is, and whether it actually supports the claim.
ela|16|l39-3|9-10|Finding the Assumption — Every argument rests on something unstated, and finding that assumption is often the fastest way to test the argument.
ela|16|l39-4|9-10|Slippery Slope and Straw Man — A slippery slope predicts disaster without showing each step; a straw man attacks a weaker version of the other side's argument.
ela|16|l39-5|9-10|Hasty Generalization and False Cause — Jumping from too few cases to a rule, or from one thing following another to one thing causing another, are two of the most natural mistakes in reasoning.
ela|16|l39-6|9-10|Appeal to Authority and Other Shortcuts — Citing an authority is fine when the authority is real and relevant; it becomes a fallacy when the name replaces the reasoning.
ela|16|l39-7|9-10|The Rhetorical Situation of a Feed — Social media is a rhetorical situation with its own audience, purpose and pressures, and those pressures reward the weakest kinds of argument.
ela|16|l39-8|9-10|Checking Before You Share — Sharing a claim is making it, so check the source, the date and the original context before passing it on.
ela|16|l39-9|9-10|Anticipating the Objection — A strong written argument states the best objection to itself fairly and answers it, which is called a counterclaim and rebuttal.
ela|16|l39-10|9-10|Disagreeing Well — Civil disagreement is not softness; it is a set of moves that keep the argument on the claim and give the other person a reason to keep listening.
ela|16|l39-11|9-10|A Structured Debate — A formal debate gives each side equal time, a fixed order and a duty to answer, which forces the moves this chapter has taught.
ela|17|l40-1|9-10|Five Kinds of Context Clues — Writers often plant the meaning of a hard word nearby, and there are five common places to look for it.
ela|17|l40-2|9-10|When the Clue Is Far Away — Sometimes no single sentence explains a word, and you must gather hints from the whole paragraph or passage.
ela|17|l40-3|9-10|Roots That Carry Meaning — A few dozen Latin and Greek roots appear in thousands of academic words, and knowing the root gives you a foothold.
ela|17|l40-4|9-10|Prefixes and Suffixes at Work — A prefix changes a word's meaning at the front, and a suffix often changes its job in the sentence at the back.
ela|17|l40-5|9-10|How Words Change Meaning — Words drift over centuries, and knowing where a word came from helps you remember it and use it well.
ela|17|l40-6|9-10|Cognates Across Languages — If you speak Spanish or another language built from Latin, thousands of English academic words are already half yours.
ela|17|l40-7|9-10|Science Words With Everyday Twins — Many science terms are ordinary words with a precise second meaning, and you must switch meanings when you switch rooms.
ela|17|l40-8|9-10|History and Math Words — History and math also borrow ordinary words and sharpen them, so read the subject's meaning, not the street meaning.
ela|17|l40-9|9-10|Choosing the Exact Word — Near-synonyms are not equal, and the precise word tells the reader more in less space.
ela|17|l40-10|9-10|Three Reference Tools — A dictionary, a thesaurus and an etymology dictionary answer three different questions, and the skill is knowing which to open.
ela|17|l40-11|9-10|Building Your Own Word List — A personal word list works when each entry holds the word, its meaning in your words, a sentence and a hook to remember it.
ela|17|l41-1|9-10|Metaphor and Simile, Closer Up — A metaphor has two parts, the thing meant and the image used, and the meaning lives in what the two share.
ela|17|l41-2|9-10|Extended Metaphor and Conceit — When a writer keeps one comparison going through a whole poem or passage, every line adds a new point of contact.
ela|17|l41-3|9-10|Symbols — A symbol is a real thing in a text that also stands for an idea, and you know it is a symbol because the text keeps returning to it.
ela|17|l41-4|9-10|Allegory: The Whole Story Stands for Something — An allegory is a story in which the characters and events line up point by point with a second meaning outside the story.
ela|17|l41-5|9-10|Three Kinds of Irony — Irony is a gap between what is said or expected and what is true, and it comes in verbal, situational and dramatic forms.
ela|17|l41-6|9-10|Paradox and Oxymoron — A paradox seems to contradict itself but holds a truth, and an oxymoron packs that contradiction into two words.
ela|17|l41-7|9-10|Allusion and How to Look One Up — An allusion borrows a famous story in a few words, and a reader who does not know the story can find it in a minute.
ela|17|l41-8|9-10|Euphemism: The Softer Word — A euphemism replaces a blunt word with a gentler one, and readers should ask whether it is kindness or a way to hide the truth.
ela|17|l41-9|9-10|Reading a Figure in Its Context — The same figure of speech can mean different things in different passages, so always read it against the sentences around it.
ela|17|l41-10|9-10|What a Figure Does to Tone — Figurative language changes not only what a sentence means but how it feels, and naming that feeling is the heart of analysis.
ela|17|l41-11|9-10|Using Figures in Your Own Writing — A fresh, consistent figure strengthens your writing, while a cliché or a mixed metaphor weakens it.
ela|18|l42-1|9-10|Phrases That Name and Act — A phrase is a group of words that works as one unit in a sentence but has no subject and verb of its own.
ela|18|l42-2|9-10|Phrases That Describe — Adjectival, adverbial and participial phrases add description, and each one has to sit next to the word it describes.
ela|18|l42-3|9-10|The Absolute Phrase — An absolute phrase is a noun plus a participle that describes the whole sentence rather than one word in it.
ela|18|l42-4|9-10|Independent and Dependent Clauses — Every clause has a subject and a verb, but only an independent clause can stand alone as a sentence.
ela|18|l42-5|9-10|Parallel Structure — Items that do the same job in a sentence should have the same grammatical shape.
ela|18|l42-6|9-10|Pairs, Comparisons and Lists — Paired connectors like not only and but also, and comparisons with than or as, demand parallel parts on both sides.
ela|18|l42-7|9-10|Sentence Variety on Purpose — Good writers vary sentence length and openings on purpose, so that rhythm carries meaning.
ela|18|l42-8|9-10|Subject-Verb Agreement in Hard Cases — A verb agrees with the head of its subject, not with whatever noun happens to be closest to it.
ela|18|l42-9|9-10|Pronouns That Point Clearly — Every pronoun must point to one clear noun, and it must match that noun in number and person.
ela|18|l42-10|9-10|Modifiers in the Right Place — A modifier describes the word nearest to it, so put each one next to the word it belongs with.
ela|18|l42-11|9-10|Conventions and When to Break Them — Usage conventions are agreements among readers; a writer follows them by default and breaks one only on purpose, for an effect.
ela|18|l42-12|9-10|Editing a Real Draft — Editing is a set of separate passes, each hunting one kind of problem, done after the ideas are in place.
ela|18|l43-1|9-10|The Semicolon — A semicolon joins two complete sentences that belong together, and it separates items in a list whose items already contain commas.
ela|18|l43-2|9-10|The Colon — A colon follows a complete sentence and announces what comes next: a list, an explanation or a quotation.
ela|18|l43-3|9-10|Dashes and Parentheses — A dash breaks a sentence for emphasis or a sudden turn; parentheses tuck in a quiet aside.
ela|18|l43-4|9-10|Commas That Change the Meaning — A pair of commas around a clause says the clause is extra; no commas say the clause is needed to identify the noun.
ela|18|l43-5|9-10|Other Commas That Matter — Commas mark direct address, separate list items, and set off introductions, and skipping one can change who or what a sentence means.
ela|18|l43-6|9-10|Hyphens — A hyphen joins words that work together as one unit, especially two-word descriptions placed before a noun.
ela|18|l43-7|9-10|Punctuating Dialogue — Quotation marks hold exactly what a speaker said, and commas, periods and speaker tags follow a fixed set of rules.
ela|18|l43-8|9-10|Quotations Inside Quotations — In American usage, single quotation marks show a quotation inside another quotation, and every quoted word must be exactly what the source said.
ela|18|l43-9|9-10|Ellipses and Brackets — An ellipsis shows where you left words out of a quotation, and brackets show where you added or changed a word; both keep the reader honest.
ela|18|l43-10|9-10|Capitalization and Titles — Capitalize the first word of a sentence, proper nouns and the main words of titles, and know that italics and quotation marks tell readers what kind of title it is.
ela|18|l43-11|9-10|Spelling in the Age of Autocorrect — Autocorrect fixes typos but not wrong words, so a writer still has to know the words that sound alike and proofread with human eyes.
ela|18|l43-12|9-10|MLA Format and Citations — MLA style tells a reader where your evidence came from through short in-text citations and a matching Works Cited list.
ela|19|l44-1|9-10|Narrowing a Topic to a Question — A topic is a subject; a research question is a specific thing you want to find out, and only the question can guide a search.
ela|19|l44-2|9-10|Searching Like a Researcher — Good searching means choosing precise keywords, using search tools on purpose, and knowing when to leave the open web for a library database.
ela|19|l44-3|9-10|Authority and Accuracy — Ask who is behind a source and whether its claims can be checked; a source that hides its author or its evidence has not earned your trust.
ela|19|l44-4|9-10|Currency and Purpose — Check when a source was made and why; a date tells you whether the information is still good, and a purpose tells you which way it might lean.
ela|19|l44-5|9-10|Primary and Secondary Sources — A primary source comes from the time or the person you are studying; a secondary source studies primary sources, and a strong paper uses both.
ela|19|l44-6|9-10|Quote, Paraphrase, Summarize — Take notes in three modes, quoting exact words, paraphrasing in your own words, or summarizing the whole, and mark which mode each note is in.
ela|19|l44-7|9-10|The Source Log — Record every source's full details the moment you find it, in one place, so that nothing you use is ever lost or uncitable.
ela|19|l44-8|9-10|Synthesis, Not Stacking — Synthesis puts sources in conversation around your own idea; stacking just lines them up one after another with no thinking between them.
ela|19|l44-9|9-10|What Plagiarism Is — Plagiarism is presenting someone else's words or ideas as your own, on purpose or by accident, and the cure is careful notes and clear credit.
ela|19|l44-10|9-10|MLA In-Text Citations — In MLA style, a short note in parentheses, usually the author's last name and a page number, points the reader to the full entry in the works cited list.
ela|19|l44-11|9-10|The Works Cited List — A works cited list gives full details for every source cited, in alphabetical order, so a reader can find each one.
ela|19|l44-12|9-10|The Annotated Bibliography — An annotated bibliography adds a short paragraph under each citation that summarizes the source and judges how useful it is.
ela|19|l45-1|9-10|A Claim About How a Text Means — A literary analysis argues a claim about how a text creates its meaning, not just what it is about.
ela|19|l45-2|9-10|Evidence and Commentary — Each piece of textual evidence needs to be introduced, quoted precisely, and then explained; the explanation is where the analysis lives.
ela|19|l45-3|9-10|Organize by Idea, Not by Plot — Arrange body paragraphs by the reasons that support your claim, not by the order of events in the text.
ela|19|l45-4|9-10|Argument With a Counterclaim — An argument essay states a claim, supports it with evidence and reasoning, and takes the strongest opposing view seriously before answering it.
ela|19|l45-5|9-10|Explaining With Structure and Precision — An informative essay explains a subject clearly by choosing a structure that fits the material and using precise, defined terms.
ela|19|l45-6|9-10|Narrative Technique — A narrative uses specific techniques, such as pacing, dialogue, sensory detail and reflection, to make a reader live an experience rather than hear about it.
ela|19|l45-7|9-10|Introductions That Earn Attention — An introduction should start with something specific, move toward the subject, and end with the claim; it should never start with a dictionary or a statement about all of history.
ela|19|l45-8|9-10|Conclusions That Do More — A conclusion should show what the argument adds up to, answering why it matters, rather than repeating the introduction in different words.
ela|19|l45-9|9-10|Sentences With Style — Style at the sentence level comes from choices you can control: strong verbs, concrete nouns, varied length, and putting the important word where the reader will feel it.
ela|19|l45-10|9-10|Revising for Clarity and Concision — Revision means cutting words that do no work and rebuilding sentences a reader could misread; it is a separate pass from proofreading.
ela|19|l45-11|9-10|Writing on Demand — A timed essay rewards a plan: read the prompt closely, budget minutes, outline in three lines, and leave time to reread.
ela|19|l45-12|9-10|Building a Portfolio — A writing portfolio collects your best work across kinds of writing, with reflections that show what you learned, so growth becomes visible.
ela|2|l3-1|k-2|Words That Come Up Everywhere — Some words come up so often that you should know them the second you see them.
ela|2|l3-2|k-2|Tricky Words — Some words do not follow the sound rules, so you learn them by heart.
ela|2|l3-3|k-2|The -at Family — Words that end the same way are a family, and one family helps you read many words.
ela|2|l3-4|k-2|Adding -s, -ed and -ing — A small ending on a word changes when it happened or how many there are.
ela|2|l3-5|k-2|Two Words Make One — Some long words are really two small words stuck together.
ela|2|l3-6|k-2|Read Like You Talk — Good reading sounds like talking, not like a robot.
ela|2|l3-7|k-2|Read It Again — Reading the same book again makes you faster each time.
ela|2|l4-1|k-2|Who and What Happened — A sentence tells who or what, and what happened.
ela|2|l4-2|k-2|A Capital and an End Mark — A sentence starts with a capital letter and ends with a mark.
ela|2|l4-3|k-2|Naming Words — A noun names a person, a place or a thing.
ela|2|l4-4|k-2|Action Words — A verb tells what someone or something does.
ela|2|l4-5|k-2|Describing Words — A describing word tells more about a noun, like its size or color.
ela|2|l4-6|k-2|Telling and Asking — A telling sentence ends with a period, and an asking sentence ends with a question mark.
ela|2|l4-7|k-2|Words in Order — The words in a sentence have to be in an order that makes sense.
ela|2|l4-8|k-2|One Good Sentence — To write one good sentence, look at a picture, say who and what happened, and leave spaces.
ela|20|l46-1|11-12|Structure Carries Meaning — The order in which a text gives you its events is a choice, and the choice changes what the events mean.
ela|20|l46-2|11-12|The Narrator You Cannot Trust — An unreliable narrator tells a story the reader must read against, using the teller's own slips as evidence.
ela|20|l46-3|11-12|Setting and Order as Choices — Where and when a writer places a story, and in what order the reader meets its parts, are decisions that shape everything the story can mean.
ela|20|l46-4|11-12|Three Kinds of Irony — Irony is a gap between what is said or expected and what is meant or true, and it comes in verbal, situational and dramatic forms.
ela|20|l46-5|11-12|Ambiguity on Purpose — Ambiguity is a text's deliberate openness to more than one meaning, and it differs from vagueness because each meaning is supported by the words.
ela|20|l46-6|11-12|Satire and Its Targets — Satire uses exaggeration, irony and mock seriousness to expose something the writer wants the reader to condemn.
ela|20|l46-7|11-12|Motif and Symbol — A motif is a repeated element that builds meaning across a text; a symbol is a concrete thing that stands for something larger.
ela|20|l46-8|11-12|Archetypes — An archetype is a character, situation or image that recurs across many cultures and centuries of stories, and recognizing it lets you see how a text uses or breaks the pattern.
ela|20|l46-9|11-12|Allusion — An allusion is a brief reference to another text, person or event that borrows that reference's meaning for the new text.
ela|20|l46-10|11-12|Stream of Consciousness — Stream of consciousness narration tries to put the reader inside a mind as it actually moves, with its jumps, repetitions and half-finished thoughts.
ela|20|l46-11|11-12|Weighing Two Readings — When a text supports more than one interpretation, the stronger reading is the one that accounts for more of the text and forces less of it.
ela|20|l46-12|11-12|Writing an Interpretation — An interpretive essay argues how a text means, with a debatable claim, evidence from the text, and reasoning that connects the two.
ela|20|l47-1|11-12|Anne Bradstreet and Puritan Poetry — The first published American poet was a Puritan woman whose plain, personal poems reveal both her faith and her doubts.
ela|20|l47-2|11-12|Jonathan Edwards and the Sermon — Edwards's famous sermon uses vivid images and relentless logic to make an abstract doctrine feel like physical danger.
ela|20|l47-3|11-12|Paine and the Pamphlet — Thomas Paine's pamphlets turned the argument for independence into plain, urgent prose that ordinary readers could repeat.
ela|20|l47-4|11-12|Emerson, Thoreau and the Self — The Transcendentalists argued that truth comes through the individual's own mind and conscience, not through institutions or tradition.
ela|20|l47-5|11-12|The Dark Side of Romanticism — Hawthorne, Poe and Melville shared the Romantics' interest in the inner life but saw guilt, madness and obsession where Emerson saw light.
ela|20|l47-6|11-12|Whitman and Dickinson — Whitman and Dickinson invented two opposite poetic voices, one vast and public, one compressed and private, and both broke the rules of verse.
ela|20|l47-7|11-12|Frederick Douglass Learns to Read — Douglass's Narrative uses the story of his own literacy to argue that slavery depends on keeping minds in the dark.
ela|20|l47-8|11-12|Harriet Jacobs and the Hidden Years — Jacobs's narrative tells what Douglass's could not: how slavery bore down on women, and how she hid seven years in a tiny attic to free her children.
ela|20|l47-9|11-12|Twain and the Realist Turn — Realism after the Civil War aimed to show ordinary life and ordinary speech truthfully, and Twain made a boy's dialect carry a moral argument.
ela|20|l47-10|11-12|Naturalism: Crane, London, Chopin, Wharton — Naturalist writers showed people shaped and often crushed by forces they cannot control: nature, society, heredity and chance.
ela|20|l47-11|11-12|Dunbar and the Voices to Come — Paul Laurence Dunbar became the first widely famous Black American poet, and his poem about the mask points toward the Harlem Renaissance.
ela|21|l48-1|11-12|Levels of Diction — Diction is word choice, and every word sits at a level, from formal to casual, that tells the reader how to take the sentence.
ela|21|l48-2|11-12|Connotation Inside a Sentence — A word's connotation is the feeling it carries beyond its definition, and one swapped word can turn a whole sentence toward praise or blame.
ela|21|l48-3|11-12|Register and Audience — Register is the level of language you choose for a particular audience and situation, and matching it is the first job of any writer.
ela|21|l48-4|11-12|Sentence Length and Pace — Syntax is sentence structure, and the simplest syntax choice, how long each sentence runs, sets the pace a reader feels.
ela|21|l48-5|11-12|Word Order and Inversion — English expects subject, then verb, then object; when a writer inverts that order, the moved word grabs attention.
ela|21|l48-6|11-12|Periodic and Loose Sentences — A loose sentence gives its main point first and adds on; a periodic sentence holds the main point until the end, building suspense.
ela|21|l48-7|11-12|Fragments on Purpose — A sentence fragment is an error when it is an accident and a tool when it is a choice, and skilled writers use fragments for emphasis.
ela|21|l48-8|11-12|Where the Weight Falls — Readers remember the end of a sentence most and the beginning next, so a writer controls emphasis by what goes in those two slots.
ela|21|l48-9|11-12|Analyzing a Passage Precisely — A precise style analysis names specific choices in diction and syntax, shows them in the text, and explains the effect each one creates.
ela|21|l48-10|11-12|Imitating a Style — Imitation, writing your own content in another writer's sentence shapes, is the fastest way to feel a style from the inside.
ela|21|l48-11|11-12|Voice as a Set of Choices — A writer's voice is not a mystery gift; it is the pattern of diction and syntax choices the writer makes over and over.
ela|21|l49-1|11-12|Redundancy and Filler — Redundancy says a thing twice and filler says nothing at all; both can be cut without losing a single idea.
ela|21|l49-2|11-12|Hidden Verbs and Weak Verbs — A nominalization is a verb turned into a noun; turning it back into a verb makes the sentence shorter, clearer and more alive.
ela|21|l49-3|11-12|Clarity and Precision — Concise writing is not only short; it is exact, choosing the one word that means what you mean instead of three that circle it.
ela|21|l49-4|11-12|Sentence Variety — Good prose varies sentence length and opening so that the reader hears a rhythm instead of a metronome.
ela|21|l49-5|11-12|Paragraph Coherence and Transitions — A paragraph coheres when each sentence begins with something the reader already knows and ends with something new, and transitions mark how ideas connect.
ela|21|l49-6|11-12|Two Styles, Both Legitimate — The plain style aims to be clear and unnoticed; the ornate style aims to be felt and admired; a skilled writer can do both and chooses on purpose.
ela|21|l49-7|11-12|Writing for Busy Readers — Most real-world readers are in a hurry, so writing for them means front-loading the point, breaking the page into pieces, and cutting anything they would skip.
ela|21|l49-8|11-12|Editing Someone Else's Paragraph — Editing another writer's work means improving it without replacing their voice, and it starts with understanding what they were trying to do.
ela|21|l49-9|11-12|A Style Checklist You Will Use — A short checklist run on every draft catches most style problems, and a checklist works only if it is short enough to actually run.
ela|22|l50-1|11-12|Deductive Reasoning — A deductive argument is built so that if its premises are true, its conclusion cannot be false.
ela|22|l50-2|11-12|Inductive Reasoning — Inductive reasoning moves from observed cases to a general conclusion that is likely, never certain.
ela|22|l50-3|11-12|Valid, Sound, or Neither — A valid argument has the right form; a sound argument has the right form and true premises.
ela|22|l50-4|11-12|The Premise Nobody Stated — Most real arguments rest on an unstated premise, and finding it is the fastest way to test them.
ela|22|l50-5|11-12|Four Kinds of Evidence — Anecdote, statistic, expert testimony and analogy each support a claim in a different way and each has a built-in limit.
ela|22|l50-6|11-12|Reading Numbers Honestly — A statistic or graph answers a precise question, and the reader's job is to find out which one.
ela|22|l50-7|11-12|Attacks That Miss the Point — Fallacies of relevance answer something other than the argument: the person, a distorted version, a distraction, or the crowd.
ela|22|l50-8|11-12|Leaps That Go Too Far — Fallacies of weak induction draw a conclusion far larger than the evidence can carry.
ela|22|l50-9|11-12|Traps Built Into the Words — Some fallacies hide in the wording itself: a forced choice, a claim that assumes its own conclusion, or a word that changes meaning midway.
ela|22|l50-10|11-12|Claim, Grounds, Warrant — The Toulmin model maps an argument as a claim, the grounds that support it, and the warrant that connects them.
ela|22|l50-11|11-12|Backing, Qualifier, Rebuttal — A complete argument backs its warrant, limits its claim honestly, and answers the strongest objection.
ela|22|l50-12|11-12|Judging an Argument Whole — To evaluate an argument, map its parts, test each part with the right question, and then say exactly what it proved.
ela|22|l51-1|11-12|A Syllogism for a Nation — The Declaration of Independence is built as a deductive argument: a general premise about rights, a factual premise about the king, and a conclusion that follows.
ela|22|l51-2|11-12|The Charges as Evidence — The Declaration's list of grievances is an inductive case built to prove a single factual premise about the king.
ela|22|l51-3|11-12|What the Draft Reveals — Jefferson's rough draft shows that the Declaration's premises were chosen and its most difficult paragraph was cut.
ela|22|l51-4|11-12|The Preamble's Purposes — The Preamble to the Constitution is one sentence that states who is acting, why, and what they are doing.
ela|22|l51-5|11-12|Federalist No. 10 and Faction — Madison's Federalist No. 10 argues by defining a problem, ruling out impossible cures, and showing a large republic is the remaining answer.
ela|22|l51-6|11-12|Federalist No. 51 and Ambition — Federalist No. 51 argues from a premise about human nature to a design in which each branch checks the others.
ela|22|l51-7|11-12|Seneca Falls Borrows the Form — The Declaration of Sentiments reused the Declaration of Independence's structure, changing one premise to reach a new conclusion.
ela|22|l51-8|11-12|Douglass and the Fourth of July — Douglass argued that the Declaration's premises condemned slavery, and used the nation's own holiday to make the contradiction visible.
ela|22|l51-9|11-12|Lincoln Rereads the Declaration — Lincoln's major speeches argue from the Declaration's premise, using compression and balanced sentences to make the logic memorable.
ela|22|l51-10|11-12|How Later Writers Argued Back — Later writers engaged the founding texts by granting a premise, exposing a contradiction, or contesting a definition, and each move is a model.
ela|22|l51-11|11-12|Your Argument, Their Premise — An argument in dialogue with a foundational text states the text's premise fairly, adds your grounds, and shows the conclusion follows.
ela|23|l52-1|11-12|The Semicolon Joins Equals — A semicolon joins two complete sentences that belong together, and it separates list items that already contain commas.
ela|23|l52-2|11-12|The Colon Points Forward — A colon announces that what follows explains or completes what came before, and the words before the colon must form a complete sentence.
ela|23|l52-3|11-12|Dashes for Interruption and Emphasis — The em dash interrupts a sentence or throws weight on what follows it; the en dash joins ranges; the hyphen joins words.
ela|23|l52-4|11-12|Parentheses Lower the Voice — Parentheses hold an aside the sentence can live without, and the sentence must still read correctly with the parentheses and their contents removed.
ela|23|l52-5|11-12|Brackets Inside Quotations — Square brackets mark words a writer has changed or added inside someone else's words, so the reader always knows which words belong to the source.
ela|23|l52-6|11-12|Commas in Complex Sentences — In long sentences, commas mark structure: after an introductory clause, around a nonrestrictive clause, and before a conjunction that joins two complete clauses.
ela|23|l52-7|11-12|The Serial Comma Debate — The serial comma before the last item of a list is required by some style guides and dropped by others, but every guide agrees that a list must not be misreadable.
ela|23|l52-8|11-12|Hyphens and Compound Modifiers — Hyphenate two or more words that act as one adjective before a noun, but not after the noun and not after an adverb ending in -ly.
ela|23|l52-9|11-12|Quotations and Ellipses — In scholarly writing, quotation marks, ellipses and citations work together so a reader knows exactly which words are the source's and exactly what was cut.
ela|23|l52-10|11-12|Titles in Italics and Quotation Marks — Italicize the titles of whole, standalone works and put the titles of short works and parts of larger works in quotation marks.
ela|23|l52-11|11-12|How Editors Decide — Professional editors follow a house style guide, keep a style sheet of decisions, and choose the punctuation that makes each sentence clearest for its readers.
ela|23|l53-1|11-12|Prescriptive and Descriptive — Prescriptive rules tell writers what they should do; descriptive study records what speakers and writers actually do; a skilled writer uses both.
ela|23|l53-2|11-12|Rules That Changed — Several famous rules, including the bans on split infinitives, sentence-ending prepositions and singular they, were never solid, and today's major guides have loosened or dropped them.
ela|23|l53-3|11-12|Usage Still Argued Today — Some usage questions, such as less versus fewer, who versus whom, and literally as an intensifier, remain genuinely contested, and the writer's best move is to know the dispute and choose for the reader.
ela|23|l53-4|11-12|Matching the Room — Register is the level of formality a writer chooses to fit the reader and the occasion, and shifting it on purpose is a skill, not a betrayal of your voice.
ela|23|l53-5|11-12|Email That Gets Answered — A good email has a subject line that says what it is, a greeting fitted to the reader, one clear request near the top, and a closing with your full name.
ela|23|l53-6|11-12|Applications and Cover Letters — A cover letter is a short argument that you fit a specific job, written in a formal register, with every claim backed by a detail and every word checked.
ela|23|l53-7|11-12|Errors That Cost Credibility — A short list of errors, mostly involving apostrophes, homophones and agreement, does most of the damage to a writer's credibility, and each one can be checked with a simple test.
ela|23|l53-8|11-12|The Ten-Minute Edit — When time is short, edit in passes with a fixed order: meaning first, then the personal error list, then the mechanical checks, then a final read aloud.
ela|23|l53-9|11-12|Settling a Usage Question — A usage question is settled by consulting the right reference in the right order: the dictionary for words, the style guide for format, a usage guide for disputes, and real examples for what writers actually do.
ela|23|l53-10|11-12|Language Variety — Every variety of English, including African American English and the Englishes of bilingual communities, has consistent grammar; Standard English is one variety among many, expected in certain settings.
ela|23|l53-11|11-12|Respect on the Page — Respectful usage means calling people and groups what they call themselves, getting names and titles right, and choosing words that describe rather than judge.
ela|24|l54-1|11-12|A Question Worth a Semester — A strong research question is open, specific and arguable, and it cannot be settled by a single lookup.
ela|24|l54-2|11-12|Making a Research Plan — A research plan turns a big question into a calendar of small tasks, and it fixes deadlines for reading, drafting and revising before you begin.
ela|24|l54-3|11-12|Scholarly Sources and Databases — Scholarly sources are written by experts, checked by other experts and found mostly through library databases rather than open web searches.
ela|24|l54-4|11-12|Reading Sources That Fight Back — Difficult scholarly texts are read in passes: first for the shape of the argument, then for the parts that answer your question, then closely.
ela|24|l54-5|11-12|Synthesis Across Sources — Synthesis means placing sources in conversation with each other, organized by idea rather than by source, so your own claim can emerge from their agreements and conflicts.
ela|24|l54-6|11-12|Organizing a Long Argument — A long argument holds together when each section answers one sub-question, the sections are ordered by logic, and every part points back to the thesis.
ela|24|l54-7|11-12|MLA and APA Formatting — MLA and APA are two systems for the same job, letting readers find your sources; MLA cites author and page, APA cites author and year.
ela|24|l54-8|11-12|Quotation, Paraphrase, Summary — Quote when the exact words matter, paraphrase when the idea matters, summarize when only the gist matters, and cite all three.
ela|24|l54-9|11-12|Drafting in Stages — Draft the body first, one section at a time, from the outline and the grid; write the introduction last, when you finally know what the paper says.
ela|24|l54-10|11-12|Revision With a Reader — Revision means re-seeing the whole argument through a reader's eyes; a real reader finds the places where the paper made sense only to you.
ela|24|l54-11|11-12|Academic Integrity — Academic integrity means every idea and every phrase that is not yours is marked as someone else's, so a reader can always tell your thinking from your sources.
ela|24|l55-1|11-12|Structure for the Ear — A listener cannot reread, so a talk needs fewer points, a clearer map, and more repetition than a paper.
ela|24|l55-2|11-12|Evidence on the Screen — Slides exist to show what words cannot, so each slide should carry one image, one number or one short quotation, never a paragraph to be read aloud.
ela|24|l55-3|11-12|Timing and Rehearsal — A talk that fits its time is built by rehearsing out loud with a clock, cutting until there is slack, and planning what to drop if time runs short.
ela|24|l55-4|11-12|Anticipating the Questions — The hard questions can be predicted from your own paper's weak points, and the best answers are prepared before anyone raises a hand.
ela|24|l55-5|11-12|Answering Without Folding — Answer a challenge by restating it fairly, conceding what is true, holding what the evidence supports, and marking clearly where each part ends.
ela|24|l55-6|11-12|Discussion at a High Level — A high-level discussion builds on what others said, names disagreements precisely, and moves the group toward a better answer than anyone brought in.
ela|24|l55-7|11-12|Listening to Evaluate — Evaluative listening tracks a speaker's claim, evidence and reasoning as they go by, and judges the argument rather than the delivery.
ela|24|l55-8|11-12|Feedback That Can Be Used — Useful feedback is specific, points at the work rather than the person, names one or two changes that matter most, and says what to keep.
ela|24|l55-9|11-12|Measuring Your Own Growth — Reflection compares specific earlier work with specific current work, names what changed and how, and sets the next thing to learn.
ela|24|l55-10|11-12|What to Carry Forward — The habits of this course, asking real questions, checking sources, crediting others, revising with readers and answering challenges honestly, are the same habits college and work reward.
ela|3|l5-1|k-2|Who Is in the Story — The characters are the people or animals a story is about.
ela|3|l5-2|k-2|Where and When It Happens — The setting is where and when a story happens.
ela|3|l5-3|k-2|What Happens — Events are the things that happen in a story, one after another.
ela|3|l5-4|k-2|What a Character Wants — Every character wants something, and that want moves the story.
ela|3|l5-5|k-2|How a Character Feels — Words and pictures show how a character feels.
ela|3|l5-6|k-2|The Problem and the Fix — Most stories have a problem, and the story shows how it gets solved.
ela|3|l5-7|k-2|Tell It in Order — To retell a story, say the events in order: first, next, then, last.
ela|3|l5-8|k-2|Ask Good Questions — Who, what, where, when and why questions help you understand a story.
ela|3|l5-9|k-2|Two Tales, One Lesson — Two versions of a tale can differ, but each story teaches a lesson.
ela|3|l6-1|k-2|Some Books Are Made Up — A story book is made up by the author, even if it feels real.
ela|3|l6-2|k-2|Some Books Are True — A true book gives facts about real things.
ela|3|l6-3|k-2|Headings Tell You What Is Next — A heading is a big title on a page that tells what that part is about.
ela|3|l6-4|k-2|Labels and Captions — Labels name the parts of a picture, and captions tell what the picture shows.
ela|3|l6-5|k-2|The Table of Contents — The table of contents lists the parts of a book and their page numbers.
ela|3|l6-6|k-2|Topic and Details — A true book has one main topic, and the details are the facts about it.
ela|3|l6-7|k-2|Ask, Then Find the Answer — Ask a question first, then use the book's parts to find the answer.
ela|3|l6-8|k-2|Two Books, One Animal — Two books can be about the same animal but tell it in different ways.
ela|3|l6-9|k-2|The Author and the Illustrator — The author writes the words, and the illustrator makes the pictures.
ela|4|l7-1|k-2|A Beginning, a Middle, an End — Every story has a beginning, a middle and an end.
ela|4|l7-2|k-2|Draw It First — Drawing your story first helps you know what to write.
ela|4|l7-3|k-2|Something That Really Happened — A small moment is a short, true story about one thing that happened to you.
ela|4|l7-4|k-2|First, Next, Then, Last — Order words like first, next, then and last show what happened in order.
ela|4|l7-5|k-2|Details a Reader Can See — Details tell what you saw, heard and felt so a reader can see it too.
ela|4|l7-6|k-2|What I Think and Why — An opinion tells what you think, and a reason tells why.
ela|4|l7-7|k-2|A Page of True Facts — A fact book page teaches a reader something true about one topic.
ela|4|l7-8|k-2|Fixing a Sentence — A sentence starts with a capital letter, has a space between words and ends with a mark.
ela|4|l8-1|k-2|Listen and Take Turns — Good talk means one person speaks while the others listen, and then you switch.
ela|4|l8-2|k-2|Ask When You Do Not Know — If you do not understand, ask a question; it helps you and the speaker.
ela|4|l8-3|k-2|Loud and Clear — Speak loud enough to reach the back, slow enough to be understood, and look up.
ela|4|l8-4|k-2|Tell It in Order — When you tell a story or give directions, say the steps in the order they happen.
ela|4|l8-5|k-2|Describe What You See — To describe a person, place or thing, tell details like size, color, sound and feel.
ela|4|l8-6|k-2|Share and Get Feedback — When you share your writing, listeners can tell you what they liked and what they wonder.
ela|4|l8-7|k-2|Words That Show Feelings — Feeling words like proud, scared and excited tell a reader what is inside you.
ela|4|l8-8|k-2|New Words from Books — Books give you new words; say them, and use them when they fit.
ela|5|l9-1|3-5|What un- Does to a Word — A prefix is a word part added to the front of a word, and un- flips the word to mean not or the opposite.
ela|5|l9-2|3-5|Again and Before: re- and pre- — The prefix re- means again or back, and the prefix pre- means before.
ela|5|l9-3|3-5|Not and Wrong: dis- and mis- — The prefix dis- means not or the opposite of, and mis- means wrong or badly.
ela|5|l9-4|3-5|Full of It or Without It — A suffix is a word part added to the end of a word; -ful means full of and -less means without.
ela|5|l9-5|3-5|How and Who: -ly and -er — The suffix -ly usually tells how something is done, and the suffix -er often names a person who does something or compares two things.
ela|5|l9-6|3-5|Turning Actions into Things — The suffix -tion turns an action word into a naming word for the act or the result.
ela|5|l9-7|3-5|Roots from Far Away — A root is a word part from Greek or Latin that carries the core meaning; graph means write and tele means far.
ela|5|l9-8|3-5|Carry, Say and Hear — The Latin roots port (carry), dict (say) and aud (hear) unlock many everyday words.
ela|5|l9-9|3-5|Parts Help You Spell — Knowing the word parts tells you how to spell a long word, because each part keeps its own spelling.
ela|5|l9-10|3-5|Build Your Own Words — You can build and read new words by snapping known parts together, and a word part wall keeps the parts where you can see them.
ela|5|l10-1|3-5|When the Sentence Defines It — A context clue is a hint in the words around an unknown word, and the easiest kind is a definition given right in the sentence.
ela|5|l10-2|3-5|Examples Point the Way — An example clue gives you samples of the unknown word so you can work out what group they belong to.
ela|5|l10-3|3-5|Same As or Opposite Of — A synonym clue gives a word that means the same, and a contrast clue gives a word that means the opposite.
ela|5|l10-4|3-5|Same Word, Different Jobs — Many words have more than one meaning, and the sentence around the word tells you which meaning is in use.
ela|5|l10-5|3-5|Shades of Meaning — Synonyms are not all exactly alike; walk, stroll and march each carry a different shade of meaning.
ela|5|l10-6|3-5|Guide Words Find the Page — Dictionary words are in alphabetical order, and the two guide words at the top of each page tell you if your word is on that page.
ela|5|l10-7|3-5|Reading an Entry — A dictionary entry shows how to say the word, its part of speech and its meanings, with more than one meaning numbered.
ela|5|l10-8|3-5|The Glossary in the Back — A glossary is a short dictionary in the back of a book that defines only the words used in that book.
ela|5|l10-9|3-5|Similes and Idioms — A simile compares two things using like or as, and an idiom is a saying whose meaning is different from its words.
ela|5|l10-10|3-5|Words in Every Subject — Academic words like compare, evidence and structure appear in every subject, so learning them once pays off all day.
ela|6|l11-1|3-5|Traits Show in Actions — A character trait is what a character is like, and you find it by watching what the character does and says.
ela|6|l11-2|3-5|Why Characters Act — A motive is the reason a character does something, and feelings often change what a character decides to do.
ela|6|l11-3|3-5|When a Character Changes — Many characters are different at the end of a story than at the start, and the change usually comes from what happens to them.
ela|6|l11-4|3-5|Setting Is Time and Place — The setting is where and when a story happens, and it shapes what the characters can and cannot do.
ela|6|l11-5|3-5|The Shape of a Plot — A plot moves from a problem, through events, to a climax, and then to a solution.
ela|6|l11-6|3-5|Three Kinds of Trouble — A conflict is the struggle in a story, and it can be against another character, against nature, or inside the character's own mind.
ela|6|l11-7|3-5|What Dialogue Shows — Dialogue is the words characters say, and it reveals who they are and what they want without the writer telling you.
ela|6|l11-8|3-5|Summarize in a Few Sentences — A summary retells only the main character, the problem, the big events and the ending, in your own words and in order.
ela|6|l11-9|3-5|Prove It From the Page — When you explain a story, back up your idea with evidence: the exact words or events from the text that show it.
ela|6|l11-10|3-5|Fables and Their Lessons — A fable is a very short story, usually with animal characters, that ends with a lesson about how to live.
ela|6|l11-11|3-5|Folktales and Myths Worldwide — Folktales and myths are old stories passed down by many people, and each culture's stories carry that culture's ideas about how to live.
ela|6|l12-1|3-5|Theme Is Not Topic — The topic is what a story is about on the surface; the theme is the big idea or lesson underneath it.
ela|6|l12-2|3-5|What the Character Learns — The fastest way to a theme is to ask what the main character learned, and how that lesson could apply to anyone.
ela|6|l12-3|3-5|Two Stories, One Theme — Different stories from different times and places can share a theme, and comparing them shows what each one adds.
ela|6|l12-4|3-5|First Person, Third Person — In first person a character tells the story using I and me; in third person a narrator outside the story uses he, she and they.
ela|6|l12-5|3-5|What the Narrator Lets You See — A narrator's point of view controls what you know, so a reader has to notice what the narrator cannot see or chooses not to say.
ela|6|l12-6|3-5|One Event, Two Tellers — Comparing the same event from two characters shows what each one knows, feels and leaves out, and helps you find the fuller story.
ela|6|l12-7|3-5|Why the Author Wrote It — Authors write to entertain, to inform, or to persuade, and knowing the purpose helps you know how to read.
ela|6|l12-8|3-5|Making an Inference — An inference is a smart guess that joins a clue in the text with what you already know.
ela|6|l12-9|3-5|Backing Up Your Guess — A good inference comes with its evidence attached: the clue you used and the reasoning that connects it.
ela|6|l13-1|3-5|Lines and Stanzas — A poem is built from lines, and lines are grouped into stanzas; where a line breaks is a choice the poet made.
