ela|6|l13-2|3-5|Rhythm and Rhyme — Rhythm is the beat made by stressed and unstressed syllables, and rhyme is matching sounds at the ends of lines.
ela|6|l13-3|3-5|Saying It Again — Poets repeat words, lines and sounds on purpose, and the repetition tells you what matters most.
ela|6|l13-4|3-5|Reading a Poem Aloud — A poem is meant to be heard, so read it aloud slowly, following the punctuation, not just the line ends.
ela|6|l13-5|3-5|Pictures Made of Words — Imagery is language that makes a picture, sound, smell, taste or touch in the reader's mind, using the five senses.
ela|6|l13-6|3-5|Words That Sound Like Sounds — Some words sound like the noise they name, and poets use them to put sound directly into a line.
ela|6|l13-7|3-5|Dickinson and Rossetti — Emily Dickinson and Christina Rossetti wrote short poems with plain words that still carry big feelings when you read them closely.
ela|6|l13-8|3-5|Stevenson and Dunbar — Robert Louis Stevenson wrote poems from a child's eye, and Paul Laurence Dunbar wrote poems full of music and feeling about freedom.
ela|6|l13-9|3-5|Reading a Script — A play is written as a script with a cast list, lines of dialogue, stage directions and scenes, and each part has a job.
ela|6|l13-10|3-5|Reader's Theater — In reader's theater you read a script aloud with a group, using your voice to become the character, with no costumes or memorizing needed.
ela|6|l13-11|3-5|Poem, Story, Play — A poem, a story and a play can all tell about the same thing, but each form gives the reader something different.
ela|7|l14-1|3-5|The Topic Is Not the Point — The topic is what a text is about in a word or two; the main idea is what the text says about that topic.
ela|7|l14-2|3-5|Details Hold Up the Idea — Supporting details are the facts, examples and reasons that prove or explain the main idea.
ela|7|l14-3|3-5|The Main Idea of a Whole Article — A whole article has one main idea, and each paragraph's main idea is a detail that supports it.
ela|7|l14-4|3-5|A Summary in Your Own Words — A summary tells the main idea and the biggest details in a few sentences, in your words, not the author's.
ela|7|l14-5|3-5|What It Says and What It Hints — A text says some things explicitly, right on the page, and implies other things that you must figure out from clues.
ela|7|l14-6|3-5|Headings and Bold Words — Headings tell you what a section is about, and bold words point to terms the author wants you to learn.
ela|7|l14-7|3-5|Captions, Diagrams, Maps and Sidebars — Pictures, diagrams, maps and sidebars carry facts that the paragraphs may not say, so read them as part of the text.
ela|7|l14-8|3-5|Skim First, Scan for Answers — Skimming means reading fast to get the general idea; scanning means moving your eyes to find one specific thing.
ela|7|l14-9|3-5|Ask Before, During and After — Good readers ask questions before they read, while they read and after they finish, and then check whether the text answered them.
ela|7|l15-1|3-5|Sequence and Description — A sequence text tells events or steps in order, and a description text tells what something is like, feature by feature.
ela|7|l15-2|3-5|Compare and Contrast — A compare-and-contrast text shows how two things are alike and how they are different.
ela|7|l15-3|3-5|Cause and Effect — A cause-and-effect text explains why something happens: this cause leads to that effect.
ela|7|l15-4|3-5|Problem and Solution — A problem-and-solution text names something wrong and then tells how people fixed it or could fix it.
ela|7|l15-5|3-5|Words That Give Away the Shape — Signal words like first, both, because and solved tell you which structure a text is using before you finish it.
ela|7|l15-6|3-5|Notes That Match the Shape — Take notes in the shape of the text: a numbered list for sequence, a two-column chart for compare and contrast, arrows for cause and effect.
ela|7|l15-7|3-5|Comparing Two Texts — When two texts cover the same topic, compare what each one includes, what each leaves out, and how each is built.
ela|7|l15-8|3-5|The Author's Point and Reasons — An author often makes a point and gives reasons for it; your job is to find the point and check whether the reasons really hold it up.
ela|7|l15-9|3-5|Reading Science and History — A science text is usually built on causes, steps and diagrams, while a history text is built on dates, people and reasons, so read each with a different set of questions.
ela|7|l15-10|3-5|Timelines and Steps — Timelines and numbered steps are sequences drawn out, so read them left to right or top to bottom and check the spacing between events.
ela|7|l16-1|3-5|Reading a Chart or Graph — A chart or graph is a text made of shapes and numbers, so read its title, its labels and its key before you read its bars or lines.
ela|7|l16-2|3-5|Tables and Maps — A table lines facts up in rows and columns, and a map shows where; both answer questions if you read their headings and their keys.
ela|7|l16-3|3-5|Primary and Secondary Sources — A primary source was made at the time by someone who was there; a secondary source was made later by someone who studied it.
ela|7|l16-4|3-5|Fact or Opinion — A fact can be checked and shown true or false; an opinion is what someone thinks or feels, and it cannot be proved.
ela|7|l16-5|3-5|Asking a Research Question — A good research question is specific, can be answered with sources, and is something you do not already know.
ela|7|l16-6|3-5|Notes in Your Own Words — Take research notes as short phrases in your own words, and put quotation marks around anything you copy exactly.
ela|7|l16-7|3-5|Keeping Track of Sources — Write down where every fact came from, with the title, the maker, the date and the page or web address, so you and your reader can find it again.
ela|7|l16-8|3-5|A Solid Website or a Shaky One — Trust a website more when you can tell who made it, when it was made, and where its facts came from; trust it less when it hides those things or makes wild claims.
ela|7|l16-9|3-5|Putting the Answer Together — Research ends when you can state your answer in your own words, back it with your best sources, and say honestly what you still do not know.
ela|8|l17-1|3-5|The Topic Sentence Takes a Side — A topic sentence tells your reader exactly what you believe in one clear sentence.
ela|8|l17-2|3-5|Reasons Hold the Claim Up — A reason answers the question why, and a strong reason is one your reader would care about.
ela|8|l17-3|3-5|Examples Make Reasons Real — An example is a specific fact or moment that shows a reason is true.
ela|8|l17-4|3-5|Linking Words Connect Ideas — Linking words like because, for example, also and therefore show the reader how your sentences fit together.
ela|8|l17-5|3-5|The Closing Sentence — A closing sentence restates the claim in new words and leaves the reader with something to remember.
ela|8|l17-6|3-5|Plan Before You Draft — A short plan, made before you write, keeps your paragraph in order and saves you from starting over.
ela|8|l17-7|3-5|Your Opinion About a Book — When you write an opinion about a book, your reasons come from what actually happens in the book.
ela|8|l17-8|3-5|Considering the Other Side — Naming what the other side thinks, and answering it, makes your opinion stronger, not weaker.
ela|8|l17-9|3-5|A Letter to the Principal — A letter turns your opinion paragraph into a message for a real person, with a greeting, a polite tone and a request.
ela|8|l18-1|3-5|Pick One Small Moment — A strong true story zooms in on one small moment instead of listing a whole day.
ela|8|l18-2|3-5|A Beginning That Hooks — Start inside the action, with a line of talk, a sound or a picture, so the reader has to keep going.
ela|8|l18-3|3-5|Talk and the Five Senses — Dialogue and sensory details let the reader hear, see and feel the moment instead of being told about it.
ela|8|l18-4|3-5|An Ending That Satisfies — A good ending lands on the feeling of the moment; it does not just stop or say the end.
ela|8|l18-5|3-5|Introduce and Group the Facts — Informative writing opens by naming the topic, then puts related facts together in groups.
ela|8|l18-6|3-5|Headings, Pictures and Definitions — Headings, pictures and defined words help a reader find and understand the facts.
ela|8|l18-7|3-5|Writing a How-To — A how-to lists what you need, then gives numbered steps in the exact order a reader must do them.
ela|8|l18-8|3-5|From Notes to Paragraph — Writing from research means turning notes in your own words into organized paragraphs, and saying where the facts came from.
ela|8|l18-9|3-5|Writing About a Text With Evidence — When you write about a text, make a point and then prove it with words or details from the text itself.
ela|8|l18-10|3-5|Pacing and Transitions — Pacing is how fast a piece moves, and transition words carry the reader smoothly from one part to the next.
ela|8|l19-1|3-5|Drafting Is Not Finishing — A first draft is for getting ideas down; the real work of writing happens in the drafts that follow.
ela|8|l19-2|3-5|Revising: Add, Cut, Move, Replace — Revising changes what a piece says, using four moves: add, cut, move and replace.
ela|8|l19-3|3-5|Feedback That Helps — Good feedback from a reader is specific, kind and about the writing, and it always includes a question.
ela|8|l19-4|3-5|Strong Verbs and Exact Nouns — A strong verb and an exact noun do more work than a weak word propped up with extra ones.
ela|8|l19-5|3-5|Sentence Variety — Mixing short and long sentences, and different openings, keeps a reader awake.
ela|8|l19-6|3-5|Capitals and Commas in a Series — Editing fixes the rules: capitals at sentence starts and on names, and commas between items in a list.
ela|8|l19-7|3-5|Quotation Marks in Dialogue — Quotation marks go around the exact words a person says, with a comma or end mark inside them.
ela|8|l19-8|3-5|Apostrophes — An apostrophe shows a missing letter in a contraction or shows that something belongs to someone.
ela|8|l19-9|3-5|Spelling Strategies — Good spellers use strategies: word parts, patterns, a personal word list and a dictionary check.
ela|8|l19-10|3-5|Read It Aloud — Reading your draft out loud lets your ears catch mistakes your eyes skip over.
ela|8|l19-11|3-5|Type It and Share It — Typing a final copy and sharing it with a real reader is the last step of the process, and it is the reason for all the rest.
ela|9|l20-1|3-5|Subject and Predicate — Every sentence has a subject (who or what) and a predicate (what happens), and it needs both.
ela|9|l20-2|3-5|Nouns and Pronouns — Nouns name people, places, things and ideas, and pronouns stand in for nouns so you do not repeat them.
ela|9|l20-3|3-5|Verbs, Adjectives and Adverbs — Verbs show action or being, adjectives describe nouns, and adverbs describe verbs.
ela|9|l20-4|3-5|Prepositions Show Where and When — A preposition is a small word like under, on or after that links a noun to the rest of the sentence.
ela|9|l20-5|3-5|Simple and Compound — A simple sentence has one subject and one predicate; a compound sentence joins two with a comma and a joining word.
ela|9|l20-6|3-5|Complex Sentences — A complex sentence joins a full sentence to a part that cannot stand alone, using words like because, when or although.
ela|9|l20-7|3-5|Fragments Are Missing a Part — A fragment is a piece of a sentence that is missing a subject or a predicate, and you fix it by adding the missing part.
ela|9|l20-8|3-5|Run-ons Never Stop — A run-on jams two or more sentences together without the right stop, and you fix it with a period, a comma and a conjunction, or a joining word like because.
ela|9|l20-9|3-5|Subject and Verb Agree — A singular subject takes a singular verb and a plural subject takes a plural verb, and they must match.
ela|9|l20-10|3-5|Past, Present, Future — A verb's tense tells when something happens, and a piece of writing should stay in one tense unless the time really changes.
ela|9|l20-11|3-5|Choosing the Right Word — Words that sound the same are spelled differently for a reason, and the words you choose should fit where you are and who is listening.
ela|9|l21-1|3-5|Come Prepared — A good discussion starts before it starts: read the text, mark what you noticed, and bring one thing to say.
ela|9|l21-2|3-5|Listen and Build On — Listening means taking in what someone said and connecting your idea to theirs, not just waiting for your turn.
ela|9|l21-3|3-5|Ask and Disagree Politely — Questions and polite disagreement make a discussion stronger, as long as you talk about the idea and not the person.
ela|9|l21-4|3-5|Summarize What You Heard — A summary of a speaker gives the main point and the biggest reasons in your own words, in a few sentences.
ela|9|l21-5|3-5|Check What You Heard — When you are not sure what a speaker meant, ask a clear question instead of guessing.
ela|9|l21-6|3-5|Put It in Order — A short talk needs a clear order: tell what it is about, give two or three parts in a sequence, and end on purpose.
ela|9|l21-7|3-5|Details That Help — A talk comes alive with a few specific details, facts and examples that support each main point.
ela|9|l21-8|3-5|Pace and Volume — Speak slowly enough to be understood, loudly enough to reach the back, and pause where a period would go.
ela|9|l21-9|3-5|Pictures and Objects — A picture or object can show what words alone cannot, but it should help the point, not replace it.
ela|9|l21-10|3-5|Record and Listen Back — Recording yourself and listening back shows you what your audience hears, so you can fix it before it counts.
ela|9|l21-11|3-5|Audience and Purpose — Who is listening and why you are talking should change your words, your details and your tone.
ss|1|l1-1|k-2|My Name and My Birthday — Every person has a name and a birthday. They are part of who you are.
ss|1|l1-2|k-2|Things I Like and Can Do — What you like and what you can do are part of who you are.
ss|1|l1-3|k-2|Families Come in Many Shapes — A family is the people who love you and take care of you. Families are not all the same size.
ss|1|l1-4|k-2|We Help Each Other — People in a family have jobs, and they help each other.
ss|1|l1-5|k-2|Things We Do Every Year — A tradition is something a family does again and again.
ss|1|l1-6|k-2|Grandpa's Story From Long Ago — Family stories tell us what life was like long ago.
ss|1|l1-7|k-2|Same and Different — Classmates are the same in some ways and different in others, and that is good.
ss|1|l1-8|k-2|My Class Is a Group — Your class is a group of many different people who belong together.
ss|1|l2-1|k-2|Rules Keep Us Safe — Many rules keep people from getting hurt.
ss|1|l2-2|k-2|Rules Are Fair — Rules make things fair so everyone gets a turn.
ss|1|l2-3|k-2|Rules at Home — Homes have rules too, and grown-ups make most of them.
ss|1|l2-4|k-2|On the Bus and at the Pool — Different places have different rules because they have different dangers.
ss|1|l2-5|k-2|Our Class Makes Rules — A class can make its own rules by sharing ideas and voting.
ss|1|l2-6|k-2|When a Rule Is Broken — When you break a rule, you can make it right.
ss|1|l2-7|k-2|Turns, Ears and the Truth — A good classmate takes turns, listens and tells the truth.
ss|1|l2-8|k-2|Leaders Who Help — A teacher, a principal and a crossing guard are leaders who help keep school working.
ss|10|l20-1|6-8|History Without Writing — For almost all of human time there was no writing, so we learn about early people from the objects and traces they left behind.
ss|10|l20-2|6-8|Tools, Bones and Layers — Stone tools, bones and the order of layers in the ground let archaeologists tell what early people did and roughly when.
ss|10|l20-3|6-8|Paintings in the Dark — Cave paintings show that Ice Age people had skill, imagination and beliefs, even though we cannot be sure what the pictures meant.
ss|10|l20-4|6-8|Skills of a Foraging Band — Hunter-gatherers lived in small mobile bands and survived through deep knowledge of plants, animals and each other.
ss|10|l20-5|6-8|The Ice Age World — During the last Ice Age, huge ice sheets covered much of the north, sea levels fell, and people adapted to a cold world full of giant animals.
ss|10|l20-6|6-8|Peopling the Continents — Modern humans spread from Africa across every continent except Antarctica, reaching the Americas near the end of the Ice Age.
ss|10|l20-7|6-8|Why Farming Began — Farming began about ten to eleven thousand years ago as a warming climate, growing populations and rich wild grain patches made settling down possible.
ss|10|l20-8|6-8|Taming Plants and Animals — Domestication means changing plants and animals over generations by choosing which ones to breed, until they depend on people and people depend on them.
ss|10|l20-9|6-8|Farming Around the World — Farming was invented separately in several parts of the world, each with its own crops and animals.
ss|10|l20-10|6-8|Surplus, Storage and Specialists — When farmers grew more than they could eat, some people could stop farming and become specialists, and societies began to divide into classes.
ss|10|l20-11|6-8|Çatalhöyük: A Town Without Streets — Çatalhöyük in Turkey was one of the largest early farming settlements, a packed town of mud-brick houses entered through the roof.
ss|10|l20-12|6-8|Was Farming Worth It? — Farming let human numbers grow and made civilization possible, but the first farmers were often less healthy, less free and harder working than the hunters they replaced.
ss|10|l21-1|6-8|Two Rivers and One Great River — The first civilizations grew in the valleys of the Tigris, Euphrates and Nile, where rivers gave water, rich soil and a road for boats.
ss|10|l21-2|6-8|Irrigation and Its Price — Irrigation let river-valley farmers grow huge harvests, but it required organized labor, shared rules and constant repair.
ss|10|l21-3|6-8|Sumer's City-States — By about 3000 BCE, Sumer in southern Mesopotamia had a dozen independent walled cities, each ruled by its own king and its own god.
ss|10|l21-4|6-8|Cuneiform: Writing on Clay — Sumerians invented writing around 3200 BCE to keep records, and their wedge-shaped script was used for three thousand years.
ss|10|l21-5|6-8|Wheels, Bronze and Ziggurats — Sumerians used the wheel, bronze tools and monumental temples called ziggurats, inventions that spread far beyond Mesopotamia.
ss|10|l21-6|6-8|Hammurabi's Code — Around 1750 BCE, King Hammurabi of Babylon had nearly three hundred laws carved in stone, showing that justice depended on your rank.
ss|10|l21-7|6-8|Three Kingdoms on the Nile — Egypt was united around 3100 BCE and its long history is divided into the Old, Middle and New Kingdoms, separated by times of breakdown.
ss|10|l21-8|6-8|Pyramids and the Afterlife — Egyptians built pyramids as tombs because they believed the pharaoh's survival after death protected the whole land.
ss|10|l21-9|6-8|Hieroglyphs and the Rosetta Stone — Egyptian hieroglyphs were unreadable for over a thousand years until a stone with the same text in three scripts let scholars crack them.
ss|10|l21-10|6-8|Daily Life: Workers, Women and Scribes — Most Egyptians and Mesopotamians were farmers, but women, craft workers and scribes each had a distinct place, and we know about them from their own records.
ss|10|l21-11|6-8|Trade and War Between Lands — River civilizations needed goods they lacked, so they traded across great distances and fought over the routes and the wealth.
ss|10|l21-12|6-8|What They Left Us — Writing, written law, the calendar, timekeeping and the idea of the state all came from Mesopotamia and Egypt and are still part of daily life.
ss|10|l22-1|6-8|Streets on a Grid — The Indus civilization built planned cities with straight streets, standard bricks and the best drains of the ancient world.
ss|10|l22-2|6-8|A Script No One Can Read — The Indus people wrote on small seals, but their script is short, undeciphered, and hides who ruled them and what they believed.
ss|10|l22-3|6-8|Why the Cities Faded — After about 1900 BCE the Indus cities emptied out, most likely because rivers shifted and the monsoon weakened, not because of an invasion.
ss|10|l22-4|6-8|The Yellow River and Its Sorrow — Chinese civilization grew on the loess soil of the Yellow River, a river that fed millions and drowned them.
ss|10|l22-5|6-8|Shang Kings and Oracle Bones — The Shang dynasty ruled north China from about 1600 to 1046 BCE with bronze, chariots, ancestor worship and the earliest Chinese writing.
ss|10|l22-6|6-8|The Zhou and the Mandate of Heaven — When the Zhou overthrew the Shang in 1046 BCE, they explained it with the Mandate of Heaven, an idea that shaped Chinese politics for three thousand years.
ss|10|l22-7|6-8|The Olmec of the Gulf Coast — The Olmec, from about 1200 to 400 BCE, built the first cities in Mesoamerica and left colossal stone heads, jade, and ideas that later peoples inherited.
ss|10|l22-8|6-8|Norte Chico: Cities Without Pottery — Around 3000 BCE, people in the dry valleys of Peru built the oldest cities in the Americas using fish, cotton and irrigation, without pottery or grain.
ss|10|l22-9|6-8|What They Shared and How They Differed — Every early civilization had surplus food, cities, specialists, organized leaders and monuments, but they differed in writing, kings, war and what they valued.
ss|10|l22-10|6-8|How Archaeologists Work — Archaeologists recover the past through careful survey, slow digging, recording every find in place, dating it and arguing about what it means.
ss|11|l23-1|6-8|Mountains, Sea and the Polis — Greece's mountains and coast split its people into small, independent city-states that shared a language and gods.
ss|11|l23-2|6-8|Athens and Sparta — Athens and Sparta were both Greek, but one built a trading democracy and the other a military state.
ss|11|l23-3|6-8|How Athens Became a Democracy — Over about two centuries, reformers gave ordinary Athenian citizens the power to make laws and judge cases.
ss|11|l23-4|6-8|Who Was Left Out — Athenian democracy included every male citizen but shut out women, foreigners and the enslaved, who together were most of the population.
ss|11|l23-5|6-8|The Persian Wars — Outnumbered Greek city-states twice turned back the huge Persian Empire, at Marathon and then at Salamis and Plataea.
ss|11|l23-6|6-8|The Parthenon and the Theater — In the fifty years after the Persian Wars, Athens used its wealth to build the Parthenon and to invent drama.
ss|11|l23-7|6-8|Socrates, Plato and Aristotle — Three Athenian thinkers taught people to question, reason and observe, and their methods are still used today.
ss|11|l23-8|6-8|History and Medicine — Greek writers began to explain wars and diseases by evidence and natural causes instead of by the will of the gods.
ss|11|l23-9|6-8|Alexander the Great — In just over a decade, Alexander of Macedon conquered the Persian Empire and spread Greek culture from Egypt to India.
ss|11|l23-10|6-8|The Hellenistic World — After Alexander, Greek language and learning blended with Egyptian, Persian and other cultures across a wide Hellenistic world.
ss|11|l23-11|6-8|Greek Ideas in Our World — American government, science and everyday English still carry ideas and words that began in ancient Greece.
ss|11|l24-1|6-8|A Town on Seven Hills — Rome grew from a cluster of hill villages on the Tiber River into a city whose location helped it dominate Italy.
ss|11|l24-2|6-8|The Republic and Its Laws — The Roman Republic balanced power among consuls, the Senate and citizen assemblies, and the plebeians fought to win written laws and a share of power.
ss|11|l24-3|6-8|The Punic Wars — In three wars with Carthage, Rome survived Hannibal's invasion, destroyed its rival and became master of the western Mediterranean.
ss|11|l24-4|6-8|Caesar and the End of the Republic — Wealth, inequality and ambitious generals tore the Republic apart, and Julius Caesar's rise and murder ended it.
ss|11|l24-5|6-8|Augustus and the Roman Peace — Caesar's heir Octavian won the civil wars and, as Augustus, became the first emperor while keeping the forms of the Republic.
ss|11|l24-6|6-8|Roads, Water, Law and Latin — Rome held its empire together with engineering, a shared system of law and the Latin language.
ss|11|l24-7|6-8|Daily Life, Slavery and the Games — Roman life ranged from crowded apartments to grand villas, rested heavily on enslaved labor and was entertained by brutal public games.
ss|11|l24-8|6-8|Christianity in the Empire — A small religious movement from Judea spread along Roman roads, survived persecution and by the 300s became the empire's official faith.
ss|11|l24-9|6-8|Why the Western Empire Fell — No single cause ended Rome in the west; weak government, money troubles, plague, and pressure from migrating peoples combined over two centuries.
ss|11|l24-10|6-8|Rome Among Us — Roman law, language, engineering, government ideas and the Christian church outlived the empire and shaped the modern world, including the United States.
ss|11|l25-1|6-8|The Maurya Empire and Ashoka — The Maurya dynasty united most of India, and its greatest king, Ashoka, gave up conquest for a rule based on compassion.
ss|11|l25-2|6-8|Hinduism and Buddhism — Hinduism grew from ancient Indian traditions of many gods, duty and rebirth, and Buddhism arose within that world as a path to end suffering.
ss|11|l25-3|6-8|The Gupta Age and the Zero — Under the Gupta empire India produced great art, literature and the number system with zero that the whole world now uses.
ss|11|l25-4|6-8|The First Emperor — In 221 BCE the king of Qin conquered the warring states of China and ruled by harsh laws and strict standardization.
ss|11|l25-5|6-8|The Clay Army — The First Emperor's tomb, guarded by thousands of life-size clay soldiers, shows both his power and the labor it cost.
ss|11|l25-6|6-8|The Han and Confucius — The Han dynasty ruled China for four centuries by blending strong government with Confucian ideas of duty, learning and fair rule.
ss|11|l25-7|6-8|Paper and the Civil Service — Han China invented paper and began choosing officials by examination, two ideas that spread around the world.
ss|11|l25-8|6-8|Routes and Goods — The Silk Roads were a web of land and sea routes across Eurasia along which goods passed through many hands between China, India, Persia and Rome.
ss|11|l25-9|6-8|Ideas on the Move — Religions, inventions and knowledge traveled the Silk Roads with the traders, changing every society they reached.
ss|11|l25-10|6-8|A Connected World — The same routes that spread goods and ideas also carried diseases, and by the 200s CE plague struck both Rome and Han China as both empires weakened.
ss|12|l26-1|6-8|A City Between Two Seas — When Rome's western half collapsed, its eastern half lived on for a thousand years from Constantinople, a city built on a narrow strait.
ss|12|l26-2|6-8|Justinian Writes Down the Law — Emperor Justinian collected a thousand years of Roman law into one clear code, and that code still shapes laws in much of the world.
ss|12|l26-3|6-8|A Dome That Seemed to Float — Hagia Sophia showed the wealth and faith of Byzantium, and the split between its Greek church and Rome's Latin church still divides Christians today.
ss|12|l26-4|6-8|A Merchant in Mecca — Islam began in the trading city of Mecca in the early 600s, when Muhammad taught that there is one God and that all believers are equal before him.
ss|12|l26-5|6-8|Caliphs and a Growing Empire — Within a century of Muhammad's death, Muslim rulers called caliphs governed lands from Spain to India, ruling many peoples who kept their own faiths.
ss|12|l26-6|6-8|The House of Wisdom — Scholars in Baghdad gathered the knowledge of Greece, Persia and India, and added algebra, new astronomy and medicine that Europe later learned from them.
ss|12|l26-7|6-8|The Ship of the Desert — The camel turned the Sahara from a wall into a road, and along that road salt went south while gold went north.
ss|12|l26-8|6-8|Ghana, the Land of Gold — The kingdom of Ghana grew rich by taxing the salt and gold that crossed its land, and we know about it mainly from Arab writers.
ss|12|l26-9|6-8|Mali, Mansa Musa and Timbuktu — The Empire of Mali replaced Ghana, and under Mansa Musa its wealth and its city of Timbuktu became famous across Africa and Europe.
ss|12|l26-10|6-8|Great Zimbabwe — In southern Africa, the builders of Great Zimbabwe raised huge stone walls and traded gold to the coast, and their ruins later exposed a racist lie.
ss|12|l26-11|6-8|The Swahili Coast — Along Africa's east coast, city-states like Kilwa grew rich on Indian Ocean trade and blended African, Arab and Persian ways into Swahili culture.
ss|12|l26-12|6-8|A Connected World — Between 500 and 1500, goods, faiths and ideas moved along linked routes across Africa, Asia and Europe, and the trade had winners and victims.
ss|12|l27-1|6-8|Lords, Knights and Serfs — After Rome fell, Europe was held together by feudalism: lords gave land to knights in exchange for military service, and serfs worked the land for both.
ss|12|l27-2|6-8|The Church and Its Cathedrals — The Catholic Church was the one institution that reached every village in western Europe, and its cathedrals were the tallest buildings anyone had ever seen.
ss|12|l27-3|6-8|The Crusades — For two centuries European knights fought wars to take Jerusalem from Muslim rule; they failed in the end, but the wars changed trade and learning in Europe.
ss|12|l27-4|6-8|The Black Death — The plague of 1347 to 1351 killed roughly a third or more of Europe's people, and the labor shortage it left behind helped break feudalism.
ss|12|l27-5|6-8|The Great Charter of 1215 — Magna Carta forced an English king to admit that even he was under the law, an idea that later shaped the United States Constitution.
ss|12|l27-6|6-8|Genghis Khan Unites the Steppe — Genghis Khan united the horse-riding tribes of Mongolia and built the largest connected land empire in history, at a terrible cost to those who resisted.
ss|12|l27-7|6-8|The Mongol Peace and Marco Polo — Under Mongol rule the roads across Asia became safe enough for merchants and travelers like Marco Polo, and ideas, goods and disease all moved faster.
ss|12|l27-8|6-8|Japan Under the Shoguns — In Japan a military ruler called the shogun held real power while the emperor reigned in name, and samurai warriors served their lords much as knights did in Europe.
ss|12|l27-9|6-8|The Maya — In the rain forests of Central America the Maya built stone cities, invented a full writing system and tracked the sky with a calendar more exact than Europe's.
ss|12|l27-10|6-8|The Aztec and Their Island City — The Aztec built Tenochtitlan on a lake and ruled central Mexico through conquest and tribute, feeding a city of about two hundred thousand from floating farms.
ss|12|l27-11|6-8|The Inca Road — In the Andes the Inca ruled the largest empire in the Americas with roads, storehouses and knotted strings, but no wheels, no horses and no writing.
ss|12|l27-12|6-8|The World in 1450 — In 1450 the world's great societies were rich, connected within their own regions and unaware of each other across the oceans, and that was about to change.
ss|13|l28-1|6-8|Latitude and Longitude — A grid of imaginary lines, latitude and longitude, gives every spot on Earth an address made of two numbers.
ss|13|l28-2|6-8|Why Every Map Lies a Little — Because Earth is round and a map is flat, every world map distorts size, shape, distance or direction, and the mapmaker chooses which.
ss|13|l28-3|6-8|Continents and Oceans — Earth's surface is mostly one connected ocean, broken by seven large landmasses we call continents.
ss|13|l28-4|6-8|Plates That Move — Earth's outer shell is broken into huge plates that drift a few centimeters a year, carrying the continents with them.
ss|13|l28-5|6-8|Mountains, Quakes and Volcanoes — Most mountains, earthquakes and volcanoes happen where plates meet, because that is where the crust is squeezed, pulled or slid.
ss|13|l28-6|6-8|What Makes a Climate — A place's climate depends mostly on its latitude, its altitude, its distance from the ocean and the winds and currents that reach it.
ss|13|l28-7|6-8|Climate Zones of the World — Geographers sort the world into climate zones, from tropical to polar, that repeat in bands from the equator toward the poles.
ss|13|l28-8|6-8|Biomes From Rainforest to Tundra — A biome is a large community of plants and animals shaped by climate, and the same biome appears wherever the same climate does.
ss|13|l28-9|6-8|Rivers and Watersheds — Every drop of rain that falls on land belongs to a watershed, the area drained by one river system, and watersheds connect distant places.
ss|13|l28-10|6-8|Natural Hazards and Getting Ready — Natural hazards strike where geography puts them, and how many people they harm depends on how well a community has prepared.
ss|13|l28-11|6-8|Maps as Arguments — A map is never just a picture; someone chose what to show, how to color it and what to leave out, and those choices make a claim.
ss|13|l29-1|6-8|Water, Soil and Coasts — People cluster where the land can feed them and where travel is easy: river valleys, rich plains and coasts.
ss|13|l29-2|6-8|Counting the World — World population grew slowly for most of history and then very fast after 1800, and it is spread very unevenly across the land.
ss|13|l29-3|6-8|Why People Move — Migration happens when push factors drive people out of one place and pull factors draw them toward another.
ss|13|l29-4|6-8|Culture Regions and Languages — Shared language, customs and history create culture regions whose borders on the map are real but blurry.
ss|13|l29-5|6-8|Religions on the Map — The world's major religions each began in a particular place and spread along the paths of trade, migration and empire.
ss|13|l29-6|6-8|What the Earth Provides — Natural resources are gifts of geography, and the key difference is whether they grow back or run out.
ss|13|l29-7|6-8|Farming, Factories and Services — Every economy mixes three kinds of work, growing things, making things and doing things for others, and the mix shows how a place has changed.
ss|13|l29-8|6-8|Trade Routes Then and Now — Trade routes follow geography, and each new route, from camel caravan to container ship, has redrawn which places matter.
ss|13|l29-9|6-8|The World in Your Shoes — Globalization means that the parts of one product come from many countries, which lowers prices and links distant lives, for better and worse.
ss|13|l29-10|6-8|Cities and Their Problems — More than half the world's people now live in cities, which offer opportunity but strain housing, water, air and transport.
ss|13|l29-11|6-8|Human Choices, Changed Land — People reshape the environment wherever they live, and the wisest choices weigh what is gained today against what is lost for tomorrow.
ss|14|l30-1|6-8|Why Three Branches — The framers split power into three branches so that no one person or group could rule alone.
ss|14|l30-2|6-8|The Preamble in Plain Words — The Preamble is one sentence that names who made the Constitution and six reasons why.
ss|14|l30-3|6-8|Congress Makes the Laws — Congress has two houses, and a bill must pass both before it can become a federal law.
ss|14|l30-4|6-8|The President Carries Out Laws — The president leads the executive branch, which enforces the laws but cannot write them.
ss|14|l30-5|6-8|Courts Decide What Laws Mean — The judicial branch interprets laws and can strike down laws that break the Constitution.
ss|14|l30-6|6-8|How Each Branch Checks the Others — Checks and balances give each branch tools to block the other two, so power stays shared.
ss|14|l30-7|6-8|What the Nation Does and What Illinois Does — Federalism divides power between the national government and the states, with some powers shared.
ss|14|l30-8|6-8|The Illinois Constitution of 1970 — Illinois has had four constitutions, and the 1970 one is the plan the state government follows today.
ss|14|l30-9|6-8|The General Assembly and the Governor — Illinois makes its laws through a two-house General Assembly, and the governor can sign, veto or change a bill.
ss|14|l30-10|6-8|The Illinois Supreme Court — Illinois voters elect their judges, including the seven justices of the Illinois Supreme Court.
ss|14|l30-11|6-8|Counties, Cities and School Boards — Local governments in Illinois make the decisions that touch your daily life most directly.
ss|14|l30-12|6-8|The Budget and Taxes — Governments pay for what they do with taxes, and a budget is the yearly fight over how to spend them.
ss|14|l31-1|6-8|Why the First Ten Amendments Exist — The Bill of Rights was added in 1791 to list the freedoms the new government could not take away.
ss|14|l31-2|6-8|Arms, Soldiers and Searches — The Second, Third and Fourth Amendments limit what the government can do with weapons, soldiers and searches.
ss|14|l31-3|6-8|Rights of the Accused — The Fifth through Eighth Amendments protect anyone accused of a crime, whether guilty or innocent.
ss|14|l31-4|6-8|Freedom, Citizenship and the Vote — The Thirteenth, Fourteenth and Fifteenth Amendments ended slavery and promised citizenship and the vote to Black Americans.
ss|14|l31-5|6-8|Women and Young Voters — The Nineteenth and Twenty-Sixth Amendments extended the vote to women and to citizens as young as eighteen.
ss|14|l31-6|6-8|How a Court Case Works — A case moves from a trial court through appeals, and only a few reach the Supreme Court.
ss|14|l31-7|6-8|Brown: Separate Is Not Equal — In 1954 the Supreme Court ruled that segregated public schools violate the Fourteenth Amendment.
ss|14|l31-8|6-8|Tinker: Speech at the Schoolhouse Gate — Tinker v. Des Moines protects student speech unless it substantially disrupts school.
ss|14|l31-9|6-8|Miranda: You Have the Right — Miranda v. Arizona requires police to tell suspects their rights before questioning them in custody.
ss|14|l31-10|6-8|Voting in Illinois — Voting in Illinois is easy to do and easy to skip, and the people who skip it still get governed.
ss|14|l31-11|6-8|Parties, Media and Finding the Truth — Parties and media help you sort information, but only checking sources tells you what is true.
ss|14|l31-12|6-8|Ways to Take Part Now — You do not need to be eighteen to petition, speak at a meeting, serve, or argue well with someone who disagrees.
ss|15|l32-1|9-10|Song China's Economic Revolution — Between about 960 and 1279, China under the Song dynasty built the most productive economy on Earth, driven by rice, iron, paper money and cities.
ss|15|l32-2|9-10|Dar al-Islam and Its Scholars — By 1200 the Muslim world stretched from Spain to India, tied together by one faith, one language of learning and a shared body of knowledge.
ss|15|l32-3|9-10|The Mongol Peace — The Mongols conquered by terror, but the empire they built made travel across Asia safer and faster than ever before.
ss|15|l32-4|9-10|The Indian Ocean and the Monsoon — Predictable monsoon winds turned the Indian Ocean into a highway linking East Africa, Arabia, India, Southeast Asia and China.
ss|15|l32-5|9-10|Gold, Salt and the Empire of Mali — Camel caravans across the Sahara traded West African gold for salt and made Mali one of the richest states of its age.
ss|15|l32-6|9-10|Travelers as Evidence — Accounts by travelers like Marco Polo and Ibn Battuta are rich sources, but historians test them against other evidence before trusting them.
ss|15|l32-7|9-10|The Black Death — Between 1346 and 1351 a plague carried along trade routes killed roughly a third of the people of Europe and devastated the Middle East and North Africa.
ss|15|l32-8|9-10|A World With Fewer People — The plague's death toll shifted power toward surviving workers, weakened serfdom in western Europe and shook people's faith in old authorities.
ss|15|l32-9|9-10|The Renaissance in Italy — In the wealthy city-states of Italy, scholars and artists revived ancient learning and turned their attention to the human world.
ss|15|l32-10|9-10|The Printing Press — Around 1450 Johannes Gutenberg combined movable metal type, oil-based ink and a press, and books became cheap enough to change who could read.
ss|15|l32-11|9-10|The Fall of Constantinople — In 1453 Ottoman cannon breached the walls of Constantinople, ending the Byzantine Empire and reshaping trade and power in the eastern Mediterranean.
ss|15|l32-12|9-10|Taking Stock in 1450 — In 1450 no single region led the world; China, the Islamic lands, Europe and the Americas each had strengths the others lacked.
ss|15|l33-1|9-10|Portugal Learns the Atlantic — Portuguese sailors combined borrowed tools and new ships to work their way down Africa's coast and around it to India by 1498.
ss|15|l33-2|9-10|Columbus and the Taino — Columbus reached the Caribbean in 1492 believing he was near Asia; for the Taino people who met him, the arrival began a catastrophe.
ss|15|l33-3|9-10|Why the Conquests Succeeded — Small Spanish forces toppled the Aztec and Inca empires because of disease, Indigenous allies, steel and horses, and timing, not because of Spanish courage alone.
ss|15|l33-4|9-10|Crops and Animals Change Places — After 1492 plants and animals crossed the Atlantic in both directions and permanently changed what people ate and how they lived on every continent.
ss|15|l33-5|9-10|The Great Dying — Eurasian diseases killed the majority of the Indigenous population of the Americas within about a century, the largest demographic disaster in recorded history.
ss|15|l33-6|9-10|Silver and the First Global Trade — Silver from Potosí and Mexico, mined with forced labor, flowed to Spain and to China and tied the world's economies together for the first time.
ss|15|l33-7|9-10|The Transatlantic Slave Trade — Over about 350 years, roughly twelve and a half million Africans were forced onto ships bound for the Americas, most to work sugar and other plantations.
ss|15|l33-8|9-10|Resistance and Survival — Africans resisted enslavement at every stage, from kings who protested to captives who revolted at sea and communities of escapees who fought colonial armies.
ss|15|l33-9|9-10|The Reformation — Beginning in 1517, Martin Luther's protest split Western Christianity, and the printing press turned a local argument into a continental revolt.
ss|15|l33-10|9-10|The Scientific Revolution — Between about 1543 and 1687, European thinkers replaced ancient authority with observation, measurement and mathematics as the way to know how nature works.
ss|15|l33-11|9-10|One Connected World — By 1650 Europeans, Africans and Indigenous Americans lived, worked and mixed in a single Atlantic world whose trade and people reached across the Pacific to Asia.
ss|15|l34-1|9-10|The Ottomans at Their Height — Under Suleiman in the mid-1500s the Ottoman Empire ruled three continents through a disciplined slave army, a professional bureaucracy and a policy of governing non-Muslims through their own communities.
ss|15|l34-2|9-10|Safavid Persia and Its Rival — The Safavids made Shia Islam the religion of Persia, fought the Sunni Ottomans for two centuries, and under Shah Abbas built Isfahan into one of the world's great cities.
ss|15|l34-3|9-10|The Mughals from Akbar to Aurangzeb — The Mughals ruled most of India for two centuries by combining military power, an efficient land tax and, under Akbar, religious tolerance that later rulers abandoned.
ss|15|l34-4|9-10|Ming to Qing China — In 1644 the Manchus replaced the Ming and built the Qing, the largest Chinese empire ever, while keeping most of China's traditional government.
ss|15|l34-5|9-10|Tokugawa Japan Closes Its Doors — After 1600 the Tokugawa shoguns ended a century of civil war, controlled the warrior lords tightly, and sealed Japan off from almost all foreign contact for more than two centuries.
ss|15|l34-6|9-10|Russia Under Peter — Peter the Great forced Russia to copy European armies, navies and customs, built a new capital on the Baltic, and made the tsar's power more absolute than ever.
ss|15|l34-7|9-10|Louis XIV and the Absolute State — Louis XIV of France claimed that all authority came from God through the king, and built Versailles, a professional bureaucracy and a huge army to make that claim real.
ss|15|l34-8|9-10|England's Constitutional Road — England fought a civil war, executed one king and expelled another, and by 1689 had settled that Parliament, not the crown alone, held the power to tax, legislate and limit the monarch.
ss|15|l34-9|9-10|Paying for Power — Every early modern state faced the same problem, paying for bigger armies, and the ones that could borrow cheaply and tax reliably won the wars.
ss|15|l34-10|9-10|The Enlightenment — Thinkers of the 1700s argued that reason could discover laws of government as science had discovered laws of nature, and that legitimate power rests on the consent of the governed.
ss|15|l34-11|9-10|Comparing the Empires — Empires of this era differed in how they treated religious minorities, how open they were to trade, and what they built, and those choices shaped their strength.
ss|15|l34-12|9-10|Why Empires Faded — By 1750 the Safavids had fallen, the Mughals had fragmented and the Ottomans had stalled, undone by costly wars, succession fights, money troubles and rising rivals.
ss|16|l35-1|9-10|A Revolution the World Watched — The American Revolution was a world event: it grew from a world war, was won with foreign help, and gave people everywhere a model for government by consent.
ss|16|l35-2|9-10|Three Estates and a Tennis Court — France's king called the Estates-General to solve a money crisis, and the Third Estate turned it into a claim that the nation, not the king, held power.
ss|16|l35-3|9-10|Rights on Paper, Women Left Off — The Declaration of the Rights of Man announced equal rights for men, and Olympe de Gouges answered with a declaration for women that cost her her life.
ss|16|l35-4|9-10|The Republic and the Terror — War and fear pushed the French Revolution from a limited monarchy to a republic that executed its king and then thousands of its own citizens.
ss|16|l35-5|9-10|Napoleon's Empire and Its Fall — Napoleon ended the revolution's chaos, spread its laws across Europe by conquest, and was finally defeated by the nations his conquests helped awaken.
ss|16|l35-6|9-10|Haiti: The Enslaved Free Themselves — In Saint-Domingue, half a million enslaved people rose in 1791, forced France to abolish slavery, defeated Napoleon's army, and founded Haiti in 1804.
ss|16|l35-7|9-10|Bolívar and Spanish South America — When Napoleon toppled Spain's king, Spanish America's colonies broke away, and Simón Bolívar led armies across the Andes to win independence for the north of South America.
ss|16|l35-8|9-10|Hidalgo's Cry in Mexico — Mexico's independence began as a rising of the poor led by a priest, was crushed, and was finished eleven years later by the very elites who had fought against it.
ss|16|l35-9|9-10|1848: The Springtime of Peoples — In 1848 revolutions swept across Europe demanding constitutions and nations of their own, won for a few months, and were mostly crushed, though the ideas survived.
ss|16|l35-10|9-10|The Fight to End Slavery — Abolition was won piece by piece across the Atlantic world by the enslaved, free Black activists and religious reformers who used the language of rights against slavery.
ss|16|l35-11|9-10|Who Owns the Rights of Man — The declarations of 1776 and 1789 announced universal rights, and the people they excluded used those same words to demand inclusion, a pattern that has never stopped.
ss|16|l36-1|9-10|Coal, Capital and Canals — Britain industrialized first because it had cheap coal, money to invest, waterways to move heavy goods, and laws that made business relatively safe.
ss|16|l36-2|9-10|Cotton and the First Machines — Demand for cotton cloth drove a chain of inventions in spinning and weaving that moved cloth-making from cottages into mills.
ss|16|l36-3|9-10|Steam Changes Everything — James Watt's improved steam engine freed factories from rivers and turned coal into motion, powering mills, mines and eventually ships and trains.
ss|16|l36-4|9-10|Life Inside the Factory — The factory system replaced the rhythms of home and farm with the clock, the bell and the machine, and made discipline, not skill, the first demand on workers.
ss|16|l36-5|9-10|The Railroad Age — Railroads shrank distance, created a new market for iron and coal, and set the pace of an economy that now ran on timetables.
ss|16|l36-6|9-10|The Shock City — Industrial cities grew faster than anyone could plan for, packing workers into crowded, unsanitary housing where disease killed the young in staggering numbers.
ss|16|l36-7|9-10|Children, Inspectors and the Law — Investigations into child labor produced the first factory laws, which were weak at first but established that government could regulate work.
ss|16|l36-8|9-10|Unions and the Strike — Workers learned that acting together gave them power an individual lacked, and they built unions, strikes and political movements despite laws against them.
ss|16|l36-9|9-10|Marx and the Critics of Capitalism — Thinkers from Adam Smith to Karl Marx argued about whether the new economy was fair and whether it should be left alone, reformed or overthrown.
ss|16|l36-10|9-10|Europe, America and Japan Catch Up — Industry spread beyond Britain when other nations copied its machines and added their own resources, banks and government support.
ss|16|l36-11|9-10|Steel, Wires and Light — A second wave of technology after 1850, built on steel, electricity and the telegraph, made industry faster, bigger and more connected than the age of steam.
ss|16|l36-12|9-10|New Air, New Time, New Lives — Industrialization remade the environment and daily life, bringing cheaper goods, longer lives and new freedoms alongside smoke, pollution and a faster world.
ss|16|l37-1|9-10|Italy Made by Diplomacy and Volunteers — Italy was united between 1859 and 1870 by a combination of Cavour's careful diplomacy in the north and Garibaldi's volunteer army in the south.
ss|16|l37-2|9-10|Bismarck and the German Empire — Otto von Bismarck united Germany under Prussia through three short wars, creating the most powerful state in Europe and a new balance of power.
ss|16|l37-3|9-10|Japan Remakes Itself — After being forced open by American warships, Japan's Meiji leaders overturned their old order and built a modern state strong enough to defeat a European empire by 1905.
ss|16|l37-4|9-10|Berlin Draws the Lines — Between 1870 and 1914 European powers divided nearly all of Africa among themselves, setting the rules at a conference in Berlin to which no African was invited.
ss|16|l37-5|9-10|Why Empire: Motives and Excuses — Europeans took empires for profit, power and prestige, and justified them with claims about race, religion and civilization that many people at the time also challenged.
ss|16|l37-6|9-10|Rubber, Resistance and Adwa — Colonial rule in Africa ranged from brutal forced labor in the Congo to indirect rule elsewhere, and Africans resisted by war, by revolt and by organizing, with Ethiopia keeping its independence.
ss|16|l37-7|9-10|India Under the Company and the Crown — A trading company came to rule India, a massive rebellion in 1857 nearly ended that rule, and afterward the British Crown governed India directly for ninety years.
ss|16|l37-8|9-10|China's Century of Humiliation — From the Opium War of 1839 to the Boxer uprising of 1900, foreign powers forced China to give up ports, rights and land, while the Qing dynasty weakened from within.
ss|16|l37-9|9-10|Living Under Empire — Empire changed daily life for the ruled through law, school, work and disease, and colonized people used the rulers' own tools and ideas to challenge them.
ss|16|l37-10|9-10|Alliances and Arms — Between 1879 and 1907 Europe divided into two armed alliance blocs, so that a quarrel between any two powers threatened to pull in all of them.
ss|16|l37-11|9-10|A Spark Waiting — By 1914 the world was more connected, more armed and more divided than ever, and a murder in the Balkans set off the alliances that turned a local crisis into a world war.
ss|17|l38-1|9-10|Why Europe Went to War — World War I began when a single assassination pulled a web of alliances, rivalries and war plans into a conflict that no single government fully chose.
ss|17|l38-2|9-10|Life and Death in the Trenches — New weapons made attacking almost suicidal, so the Western Front froze into trenches where millions died to move the line a few miles.
ss|17|l38-3|9-10|The War at Home — Total war pulled whole societies into the fight: factories, farms, women's labor, food rationing and government control of what people could say.
ss|17|l38-4|9-10|Russia's Two Revolutions — War and hunger toppled the Russian tsar in March 1917, and eight months later Lenin's Bolsheviks seized power and pulled Russia out of the war.
ss|17|l38-5|9-10|The Treaty That Settled Nothing — The Treaty of Versailles punished Germany, redrew the map of Europe and created a League of Nations, but left grievances that helped bring the next war.
ss|17|l38-6|9-10|A Depression Around the World — The crash of 1929 spread through debts, trade and banks until a quarter of workers in some countries had no job, and desperate people looked for anyone with answers.
ss|17|l38-7|9-10|Dictators of the 1930s — Mussolini, Hitler and Stalin built states that claimed total control over their people, using terror, propaganda and the promise of national greatness.
ss|17|l38-8|9-10|The Road to War — Through the 1930s Japan, Italy and Germany seized territory step by step while the democracies, hoping to avoid another war, gave way until they could not.
ss|17|l38-9|9-10|A War on Every Ocean — World War II was fought across Europe, Asia, Africa and the Pacific; the turning points came at Stalingrad and Midway, and it ended with the total defeat of Germany and Japan.
ss|17|l38-10|9-10|The Holocaust — Nazi Germany and its helpers murdered about six million Jews and millions of others in a planned, industrial killing that we know about through the killers' own records and the survivors' testimony.
ss|17|l38-11|9-10|The Bomb — Scientists working in secret, including at the University of Chicago, built a weapon that destroyed two cities in August 1945 and changed what war could mean.
ss|17|l38-12|9-10|A New Order — The victors of 1945 built the United Nations to prevent another world war, but power now rested with two superpowers that were already drifting apart.
ss|17|l39-1|9-10|The Iron Curtain Falls — Between 1945 and 1949 the wartime alliance broke into two hostile blocs, one led by the United States and one by the Soviet Union, divided by what Churchill called an iron curtain.
ss|17|l39-2|9-10|Marshall Plan, NATO and the Warsaw Pact — The United States rebuilt Western Europe with money and bound it with a military alliance, and the Soviet Union answered with its own bloc.
ss|17|l39-3|9-10|Living with the Bomb — Both superpowers built enough nuclear weapons to destroy each other many times over, and the fear of that outcome kept them talking even as they competed.
ss|17|l39-4|9-10|China's Revolution and the Korean War — Communist victory in China in 1949 and the invasion of South Korea in 1950 turned the Cold War into shooting wars in Asia.
ss|17|l39-5|9-10|Thirteen Days over Cuba — In October 1962 the discovery of Soviet missiles in Cuba brought the superpowers closer to nuclear war than at any other moment, and both leaders chose to step back.
ss|17|l39-6|9-10|Vietnam and Afghanistan — Each superpower fought a long war against a determined local enemy, in Vietnam and in Afghanistan, and each lost, at great cost to the people whose country it was.
ss|17|l39-7|9-10|Gandhi and the Partition of India — Gandhi's movement of nonviolent resistance helped end British rule in India in 1947, but independence came with a partition that killed hundreds of thousands.
ss|17|l39-8|9-10|Africa Wins Independence — Between 1957 and the mid-1970s almost every colony in Africa became independent, some by negotiation and some by war, but colonial borders and economies remained.
ss|17|l39-9|9-10|The Middle East after 1948 — The creation of Israel in 1948, the Palestinian refugees it produced, oil, and superpower rivalry made the Middle East one of the Cold War's most dangerous regions.
ss|17|l39-10|9-10|Apartheid and Its End — South Africa built a legal system of racial separation in 1948 and dismantled it in 1994 after decades of resistance, world pressure, and a negotiated transfer of power.
ss|17|l39-11|9-10|1989: The Year of Revolutions — In a single year, peaceful movements across Eastern Europe toppled communist governments because the Soviet Union, under Gorbachev, would no longer send tanks.
ss|17|l39-12|9-10|The Soviet Union Dissolves — Reforms meant to save the Soviet Union instead released the forces that broke it apart, and on Christmas Day 1991 it ceased to exist.
ss|17|l40-1|9-10|Wired Together — In one generation the internet went from a scientists' tool to the nervous system of the world, changing how people work, learn, shop and argue.
ss|17|l40-2|9-10|Trade Without Borders — Falling shipping costs and new trade agreements tied the world's economies together, creating enormous wealth and sharp losers at the same time.
ss|17|l40-3|9-10|China Rises — After 1978 China combined one-party rule with market reforms and became the world's factory and its second-largest economy, changing the balance of power.
ss|17|l40-4|9-10|Europe Unites, Mostly — Countries that had fought two world wars built a union with open borders and a shared currency, and then discovered how hard it is to share decisions.
ss|17|l40-5|9-10|September 11 and the Wars After — The attacks of September 11, 2001, led the United States into two long wars whose costs and results are still debated.
ss|17|l40-6|9-10|People on the Move — More people live outside the country of their birth than ever before, and the world's response to migrants and refugees divides nations and neighbors.
ss|17|l40-7|9-10|Rights Beyond Borders — Since 1945 the world has written rules that say governments may not do anything they like to their own people, and has struggled to enforce them.
ss|17|l40-8|9-10|A Warming Planet — Burning coal, oil and gas has raised the amount of carbon dioxide in the air and warmed the Earth, and no country can fix it alone.
ss|17|l40-9|9-10|Disease in a Connected World — Diseases travel as fast as people do, and pandemics from 1918 to COVID-19 show both what global cooperation can do and how easily it fails.
ss|17|l40-10|9-10|The Widening Gap — The world has grown richer and less poor at the same time that wealth within many countries has concentrated at the top, and both trends shape politics.
ss|17|l40-11|9-10|Where the News Comes From — Every story you see was chosen, shaped and delivered by someone with reasons, and knowing how news is made is the first step to judging it.
ss|17|l40-12|9-10|Checking What You See — A few habits, such as pausing, reading laterally, tracing to the original, and asking what would change your mind, turn a reader into a citizen who is hard to fool.
ss|18|l41-1|9-10|Where Eight Billion People Live — People are spread very unevenly across the Earth, and the pattern follows water, soil, climate and history.
ss|18|l41-2|9-10|The Demographic Transition — As countries industrialize, death rates fall first and birth rates fall later, and the gap between them produces a burst of population growth.
ss|18|l41-3|9-10|Reading a Population Pyramid — A population pyramid shows a country's age and sex structure, and its shape reveals past events and future needs.
ss|18|l41-4|9-10|Push, Pull and the Costs Between — People migrate when something pushes them from home, something pulls them elsewhere, and the obstacles in between are not too high.
ss|18|l41-5|9-10|The Great Migration to Chicago — Between 1910 and 1970 about six million Black Americans left the South, and Chicago became one of their main destinations.
ss|18|l41-6|9-10|Migration Today — International migration today is driven by wages, war and family, and Illinois's population depends on it.
ss|18|l41-7|9-10|Language Families on the Map — The world's roughly seven thousand languages descend from a smaller number of families, and their map records ancient migrations and modern empires.
ss|18|l41-8|9-10|Religion on the Map — The world's major religions each have a geography: a hearth where they began, routes by which they spread, and a landscape they shape.
ss|18|l41-9|9-10|Ethnicity, Identity and Conflict — Ethnic identity is built from shared history, language and culture, and when borders and power ignore it the result can be division or violence.
ss|18|l41-10|9-10|Folk Culture, Pop Culture and Landscape — Folk culture is local and slow to change, popular culture spreads fast and wide, and both leave their marks on the landscape.
ss|18|l41-11|9-10|Gender and Development — Where girls are educated and women can work and own property, birth rates fall, incomes rise and whole societies develop faster.
ss|18|l42-1|9-10|Why Cities Grow Where They Do — More than half of humanity now lives in cities, and cities grow at sites where transportation routes, resources and markets meet.
ss|18|l42-2|9-10|Models of the City — Geographers use simple models, rings, wedges and multiple centers, to explain how land use sorts itself inside a city.
ss|18|l42-3|9-10|Life in the Fast-Growing City — Rapid urbanization brings opportunity and severe strain, from housing and water to transport, and cities handle it very differently.
ss|18|l42-4|9-10|From Subsistence to Agribusiness — Farming ranges from families growing their own food to global corporations, and Illinois sits at the industrial end of that range.
ss|18|l42-5|9-10|Industry and the Global Division of Labor — Factories locate where materials, labor, markets and transport line up, and since the 1970s much manufacturing has moved across the world in search of lower costs.
ss|18|l42-6|9-10|Measuring Development — Development is measured by income, health and education together, and every measure has strengths and blind spots.
ss|18|l42-7|9-10|Powering the World — About four-fifths of the world's energy still comes from coal, oil and gas, and shifting that mix is the central resource question of this century.
ss|18|l42-8|9-10|Water and Food Security — Fresh water and food are unevenly distributed, and securing them depends as much on politics and infrastructure as on rainfall.
ss|18|l42-9|9-10|Climate, Change and Sustainability — Human activity is warming the climate, the effects fall unevenly across the map, and sustainability means meeting needs now without wrecking the future.
ss|18|l42-10|9-10|States, Nations and Borders — A state is a territory with a government; a nation is a people; the two rarely match perfectly, and borders are drawn by history, not nature.
ss|18|l42-11|9-10|Conflict and Cooperation Across Lines — Borders create conflict over land, people and resources, but states also cooperate through treaties, alliances and shared rules.
ss|19|l43-1|9-10|Where the Ideas Came From — The founders borrowed ideas about natural rights, consent, and divided power from European thinkers of the Enlightenment.
ss|19|l43-2|9-10|Practice in the Colonies — Long before independence, colonists governed themselves through local assemblies and expected to be taxed only with their consent.
ss|19|l43-3|9-10|Declaring the Reasons — The Declaration of Independence stated a theory of government and a list of evidence, and its words have been claimed by every later movement for rights.
ss|19|l43-4|9-10|A First Try That Failed — The Articles of Confederation created a national government too weak to tax, regulate trade, or keep order, and its failures pushed leaders toward a new plan.
ss|19|l43-5|9-10|The Deals of 1787 — The Constitution was built from compromises over representation and slavery that satisfied no one completely and shaped the country for generations.
ss|19|l43-6|9-10|The Fight to Ratify — Federalists and Anti-Federalists argued in print over whether the new government was too powerful, and the Bill of Rights was the price of ratification.
ss|19|l43-7|9-10|The Shape of the Document — The Constitution is a short framework of seven articles, and its amendment process is deliberately hard so that change requires wide agreement.
ss|19|l43-8|9-10|Federalism, Then and Now — Federalism divides power between the national government and the states, and the balance between them has shifted repeatedly through history.
ss|19|l43-9|9-10|Three Branches, Many Brakes — Separation of powers gives each branch its own job, and checks and balances give each branch tools to block the others.
ss|19|l43-10|9-10|Checks Under Pressure — Real crises, from a seized steel industry to a president's secret tapes, show the branches actually using their checks against one another.
ss|19|l43-11|9-10|Illinois Builds Its Own — Illinois has had four constitutions, and its current one, written in 1970, shares the federal design while giving voters and the governor different tools.
ss|19|l44-1|9-10|Two Kinds of Protection — Civil liberties are limits on what government may do to you; civil rights are guarantees that government will treat you equally.
ss|19|l44-2|9-10|The First Amendment in Court — The First Amendment's forty-five words have been defined case by case, and the rule today protects even hateful speech unless it is meant to cause immediate lawless action.
ss|19|l44-3|9-10|Fair Process, Equal Law — The Fourteenth Amendment requires states to follow fair procedures before taking life, liberty, or property and to treat people equally under the law.
ss|19|l44-4|9-10|Bringing the Bill of Rights Home — The Bill of Rights originally limited only the national government; through the Fourteenth Amendment, the Supreme Court has applied nearly all of it to the states, one right at a time.
ss|19|l44-5|9-10|The Court Claims Its Power — Marbury against Madison established judicial review, and McCulloch against Maryland established that national power reaches beyond the words listed in the Constitution.
ss|19|l44-6|9-10|Equal Schools, Equal Trials — Brown against Board of Education ended legal school segregation, and Gideon against Wainwright guaranteed poor defendants a lawyer; both turned paper rights into enforced ones.
ss|19|l44-7|9-10|Warnings, Armbands and Marriage — Miranda, Tinker, and Obergefell each extended a right into a new place: the police station, the school, and the marriage license office.
ss|19|l44-8|9-10|Elections and Parties — Elections turn opinions into power through primaries, general elections, and the Electoral College, and parties organize the choices voters face.
ss|19|l44-9|9-10|Groups, Money and Media — Interest groups, campaign money, and the news media all shape what government does between elections, and each is protected speech with real costs.
ss|19|l44-10|9-10|Measuring What People Think — Polls estimate public opinion from a random sample, and a good poll reports its margin of error while a bad sample can be spectacularly wrong.
ss|19|l44-11|9-10|Getting on the Rolls — Voting requires registering first, and Illinois makes registration easier than most states; younger citizens still vote far less than older ones.
ss|19|l44-12|9-10|Making Yourself Heard — Between elections, citizens change policy by contacting officials, speaking at public meetings, and organizing others; the tools work best when they are specific and numerous.
ss|19|l44-13|9-10|Serving and Running — Citizenship includes duties, from jury service to military registration, and the most direct form of participation is running for office or serving on a local board.
ss|2|l3-1|k-2|People Near Each Other — A community is the people who live and work near each other.
ss|2|l3-2|k-2|City, Town and Country — Communities can be big cities, small towns or the open country.
ss|2|l3-3|k-2|Helpers Who Keep Us Safe — Firefighters and nurses have jobs that keep people safe and well.
ss|2|l3-4|k-2|Helpers Who Bring and Carry — Mail carriers and bus drivers bring things and people where they need to go.
ss|2|l3-5|k-2|Every Job Matters — A librarian and a farmer help too, and every job in a community matters.
ss|2|l3-6|k-2|Places We Share — A community has places that everyone can use, like a park or a library.
ss|2|l3-7|k-2|Fixing a Problem Together — People in a community can solve a problem by each doing a small part.
ss|2|l4-1|k-2|Things We Must Have — A need is something you must have to live, like food, water, shelter and clothes.
ss|2|l4-2|k-2|Things We Would Like — A want is something you would like to have but can live without.
ss|2|l4-3|k-2|Working to Earn — People work at jobs to earn money.
ss|2|l4-4|k-2|Goods and Services — Money buys goods, which are things, and services, which is work someone does for you.
ss|2|l4-5|k-2|You Cannot Buy It All — Money runs out, so you must choose what to buy.
ss|2|l4-6|k-2|Saving Up — Saving means keeping money now so you can buy something bigger later.
ss|2|l4-7|k-2|Trading and Sharing — People can trade or share to get things without using money.
ss|2|l4-8|k-2|Farm to Store to Table — Food starts on a farm, travels to a store, and ends up on your table.
ss|2|l4-9|k-2|Food Is Work Too — Growing food is a job, and the food we buy pays the people who grew it.
ss|20|l45-1|11-12|The Bargain of 1877 — Reconstruction ended when a disputed election was settled by pulling federal troops out of the South, leaving Black Southerners to face their former masters alone.
ss|20|l45-2|11-12|Jim Crow and the Color Line — Southern states built a legal system of segregation and disfranchisement, and in 1896 the Supreme Court said the Constitution allowed it.
ss|20|l45-3|11-12|Joining the Coasts by Rail — The transcontinental railroad, finished in 1869, was built with government land, borrowed money and immigrant labor, and it made a single national market possible.
ss|20|l45-4|11-12|Carnegie and the Age of Steel — Andrew Carnegie used new technology and ruthless cost-cutting to make America the world's steel leader, then argued the rich owed their fortunes to the public.
ss|20|l45-5|11-12|Rockefeller, Trusts and the Law — John D. Rockefeller built a near-monopoly in oil through secret deals and the trust, and Congress answered in 1890 with the first antitrust law.
ss|20|l45-6|11-12|Immigrants and the New City — Millions of immigrants from southern and eastern Europe, and from Asia, filled the industrial cities, met suspicion and built the neighborhoods that made the cities work.
ss|20|l45-7|11-12|Chicago Rises From the Ashes — After the fire of 1871 Chicago rebuilt itself into the fastest-growing city in the world, the hub of railroads, grain, lumber and meat, and the birthplace of the skyscraper.
ss|20|l45-8|11-12|Haymarket and the Eight-Hour Day — A bomb at a Chicago labor rally in 1886 led to a trial that punished ideas more than deeds, set back the union movement and gave the world May Day.
ss|20|l45-9|11-12|Pullman and the Power of Government — The Pullman strike of 1894 showed that the federal government would use courts and troops on the side of employers, and it made Eugene Debs a national figure.
ss|20|l45-10|11-12|Farmers Organize: The Populists — Falling crop prices and railroad power pushed farmers to build their own party, the Populists, whose ideas outlived their defeat.
ss|20|l45-11|11-12|The Plains Wars and the Dawes Act — As railroads and settlers crossed the Plains, the United States broke treaties, destroyed the buffalo, forced Native nations onto reservations and then tried to break up the reservations themselves.
ss|20|l45-12|11-12|The White City of 1893 — The Chicago World's Fair of 1893 showed the country an ideal of order and progress, while the real city around it slid into depression and strike.
ss|20|l46-1|11-12|Muckrakers and the Jungle — Investigative journalists exposed corruption and dangerous products to a mass readership, and public outrage forced Congress to act.
ss|20|l46-2|11-12|Children in the Mills — Progressives fought child labor with photographs, state laws and a federal law the Supreme Court struck down, and the fight was not finished for another generation.
ss|20|l46-3|11-12|The Long Road to Suffrage — Women won the vote state by state and then by constitutional amendment, using patient lobbying, mass parades and civil disobedience, and Illinois was an early turning point.
ss|20|l46-4|11-12|Roosevelt and the Square Deal — Theodore Roosevelt made the presidency an active force, breaking a railroad trust, settling a coal strike and protecting vast public lands.
ss|20|l46-5|11-12|Taft, Wilson and the Election of 1912 — The 1912 election split the Republicans and put Woodrow Wilson in office, and together the three presidents remade the Constitution, the banking system and the tax code.
ss|20|l46-6|11-12|A Splendid Little War — In 1898 the United States went to war with Spain over Cuba and, in ten weeks, became an overseas power with colonies in the Caribbean and the Pacific.
ss|20|l46-7|11-12|The Philippines and the Debate Over Empire — Filipinos who had fought Spain for independence fought the United States for three more years, in a war that killed far more people than the war with Spain and split American opinion.
ss|20|l46-8|11-12|Cutting the Isthmus — Roosevelt got the Panama Canal built by backing a revolt against Colombia, and American doctors and West Indian laborers made the impossible dig possible.
ss|20|l46-9|11-12|The Great Migration Comes to Chicago — During the First World War hundreds of thousands of Black Southerners moved north for jobs and freedom, and Chicago became a capital of Black America.
ss|20|l46-10|11-12|Over There: America in the War — The United States stayed out of the First World War for nearly three years, then sent two million soldiers to France and helped decide the outcome in 1918.
ss|20|l46-11|11-12|The War at Home — Fighting the war meant organizing the whole economy and selling the war to the public, and the government's campaign against dissent tested the meaning of free speech.
ss|20|l46-12|11-12|Red Summer and the Vote — In 1919 a stone thrown at a Chicago beach set off a week of racial violence that exposed the limits of the migration's promise, even as women finally won the vote nationwide.
ss|21|l47-1|11-12|Cars, Radio and Credit — Mass production, installment buying and radio changed daily life in the 1920s and tied the economy to consumer spending.
ss|21|l47-2|11-12|Jazz, Harlem and the Dry Decade — The 1920s produced a burst of Black art and music and a failed national experiment in banning alcohol.
ss|21|l47-3|11-12|Nativism and the Klan's Return — Fear of immigrants, radicals and Black Americans drove strict immigration quotas and a huge revival of the Ku Klux Klan in the 1920s.
ss|21|l47-4|11-12|October 1929 — The stock market crash of 1929 did not cause the Depression by itself, but it exposed a weak economy built on debt and speculation.
ss|21|l47-5|11-12|Hard Times and Hoover — By 1933 a quarter of workers had no job, and President Hoover's limited responses left many Americans feeling abandoned.
ss|21|l47-6|11-12|The Dust Bowl — Drought, plowed-up grassland and high winds turned the southern Plains into a dust bowl and drove hundreds of thousands off the land.
ss|21|l47-7|11-12|The Hundred Days — In his first hundred days Roosevelt and Congress passed a flood of laws that put the federal government directly into banking, farming, industry and relief.
ss|21|l47-8|11-12|Work, Wages and Security — The Second New Deal of 1935 created lasting institutions: public jobs through the WPA, the right to unionize, and Social Security.
ss|21|l47-9|11-12|Critics Left and Right — The New Deal was attacked from the right as socialism and from the left as too timid, and the loudest critics pushed Roosevelt further.
ss|21|l47-10|11-12|Labor Rises — Protected by the Wagner Act, industrial unions organized the auto and steel industries through sit-down strikes, and Chicago saw the cost.
ss|21|l47-11|11-12|Roosevelt and the Court — After the Supreme Court struck down key New Deal laws, Roosevelt tried to add justices, lost the fight, and yet won the Court.
ss|21|l47-12|11-12|The Argument That Never Ended — The New Deal did not end the Depression, but it permanently changed what Americans expect government to do, and the debate over that role continues.
ss|21|l48-1|11-12|Staying Out — Most Americans in the 1930s wanted no part of another European war, and Congress wrote that wish into law until Roosevelt found ways around it.
ss|21|l48-2|11-12|Pearl Harbor — Japan's surprise attack on December 7, 1941, ended the debate over neutrality in a single morning and brought the United States into a global war.
ss|21|l48-3|11-12|Arsenal of Democracy — The war ended the Depression by turning the whole economy toward production, and the federal government directed it.
ss|21|l48-4|11-12|Women and War Work — With millions of men in uniform, women took factory jobs once closed to them, and the war changed ideas about women's work even though most were pushed out when it ended.
ss|21|l48-5|11-12|Japanese American Incarceration — In 1942 the government removed about one hundred twenty thousand people of Japanese ancestry, most of them citizens, from the West Coast and held them in camps without charges.
ss|21|l48-6|11-12|Europe: From North Africa to D-Day — The Allies defeated Germany by wearing it down on the Eastern Front, bombing it from the air and landing in France on June 6, 1944.
ss|21|l48-7|11-12|The Pacific: Island by Island — After turning the tide at Midway, the United States fought across the Pacific island by island in a brutal campaign that made an invasion of Japan look unbearable.
ss|21|l48-8|11-12|The Holocaust and America's Response — Nazi Germany murdered six million Jews and millions of others, and the United States, which knew much of what was happening, did little to rescue them until late in the war.
ss|21|l48-9|11-12|The Manhattan Project — Fearing a German bomb, the United States built a secret atomic weapon in three years, and Chicago was where the key experiment happened.
ss|21|l48-10|11-12|Hiroshima and Nagasaki — Two atomic bombs in August 1945 killed well over a hundred thousand people and ended the war, and the decision to use them is still debated on the evidence.
ss|21|l48-11|11-12|A New World and the GI Bill — The war left the United States the richest and most powerful nation on earth, committed to a new United Nations and to sending millions of veterans to college.
ss|21|l49-1|11-12|The Truman Doctrine and the Marshall Plan — Facing Soviet control of Eastern Europe, the United States adopted containment: using money and commitments to stop communism from spreading further.
ss|21|l49-2|11-12|Berlin, NATO and the Bomb — The Berlin airlift, the NATO alliance and the Soviet atomic bomb in 1949 turned the Cold War into a permanent, armed standoff.
ss|21|l49-3|11-12|Korea — The Korean War of 1950 to 1953 was the first shooting war of containment; it cost about thirty-six thousand American lives and ended where it began.
ss|21|l49-4|11-12|McCarthyism — Fear of Communist spies fed a wave of investigations and blacklists that ruined thousands of careers, until Senator McCarthy overreached on television.
ss|21|l49-5|11-12|Suburbs, Babies and Highways — Postwar prosperity, cheap home loans and new highways moved millions of families to the suburbs, and left cities and Black families behind.
ss|21|l49-6|11-12|The Space Race — A Soviet satellite in 1957 shocked the United States into a race for space that put Americans on the Moon twelve years later.
ss|21|l49-7|11-12|After the Missile Crisis — The Cuban Missile Crisis frightened both superpowers into the first steps toward limiting nuclear weapons, even as the arms race continued.
