ss|21|l49-8|11-12|Johnson's Great Society — Lyndon Johnson used his skill in Congress and a landslide election to pass Medicare, Medicaid, federal school aid and a war on poverty.
ss|21|l49-9|11-12|The War in Vietnam — The United States sent more than half a million troops to stop communism in Vietnam, could not win, and lost about fifty-eight thousand lives before leaving.
ss|21|l49-10|11-12|Protest and the Counterculture — The war and the draft produced the largest antiwar movement in American history and a youth culture that rejected its parents' rules.
ss|21|l49-11|11-12|Chicago, August 1968 — The 1968 Democratic convention in Chicago turned into a televised clash between police and protesters that split the party and helped elect Nixon.
ss|22|l50-1|11-12|Brown v. Board of Education — In 1954 the Supreme Court ruled that racially segregated public schools are unconstitutional, overturning the separate-but-equal rule from 1896.
ss|22|l50-2|11-12|Emmett Till and Chicago — The murder of a Chicago teenager in Mississippi in 1955, and his mother's decision to show it, turned a private crime into a national reckoning.
ss|22|l50-3|11-12|Montgomery Walks — The 1955 Montgomery bus boycott showed that a whole Black community, organized and patient, could defeat segregation with its feet and its dollars.
ss|22|l50-4|11-12|Sitting In — Beginning in 1960, Black college students used sit-ins at segregated lunch counters to take the movement into their own hands.
ss|22|l50-5|11-12|The Freedom Rides — In 1961 integrated groups rode buses into the Deep South to test a federal ruling, and the violence they met forced the government to enforce it.
ss|22|l50-6|11-12|Birmingham, 1963 — In Birmingham, the movement chose the most segregated city in America and let the nation watch police attack children, which pushed a president to act.
ss|22|l50-7|11-12|The March on Washington — On August 28, 1963, about a quarter of a million people gathered at the Lincoln Memorial to demand jobs and freedom, and made the movement a national majority cause.
ss|22|l50-8|11-12|Freedom Summer — In 1964 civil rights workers brought hundreds of volunteers into Mississippi to register voters and open schools, at the cost of three lives and dozens of bombings.
ss|22|l50-9|11-12|Selma and the Bridge — The 1965 Selma campaign, and the attack on marchers at the Edmund Pettus Bridge, produced the Voting Rights Act within five months.
ss|22|l50-10|11-12|Two Laws That Changed the Country — The Civil Rights Act of 1964 and the Voting Rights Act of 1965 put the federal government behind equal treatment and the ballot, and the numbers changed fast.
ss|22|l50-11|11-12|King Comes to Chicago — In 1966 the movement turned north to Chicago, where segregation was enforced not by law but by housing practices, and found it far harder to break.
ss|22|l50-12|11-12|Malcolm X and Black Power — Malcolm X and the Black Power movement argued that self-defense, pride and control of Black communities mattered as much as integration.
ss|22|l50-13|11-12|The Movements That Followed — The methods and laws of the Black freedom struggle were taken up by women, Latinos, Native Americans, people with disabilities and gay Americans.
ss|22|l50-14|11-12|Strategies and Costs — The movement won by combining courts, direct action, media and the vote, and it paid in lives, jobs, and years that its participants never got back.
ss|22|l51-1|11-12|Watergate — Watergate began as a burglary and ended a presidency because tape recordings proved the president had obstructed justice, and because courts, Congress and the press each did their job.
ss|22|l51-2|11-12|Trust After Watergate — Vietnam, Watergate and revelations about the CIA and FBI drove public trust in government down sharply, and Congress answered with laws that limit presidents and open records.
ss|22|l51-3|11-12|Oil Shocks and Stagflation — In the 1970s two oil shocks and rising prices combined with high unemployment into stagflation, a problem the old economic playbook could not solve, and the cure of the early 1980s was painful.
ss|22|l51-4|11-12|Reagan and the Right — Ronald Reagan, born in Illinois, led a conservative movement that cut taxes, trimmed regulation, built up the military and reset the debate about the size of government.
ss|22|l51-5|11-12|The Cold War Ends — Between 1985 and 1991 the Cold War ended without a shot fired between the superpowers, through American pressure, Soviet reform and the choices of ordinary people in Eastern Europe.
ss|22|l51-6|11-12|The Nineties and the Internet — The 1990s brought a long economic expansion, a budget surplus, bitter partisan fights and a new network, built in part in Illinois, that changed daily life.
ss|22|l51-7|11-12|September 11 — On September 11, 2001, nineteen al-Qaeda hijackers killed nearly three thousand people in under two hours, and the attacks reshaped American law, war and daily life.
ss|22|l51-8|11-12|The Wars That Followed — The United States fought in Afghanistan for twenty years and in Iraq for nearly nine, wars that cost trillions of dollars and thousands of lives and whose results are still debated.
ss|22|l51-9|11-12|The Crash of 2008 — A housing bubble built on risky mortgages and complex financial bets burst in 2008, causing the worst downturn since the Depression and a fierce argument over bailouts.
ss|22|l51-10|11-12|A President from Illinois — Barack Obama rose through Chicago organizing and Illinois politics to become the first Black president, and his eight years show how a presidency is shaped by crisis and opposition.
ss|22|l51-11|11-12|Polarization, Screens and a Pandemic — Since about 2010 American politics has grown more polarized, social media has changed how people learn and argue, and the COVID-19 pandemic tested government and trust at the same time.
ss|22|l51-12|11-12|Writing History While It Happens — The recent past is the hardest history to write because the sources are incomplete, the outcomes unknown and the historian is a participant, so historians use rules to keep themselves honest.
ss|23|l52-1|11-12|Committees Do the Work — Most of what Congress decides is decided in committees, where a few dozen members shape a bill before the whole chamber ever sees it.
ss|23|l52-2|11-12|The Filibuster and the Sixty-Vote Senate — Because the Senate lets debate run until sixty senators agree to end it, most major bills need sixty votes there, not fifty-one.
ss|23|l52-3|11-12|The Budget and the Power of the Purse — Congress controls federal spending through yearly appropriations bills, but most of the budget is set by permanent law and does not come up for a vote.
ss|23|l52-4|11-12|What a President Can and Cannot Do — The president's formal powers are few and shared with Congress, so much of presidential power comes from persuasion, appointments and the size of the executive branch.
ss|23|l52-5|11-12|Executive Orders and War Powers — Executive orders and military action let presidents act fast without Congress, and the limits on both are still argued over.
ss|23|l52-6|11-12|The Bureaucracy and the Rules It Writes — Federal agencies turn broad laws into detailed rules and enforce them, which makes the unelected bureaucracy powerful and controversial.
ss|23|l52-7|11-12|Judicial Review — Since Marbury v. Madison in 1803, courts have held the power to strike down laws and government actions that conflict with the Constitution.
ss|23|l52-8|11-12|How a Case Reaches the Supreme Court — Almost every case starts in a trial court and reaches the Supreme Court only if at least four justices choose to hear it, which happens for fewer than one case in a hundred.
ss|23|l52-9|11-12|How Judges Read the Constitution — Judges disagree about whether the Constitution's meaning is fixed at the time it was written or should be read in light of present conditions, and that disagreement shapes rulings.
ss|23|l52-10|11-12|Government in Illinois — Illinois runs its own three-branch government under a 1970 constitution, and its nearly seven thousand local governments touch daily life more than Washington does.
ss|23|l52-11|11-12|Money, Maps and the Electoral College — Who wins American elections depends not only on votes but on rules about campaign money, district lines and the Electoral College, all of which are contested.
ss|23|l52-12|11-12|Evaluating a Claim — A citizen's most useful skill is checking a claim: finding who is behind it, what others say, and whether the evidence actually supports it.
ss|23|l53-1|11-12|Scarcity and Opportunity Cost — Because wants outrun resources, every choice costs the next-best thing you gave up, and economics is the study of how people and nations make those choices.
ss|23|l53-2|11-12|Incentives Change Behavior — People respond to rewards and penalties, so a change in a price, tax or rule changes what people do, often in ways the rule-maker did not intend.
ss|23|l53-3|11-12|Supply, Demand and Price — A market price moves toward the point where the amount buyers want equals the amount sellers offer, and anything that shifts supply or demand moves that price.
ss|23|l53-4|11-12|Competition and Market Structure — Markets range from many small sellers to a single seller, and the fewer the sellers, the more power each has to raise prices above what competition would allow.
ss|23|l53-5|11-12|When Markets Fail — Markets misprice things when costs fall on outsiders, when no one can be made to pay for a shared good, or when one side knows far more than the other.
ss|23|l53-6|11-12|Rules and Their Trade-offs — Government rules can correct market failures, but every rule has costs, can be captured by the industry it regulates, and has to be judged by evidence rather than intention.
ss|23|l53-7|11-12|GDP, Inflation and Unemployment — Three numbers describe the health of a whole economy: how much it produces, how fast prices rise, and what share of people who want work cannot find it.
ss|23|l53-8|11-12|Booms, Busts and the Federal Budget — Economies swing between expansion and recession, Congress can lean against the swing with spending and taxes, and decades of doing so has built a national debt about the size of a year's GDP.
ss|23|l53-9|11-12|The Federal Reserve — The Federal Reserve steers the economy by moving interest rates, trying to keep both inflation and unemployment low, and it is built to act faster than Congress and with less political pressure.
ss|23|l53-10|11-12|Trade and Tariffs — Trade lets countries specialize in what they do best and makes goods cheaper, but it also moves jobs, and tariffs protect some workers at a cost to others.
ss|23|l53-11|11-12|Budgets, Saving and Credit — A budget is a plan for scarce dollars, compound interest makes saved money grow and borrowed money grow against you, and a credit score records how you have handled both.
ss|23|l53-12|11-12|Investing, Insurance and College — Investing trades risk for growth, insurance trades a small certain cost for protection against a large uncertain one, and college is the biggest investment most students will make.
ss|24|l54-1|11-12|A Question Worth Asking — Good historical questions are open, answerable from evidence, and matter to someone beyond the person asking.
ss|24|l54-2|11-12|Primary and Secondary Sources — A primary source comes from the time and people you are studying; a secondary source is a later account built from primary ones, and the same document can be either depending on your question.
ss|24|l54-3|11-12|Sourcing Before Reading — Before you trust what a source says, ask who made it, when, for whom and why; the answer changes what its words can prove.
ss|24|l54-4|11-12|Context and Corroboration — Put a source in its moment to understand it, then check it against other independent sources before you rely on it.
ss|24|l54-5|11-12|Perspective, Bias and Reliability — Every source has a perspective; bias is a perspective that distorts; and reliability depends on which question you are asking of the source.
ss|24|l54-6|11-12|Reading Numbers as Evidence — Statistics are sources too: ask who counted, what they counted, and what the numbers cannot show before you let them prove anything.
ss|24|l54-7|11-12|Oral History — Recorded interviews preserve voices the written record left out, but memory must be sourced and corroborated like any other evidence.
ss|24|l54-8|11-12|The Ethics of Research — Research on real people carries duties: consent, honesty, care for those who can be harmed, and credit for every idea that is not your own.
ss|24|l54-9|11-12|Claim, Evidence, Reasoning — A historical argument states a debatable claim, supports it with specific evidence, and explains in its own words why that evidence supports that claim.
ss|24|l54-10|11-12|Citation: The Trail Behind You — A citation tells a reader exactly where a piece of evidence came from so they can find it and judge it for themselves.
ss|24|l54-11|11-12|Why Historians Disagree — Interpretations change because new evidence appears, new questions are asked, and new people with different experiences join the conversation.
ss|24|l55-1|11-12|Four Doors Into the Past — The best inquiry questions start close to home, with a street, a school, a family or a law, because local questions have local records you can actually reach.
ss|24|l55-2|11-12|Can It Be Answered? — A good question is one you can answer with sources that exist and that you can reach in the time you have; test that before you fall in love with the question.
ss|24|l55-3|11-12|One Street, One Lawsuit — Following a single street name through the records shows how a local question opens onto a court case, a national movement and a debate about who a city chooses to honor.
ss|24|l55-4|11-12|Where Records Live — Records are kept by the institutions that made them, so knowing who would have written something down tells you where to look for it.
ss|24|l55-5|11-12|Interviews: Asking the Living — A good interview is planned, consented to, recorded and open-ended, so the person's memory becomes evidence you can quote and check rather than a story you half remember.
ss|24|l55-6|11-12|Following a Law's Trail — A law leaves a chain of records, from its text to the debates, the cases that tested it and the numbers that changed afterward, and each link can be checked.
ss|24|l55-7|11-12|From Sources to a Claim — A claim grows out of the sources and must be revised as they push back; the source table is the tool that keeps the evidence honest.
ss|24|l55-8|11-12|Answering the Counterclaim — The strongest argument states the best case against itself, concedes what is true in it, and then shows with evidence why the claim still holds.
ss|24|l55-9|11-12|Presenting to an Audience — The form of a presentation should follow the audience and the evidence; whatever the form, the claim, the best evidence and the counterclaim must all be visible.
ss|24|l55-10|11-12|Taking Informed Action — Informed action means using what your inquiry showed to make a specific, evidence-backed request of a specific person or body with the power to answer it.
ss|24|l55-11|11-12|What Changed in Your Thinking — An honest reflection names what you believed at the start, what evidence changed it, what you still do not know, and what you would do differently next time.
ss|3|l5-1|k-2|Looking Down on a Room — A map is a drawing of a place as seen from above.
ss|3|l5-2|k-2|The Map Key — A map key tells what each symbol on a map means.
ss|3|l5-3|k-2|Bigger and Bigger Maps — A map can show a room, a school or a whole neighborhood.
ss|3|l5-4|k-2|Near, Far, Left and Right — Words like near, far, left and right tell where a thing is.
ss|3|l5-5|k-2|North, South, East and West — North, south, east and west are the four main directions on a map.
ss|3|l5-6|k-2|The Globe Is a Model of Earth — A globe is a round model of Earth that shows land and water.
ss|3|l5-7|k-2|Finding Our Home — We live in Chicago, in the state of Illinois, in the United States.
ss|3|l5-8|k-2|A Route to School — A route is the path you take to get from one place to another.
ss|3|l6-1|k-2|Hills and Plains — Land can be high and bumpy, like a hill, or low and flat, like a plain.
ss|3|l6-2|k-2|Rivers, Lakes and the Ocean — Water on Earth comes in rivers, lakes and the huge, salty ocean.
ss|3|l6-3|k-2|Flat Illinois — Most of Illinois is flat prairie land, and much of it is now farmland.
ss|3|l6-4|k-2|Four Seasons in Illinois — Illinois has four seasons, and the weather changes with each one.
ss|3|l6-5|k-2|What Weather Makes Us Do — The weather shapes what people wear and what they do.
ss|3|l6-6|k-2|People Change Places — People change the land by building roads, planting crops and digging ponds.
ss|3|l6-7|k-2|Places Shape People — The land and water of a place shape the jobs and lives of the people there.
ss|3|l6-8|k-2|Taking Care of a Place — People can keep a place healthy by picking up litter, saving water and planting trees.
ss|4|l7-1|k-2|Yesterday, Today, Tomorrow — The past is before now, the present is now, and the future is after now.
ss|4|l7-2|k-2|Old Things Are Clues — Old photos and objects are clues that tell us about the past.
ss|4|l7-3|k-2|Getting From Here to There — How people travel has changed a lot over time.
ss|4|l7-4|k-2|Cooking and Talking — Cooking and talking to far-away people changed as new tools were made.
ss|4|l7-5|k-2|School Long Ago — Schools long ago had one room, one teacher and no computers.
ss|4|l7-6|k-2|A Town in One Hundred Years — A town can change a lot in one hundred years, and old maps and photos show how.
ss|4|l7-7|k-2|Families, Play and Food — Some things stay the same over time, like families, play and eating together.
ss|4|l7-8|k-2|A Timeline of My Life — A timeline puts events in order from first to last.
ss|4|l8-1|k-2|Stars and Stripes — The flag has fifty stars for fifty states and thirteen stripes for the first thirteen states.
ss|4|l8-2|k-2|The Pledge Is a Promise — The Pledge of Allegiance is a promise to be loyal to our country.
ss|4|l8-3|k-2|The Bald Eagle — The bald eagle is a symbol of our country because it is strong and free.
ss|4|l8-4|k-2|A Bell and a Statue — The Liberty Bell and the Statue of Liberty are symbols of freedom.
ss|4|l8-5|k-2|Days for Great Leaders — Some holidays remember people, like Martin Luther King Jr. and our presidents.
ss|4|l8-6|k-2|Days for Our Country — Independence Day is our country's birthday, and Veterans Day thanks people who served.
ss|4|l8-7|k-2|People Who Made a Difference — Lincoln, Tubman, Chavez and Addams each helped other people and made things fairer.
ss|4|l8-8|k-2|Vote, Help, Speak Up — A good citizen votes, helps others and speaks up when something is wrong.
ss|5|l9-1|3-5|Neighbors and Borders — Illinois sits in the middle of the country, with five neighbor states and a Great Lake around it.
ss|5|l9-2|3-5|Rivers That Draw the Lines — Great rivers form most of the borders of Illinois and carry its goods to the sea.
ss|5|l9-3|3-5|Lake Michigan — Lake Michigan gives Chicago its drinking water, its harbor and its cool breezes.
ss|5|l9-4|3-5|Ice Flattens the Land — Giant sheets of ice called glaciers scraped most of Illinois flat and left behind rich, crushed rock.
ss|5|l9-5|3-5|Grass, Roots and Black Soil — Thousands of years of prairie grass built the deep black soil that makes Illinois farmland so rich.
ss|5|l9-6|3-5|Chicago and the North — Northern Illinois holds Chicago and most of the state's people, jobs and factories.
ss|5|l9-7|3-5|Farmland and the Shawnee Hills — Central Illinois is flat farmland, while the far south rises into rocky, forested hills.
ss|5|l9-8|3-5|Seasons, Storms and Floods — Illinois has hot summers, cold winters and dangerous tornadoes and floods.
ss|5|l9-9|3-5|Soil, Coal and Water — The most valuable natural resources in Illinois are its soil, its coal and its water.
ss|5|l9-10|3-5|Corn and Soybeans — Illinois farms grow corn and soybeans that feed animals and people around the world.
ss|5|l9-11|3-5|People Change the Land — People have reshaped Illinois by draining, plowing, building and even turning a river around.
ss|5|l10-1|3-5|The City of Mounds — A thousand years ago, Cahokia near today's St. Louis was the largest city north of Mexico.
ss|5|l10-2|3-5|The Illinois People — The state is named for the Illinois, a group of related nations who lived along its rivers.
ss|5|l10-3|3-5|French Forts and Fur Traders — For about a century, French traders and soldiers lived along the Illinois rivers, trading for furs.
ss|5|l10-4|3-5|The Twenty-First State — Illinois became a state in 1818, when it was mostly empty prairie with a few thousand settlers.
ss|5|l10-5|3-5|Canals Connect the Waters — Canals linked the Great Lakes to the East and to the Mississippi, and turned Chicago into a trading center.
ss|5|l10-6|3-5|Railroads Cross the Prairie — Railroads made Chicago the nation's biggest rail center and let towns and farms spread across the prairie.
ss|5|l10-7|3-5|From Fort to City — Chicago grew from a swampy trading post and a small fort into a big city in only a few decades.
ss|5|l10-8|3-5|The Great Fire and After — The fire of 1871 destroyed the heart of Chicago, and the rebuilt city invented the skyscraper.
ss|5|l10-9|3-5|The Great Migration — In the 1900s, hundreds of thousands of Black Americans moved from the South to Chicago for jobs and freedom.
ss|5|l10-10|3-5|Cities, Farms and Universities — Illinois today mixes a world city, deep farmland, big universities and people from everywhere.
ss|5|l10-11|3-5|Lincoln, Addams and Wells — Three Illinoisans, Abraham Lincoln, Jane Addams and Ida B. Wells, used words and action to make the country fairer.
ss|5|l10-12|3-5|Two Presidents from Illinois — Ronald Reagan and Barack Obama, two presidents with very different ideas, both started out in Illinois.
ss|6|l11-1|3-5|The First People Arrive — People have lived in North America for at least fifteen thousand years, and we know it from the tools and bones they left behind.
ss|6|l11-2|3-5|Many Lands, Many Ways — Native peoples lived in hundreds of nations, each with its own language and its own way of living that fit its land.
ss|6|l11-3|3-5|Longhouses of the Eastern Woodlands — In the eastern forests, people built longhouses from bark and grew corn, beans and squash together.
ss|6|l11-4|3-5|Bison and Tipis on the Plains — On the treeless Great Plains, people depended on the bison for food, shelter, clothing and tools.
ss|6|l11-5|3-5|Pueblos and Ditches of the Southwest — In the dry Southwest, people built towns of stone and mud brick and dug ditches to bring water to their corn.
ss|6|l11-6|3-5|Cedar and Salmon on the Northwest Coast — On the rainy Pacific Northwest coast, giant cedar trees and rivers full of salmon made rich villages possible.
ss|6|l11-7|3-5|Ice and Sea in the Arctic — In the Arctic, where nothing can be farmed, the Inuit lived by hunting seals and whales and inventing tools for ice and sea.
ss|6|l11-8|3-5|Cahokia, a City of Mounds — Around the year 1100, Cahokia in Illinois was the largest city north of Mexico, built by farmers who raised huge mounds of earth.
ss|6|l11-9|3-5|The Haudenosaunee Council — Five nations in the Northeast joined under the Great Law of Peace, with a council that decided together and clan mothers who chose the leaders.
ss|6|l11-10|3-5|Stories, Art and Trade Routes — Native peoples passed down knowledge through spoken stories and art, and traded goods across thousands of miles.
ss|6|l11-11|3-5|Native Nations Today — Native nations are still here: more than five hundred are recognized by the United States, and tens of thousands of Native people live in Illinois, many in Chicago.
ss|6|l12-1|3-5|Spices, Gold and God — Europeans in the 1400s wanted a sea route to Asia for trade, and they also wanted gold, land and converts to their religion.
ss|6|l12-2|3-5|What 1492 Set in Motion — Columbus landed in the Caribbean in 1492 believing he had reached Asia, and his voyage started centuries of contact that changed both worlds.
ss|6|l12-3|3-5|Corn Goes East, Wheat Goes West — Foods from the Americas fed the world, and animals and crops from Europe changed the Americas.
ss|6|l12-4|3-5|The Horse Changes the Plains — Horses brought by the Spanish spread north and turned Plains peoples into fast-moving hunters and riders within about two hundred years.
ss|6|l12-5|3-5|Sickness Crosses the Sea — Diseases from Europe killed more Native people than any war, because no one in the Americas had ever been exposed to them.
ss|6|l12-6|3-5|Spanish Soldiers and Missions — The Spanish conquered Native empires, forced people to work and built missions to change their religion, and in 1680 the Pueblo people drove them out.
ss|6|l12-7|3-5|French Traders on the Rivers — The French came in small numbers to trade furs, so they relied on Native alliances, learned Native languages and often married into Native families, including in Illinois.
ss|6|l12-8|3-5|English Farms and Fences — The English came as families to farm, so they took more land than the Spanish or French, and land became the main cause of war.
ss|6|l12-9|3-5|Land Lost, Lives Lost — Over three centuries, Native nations lost most of their land and many of their people through disease, war and treaties, including here in Illinois.
ss|6|l12-10|3-5|Resisting and Adapting — Native peoples fought back, made alliances, took up new tools and kept their languages and ceremonies alive, and they are still doing so.
ss|6|l12-11|3-5|Same Event, Different Stories — The same event looks different depending on who tells it, so good historians read more than one side and ask who wrote a source and why.
ss|7|l13-1|3-5|New England: Rocky Soil, Busy Harbors — New England's thin soil and good harbors pushed people toward fishing, shipbuilding and trade.
ss|7|l13-2|3-5|The Middle Colonies: Breadbasket — Rich soil and wide rivers made the Middle Colonies grow grain and welcome many kinds of people.
ss|7|l13-3|3-5|The Southern Colonies: Cash Crops — Warm weather and rich lowlands let the Southern Colonies grow crops to sell, using enslaved labor.
ss|7|l13-4|3-5|Farms, Towns, Ships and Shops — Most colonists farmed, but towns held the shops, mills and docks that connected farms to the world.
ss|7|l13-5|3-5|A Colonial Child's Day — Colonial children worked from a young age, and their jobs depended on where they lived and who they were.
ss|7|l13-6|3-5|Church and Schoolhouse — Religion was central to colonial life, and in New England it led to the first public schools.
ss|7|l13-7|3-5|Indentured Servants — Indentured servants traded years of unpaid work for the trip to America, and then went free.
ss|7|l13-8|3-5|The Middle Passage — Millions of Africans were forced across the Atlantic in chains, and hundreds of thousands were brought to the colonies.
ss|7|l13-9|3-5|Town Meetings and Assemblies — Colonists began governing themselves through town meetings and elected assemblies.
ss|7|l13-10|3-5|A Faraway King — Britain ruled the colonies but mostly left them alone, so colonists got used to running things themselves.
ss|7|l14-1|3-5|Taxes Without a Vote — After 1763, Britain began taxing the colonies directly, and colonists protested that they had no vote in Parliament.
ss|7|l14-2|3-5|Blood on King Street — British soldiers in Boston fired on a crowd in 1770, and both sides used the event to argue their case.
ss|7|l14-3|3-5|Tea in the Harbor — The Boston Tea Party and Britain's harsh punishment pushed the colonies together toward war.
ss|7|l14-4|3-5|The Big Idea — The Declaration of Independence said that all people have rights and that governments get their power from the people.
ss|7|l14-5|3-5|Soldiers and Snow — The war was won by ordinary people who suffered through hunger, cold and defeat under George Washington's leadership.
ss|7|l14-6|3-5|Help From Across the Sea — France joined the war after the American victory at Saratoga, and French help made the final victory at Yorktown possible.
ss|7|l14-7|3-5|A Loose Team of Thirteen — The Articles of Confederation made a central government too weak to tax, keep order or settle disputes.
ss|7|l14-8|3-5|Three Branches, Checked and Balanced — The Constitution split power into three branches, each able to check the others, so no one could rule alone.
ss|7|l14-9|3-5|The Bill of Rights — Many Americans would not accept the Constitution without a list of rights, so ten amendments were added in 1791.
ss|7|l14-10|3-5|Who Was Left Out — The Constitution protected slavery and did not give rights to women, Native people or most Black Americans.
ss|7|l14-11|3-5|Stretching the Promise — Over two centuries, amendments and movements extended the Constitution's rights to people first left out.
ss|7|l15-1|3-5|The First President — George Washington set the example for how a president should act, including giving up power after two terms.
ss|7|l15-2|3-5|Doubling the Country — In 1803, the United States bought the Louisiana Territory from France and doubled its size.
ss|7|l15-3|3-5|Lewis and Clark Go West — Lewis and Clark's team explored the new territory with Native help and brought back maps, plants and stories.
ss|7|l15-4|3-5|A Rulebook for New States — The Northwest Ordinance of 1787 set the steps for territories to become equal states and banned slavery north of the Ohio River.
ss|7|l15-5|3-5|Illinois Joins the Union — Illinois became the twenty-first state in 1818, and settlers poured in along its rivers and prairies.
ss|7|l15-6|3-5|Steam on the Rivers — Steamboats let people and goods travel upstream, turning rivers into two-way highways.
ss|7|l15-7|3-5|Canals and Iron Rails — Canals and then railroads linked farms to distant cities and turned Chicago into a hub.
ss|7|l15-8|3-5|The Trail of Tears — The Indian Removal Act forced Native nations off their homelands, and thousands of Cherokee died on the march west.
ss|7|l15-9|3-5|Machines Change Work — New machines like the cotton gin, the reaper and the steel plow made work faster and changed who did it.
ss|7|l15-10|3-5|A Bigger Country, a Bigger Argument — Every new state raised the question of slavery, and the Missouri Compromise only delayed the fight.
ss|8|l16-1|3-5|Rules for Everyone — A government makes the rules for a whole community and makes sure people follow them.
ss|8|l16-2|3-5|Three Levels, One Country — In the United States, three levels of government share the work: local, state and national.
ss|8|l16-3|3-5|Who Does What — Each level of government has its own jobs, and some jobs are shared.
ss|8|l16-4|3-5|Make, Carry Out, Judge — Every level of government splits its power into three branches: one makes laws, one carries them out, and one judges.
ss|8|l16-5|3-5|Mayor and Council — A city is run by an elected council that passes local laws and a mayor who carries them out.
ss|8|l16-6|3-5|Governor and General Assembly — Illinois is run by the General Assembly, which makes state laws, the governor, who carries them out, and state courts.
ss|8|l16-7|3-5|President, Congress and the Courts — The national government has Congress to make laws, the president to carry them out, and the Supreme Court to judge.
ss|8|l16-8|3-5|From an Idea to a Law — A law starts as a bill, gets voted on by the legislature, and then the executive signs it or vetoes it.
ss|8|l16-9|3-5|Elections and Voting — In an election, citizens vote to choose the people who will run their government.
ss|8|l16-10|3-5|The People Decide — In a democracy, the people hold the power, either by voting directly or by choosing representatives.
ss|8|l16-11|3-5|A Government in Your School — A student council is a small government, with the same parts and the same problems as a real one.
ss|8|l17-1|3-5|The Bill of Rights — The Bill of Rights is the first ten amendments to the Constitution, and it lists things the government cannot take from you.
ss|8|l17-2|3-5|Speaking and Believing — The First Amendment protects your freedom to speak, write, worship, gather and ask the government for change.
ss|8|l17-3|3-5|A Fair Trial — If you are accused of a crime, the Bill of Rights promises a public trial, a jury, and the chance to defend yourself.
ss|8|l17-4|3-5|Laws and Taxes — Citizens have responsibilities as well as rights, starting with obeying laws and paying taxes.
ss|8|l17-5|3-5|Serving on a Jury and Voting — Two big responsibilities, jury duty and voting, only work when ordinary people show up.
ss|8|l17-6|3-5|Speaking Up — Citizens have a responsibility to speak up when something is unfair or unsafe, in ways that are respectful and clear.
ss|8|l17-7|3-5|The Illinois Constitution — Illinois has its own constitution, written in Springfield, that sets up the state government and lists its own bill of rights.
ss|8|l17-8|3-5|Symbols of Illinois — Illinois uses symbols like the cardinal, the violet and the white oak to show what the state is proud of.
ss|8|l17-9|3-5|Petitions, Letters, Meetings — To change something, find out who decides, gather evidence, and then ask in writing and in person.
ss|8|l17-10|3-5|Volunteering — Volunteering means giving your time to help your community, and it is one of the strongest ways to make a change.
ss|8|l17-11|3-5|Jane Addams and Hull House — Jane Addams used her rights to speak up for poor families in Chicago and helped win laws that protected children.
ss|8|l17-12|3-5|Ida B. Wells Tells the Truth — Ida B. Wells used freedom of the press to expose violence against Black Americans and helped win the vote for women in Illinois.
ss|9|l18-1|3-5|Producers and Consumers — Producers make goods or do services, and consumers buy and use them; most people are both.
ss|9|l18-2|3-5|Goods and Services — Goods are things you can hold; services are work someone does for you.
ss|9|l18-3|3-5|Scarcity and the Choice You Give Up — Nothing is unlimited, so every choice means giving up the next best thing, called the opportunity cost.
ss|9|l18-4|3-5|Demand at the School Store — Demand is how much people want to buy at a price; when the price drops, more people buy.
ss|9|l18-5|3-5|Supply and Running Out — Supply is how much sellers offer; when supply is low and demand is high, the price tends to rise.
ss|9|l18-6|3-5|What a Price Tells You — A price is a signal that tells buyers how to spend and sellers what to make.
ss|9|l18-7|3-5|Earn, Save, Spend, Give — Money you earn can be spent, saved for later or given away, and a budget helps you plan all three.
ss|9|l18-8|3-5|What a Bank Does — A bank keeps money safe, pays savers interest and lends money to people who need it now.
ss|9|l18-9|3-5|Taxes and Public Goods — Taxes are money people pay to the government so it can provide public goods that everyone shares.
ss|9|l18-10|3-5|Corn Here, Oranges There — Specialization means doing what you do best, and regions specialize because of their land and climate.
ss|9|l18-11|3-5|Why Trade Makes Both Sides Better — When people and places specialize and then trade, each ends up with more than it could make alone.
ss|9|l18-12|3-5|Starting Something New — An entrepreneur takes a risk to start a business that solves a problem people will pay for.
ss|9|l19-1|3-5|Lines Around the Earth — Latitude lines measure how far north or south a place is, and longitude lines measure east or west.
ss|9|l19-2|3-5|How Big Is It Really? — Map scale tells you how much real distance one inch on the map stands for.
ss|9|l19-3|3-5|Physical Maps and Political Maps — A physical map shows land and water; a political map shows borders, states and cities that people made.
ss|9|l19-4|3-5|The Northeast — The Northeast is a small, crowded region of old cities, rocky coasts and the Appalachian Mountains.
ss|9|l19-5|3-5|The Southeast — The Southeast is warm and wet, with long coasts, wide rivers and farms that grow crops the North cannot.
ss|9|l19-6|3-5|The Southwest and the West — The Southwest is mostly hot and dry; the West has the tallest mountains, the Pacific coast and the most varied land.
ss|9|l19-7|3-5|The Heart of the Country — The Midwest is flat, fertile land between the Great Lakes and the Great Plains, crossed by big rivers.
ss|9|l19-8|3-5|Illinois Up Close — Illinois is a long, mostly flat state framed by rivers and a Great Lake, with a giant city in one corner.
ss|9|l19-9|3-5|Jobs and Food From the Land — The natural resources of a region decide much of what people there grow, make and do for work.
ss|9|l19-10|3-5|Moving People and Goods — Rivers, rails, highways and airports connect the regions and make Illinois a crossroads of the country.
ss|9|l19-11|3-5|When Nature Strikes — Each region faces its own natural hazards, and people prepare for the ones most likely where they live.
ush|1|l1-1|6-8|Arriving in the Americas — People reached the Americas at least 15,000 years ago and spread across two continents, adapting to every kind of land.
ush|1|l1-2|6-8|The Gift of Corn — When people in the Americas learned to farm, they could stay in one place, grow extra food and build larger communities.
ush|1|l1-3|6-8|The Olmec and the Maya — The Olmec and Maya built early civilizations in Mexico and Central America, with great cities, writing and a detailed calendar.
ush|1|l1-4|6-8|The Aztec Empire — The Aztec built a powerful empire from their island city of Tenochtitlan, growing rich from farming, trade and tribute.
ush|1|l1-5|6-8|The Inca Empire — The Inca ruled a long mountain empire in South America, held together by roads, runners, storehouses and a labor tax.
ush|1|l1-6|6-8|Builders of the Desert Southwest — The Ancestral Puebloans farmed a dry land and built stone and adobe towns, and their descendants still live in the Southwest.
ush|1|l1-7|6-8|The Mound Builders — For centuries, Native peoples of the Mississippi and Ohio valleys built earthen mounds, and the Mississippians created towns linked by long trade routes.
ush|1|l1-8|6-8|Nations of the Eastern Woodlands — Eastern Woodlands peoples farmed, hunted and fished in the forests, and the Haudenosaunee joined five nations under a Great Law of Peace.
ush|1|l1-9|6-8|Plains and Northwest Coast — Native peoples shaped their lives around their environments, from hunting bison on the Plains to harvesting salmon on the Northwest Coast.
ush|1|l1-10|6-8|Europe Changes — After the Middle Ages, trade, disease and new ideas shook up Europe and made Europeans eager to learn and explore.
ush|1|l1-11|6-8|New Tools and a Divided Church — Better ships and instruments made long ocean voyages possible, while the Reformation split Europe's Christians and sharpened rivalries between nations.
ush|1|l1-12|6-8|Kingdoms of Gold and Salt — West African kingdoms grew rich by controlling the trade of gold and salt across the Sahara, and cities like Timbuktu became centers of learning.
ush|1|l2-1|6-8|Portugal Finds a Way — Portugal led Europe onto the ocean, sailing around Africa to reach the riches of Asia by sea.
ush|1|l2-2|6-8|Columbus and the Taino — Columbus reached the Caribbean in 1492 seeking Asia, and his arrival brought disaster to the Taino people who lived there.
ush|1|l2-3|6-8|Dividing and Circling the Globe — Spain and Portugal agreed to split the non-European world between them, and Magellan's voyage proved how vast the Earth really was.
ush|1|l2-4|6-8|The Fall of Tenochtitlan — With Native allies, horses, steel weapons and the help of a smallpox epidemic, a small Spanish force conquered the Aztec empire by 1521.
ush|1|l2-5|6-8|The Conquest of the Inca — Francisco Pizarro took advantage of disease and a civil war to capture the Inca emperor and conquer much of the Inca empire.
ush|1|l2-6|6-8|Crops, Animals and Germs — The Columbian Exchange moved plants, animals and diseases between the hemispheres, changing diets everywhere and killing millions of Native people.
ush|1|l2-7|6-8|Life in New Spain — Spain ruled its American empire with a strict class system, forced Native labor and missions meant to make Native people Catholic.
ush|1|l2-8|6-8|The Search for a Shortcut — England, France and the Netherlands sent explorers to find a Northwest Passage to Asia, and though none found it, they claimed large parts of North America.
ush|1|l2-9|6-8|Furs, Forts and Rivers — France and the Netherlands built colonies in North America around the fur trade, working closely with Native nations.
ush|1|l2-10|6-8|England Takes On Spain — England's defeat of the Spanish Armada in 1588 weakened Spain's power at sea and opened the way for English colonies.
ush|1|l2-11|6-8|The Atlantic Slave Trade Begins — To work plantations and mines in the Americas, Europeans forced millions of Africans across the Atlantic in a slave trade that lasted more than 350 years.
ush|1|l2-12|6-8|The Middle Passage — The Middle Passage was the brutal ocean crossing that carried enslaved Africans to the Americas, and many resisted even in the worst conditions.
ush|10|l28-1|6-8|Footprints on the Moon — In July 1969, Apollo 11 astronauts became the first people to walk on the Moon, a high point of the space race.
ush|10|l28-2|6-8|Talking With Rivals — Nixon eased Cold War tensions through détente, a surprise visit to China and arms talks, and he ended the military draft.
ush|10|l28-3|6-8|Protecting the Environment — In the early 1970s, public concern about pollution led to Earth Day, the EPA and major laws to clean the air and water.
ush|10|l28-4|6-8|The Watergate Scandal — Watergate began as a break-in and grew into a cover-up that led Richard Nixon to resign in 1974, the only president ever to do so.
ush|10|l28-5|6-8|Ford and Hard Times — Gerald Ford took over a shaken nation facing an energy crisis, rising prices and slow growth.
ush|10|l28-6|6-8|Carter: Peace and a Hostage Crisis — Jimmy Carter helped make peace between Egypt and Israel but struggled with inflation and the Iran hostage crisis.
ush|10|l28-7|6-8|A New Direction in 1980 — Ronald Reagan won the 1980 election by promising smaller government, lower taxes and a stronger military.
ush|10|l28-8|6-8|Tax Cuts and Debates — Reagan cut taxes and business rules and built up the military, and Americans still debate the results.
ush|10|l28-9|6-8|A Changing Population — New immigration, movement to the Sun Belt and new leaders changed who Americans were and where they lived.
ush|10|l28-10|6-8|Reagan and Gorbachev — After a tense arms buildup, Reagan and Soviet leader Mikhail Gorbachev built trust and signed a treaty to destroy some nuclear weapons.
ush|10|l28-11|6-8|Walls Come Down — In 1989, Communist governments in Eastern Europe fell, and in 1991 the Soviet Union itself broke apart, ending the Cold War.
ush|10|l28-12|6-8|New Rights and a New War — In 1990 the Americans with Disabilities Act protected millions of people, and in 1991 the U.S. led a coalition to push Iraq out of Kuwait.
ush|10|l29-1|6-8|After the Cold War — With the Cold War over, Americans turned their attention to problems at home and elected Bill Clinton in 1992.
ush|10|l29-2|6-8|Trade and a Budget Surplus — In the 1990s, the U.S. signed NAFTA, the economy boomed and the federal government balanced its budget for the first time in decades.
ush|10|l29-3|6-8|The Digital Revolution — Personal computers and the internet changed how Americans work, learn, shop and talk to each other.
ush|10|l29-4|6-8|A Close Election in 2000 — The 2000 presidential election was so close in Florida that the Supreme Court's decision in Bush v. Gore settled the outcome.
ush|10|l29-5|6-8|September 11, 2001 — On September 11, 2001, terrorists attacked the United States, killing nearly 3,000 people and changing the nation's sense of safety.
ush|10|l29-6|6-8|Security and Two Wars — After 9/11, the U.S. created new security agencies and fought long wars in Afghanistan and Iraq.
ush|10|l29-7|6-8|Hurricane Katrina — In 2005, Hurricane Katrina flooded New Orleans, and the slow response raised hard questions about government and fairness.
ush|10|l29-8|6-8|The Great Recession — In 2008, a housing crash and a banking crisis caused the worst economic downturn since the Great Depression.
ush|10|l29-9|6-8|A Historic Election — In 2008, Barack Obama of Illinois became the first African American elected president, and his health care law sparked a long debate.
ush|10|l29-10|6-8|A Connected World — Smartphones, social media and global trade have tied Americans closely to one another and to the rest of the world.
ush|10|l29-11|6-8|Recent Elections and a Pandemic — Close, hard-fought elections and the COVID-19 pandemic marked the years after 2016.
ush|10|l29-12|6-8|A Diverse Nation Takes Part — Immigration keeps changing the country, and citizens shape its future by voting, serving and speaking up.
ush|2|l3-1|6-8|Hard Times at Jamestown — England's first lasting colony, Jamestown, nearly failed because of disease, hunger and conflict, and it depended on trade with the Powhatan people.
ush|2|l3-2|6-8|Tobacco Takes Over — Tobacco made Virginia profitable, which brought more settlers and servants, the first elected assembly, and more conflict over Powhatan land.
ush|2|l3-3|6-8|Slavery and Rebellion in Virginia — Africans were brought to Virginia in 1619, and after Bacon's Rebellion in 1676, wealthy planters turned more and more to slavery for labor.
ush|2|l3-4|6-8|The Pilgrims and the Wampanoag — The Pilgrims came to worship in their own way, wrote the Mayflower Compact to govern themselves, and survived with help from the Wampanoag.
ush|2|l3-5|6-8|A City upon a Hill — Thousands of Puritans settled Massachusetts Bay to build a model Christian community with strong churches, town governments and schools.
ush|2|l3-6|6-8|Dissenters Move On — People who challenged Puritan leaders, such as Roger Williams and Anne Hutchinson, were forced out and helped start new colonies like Rhode Island and Connecticut.
ush|2|l3-7|6-8|War Comes to New England — As colonists took more land, New England Native nations fought back in the Pequot War and King Philip's War, and both wars ended in terrible losses for Native peoples.
ush|2|l3-8|6-8|New Netherland Becomes New York — The Dutch colony of New Netherland was a busy trading center with many peoples, and in 1664 England took it over and renamed it New York.
ush|2|l3-9|6-8|Penn's Holy Experiment — William Penn founded Pennsylvania as a Quaker colony that offered religious freedom, an elected assembly and fair dealing with the Lenape.
ush|2|l3-10|6-8|Maryland and the Carolinas — Maryland began as a safe place for Catholics, while the Carolinas grew rich from trade and plantations that depended on enslaved labor.
ush|2|l3-11|6-8|Georgia, the Last Colony — Georgia, founded in 1732, was meant to protect South Carolina from Spanish Florida and to give poor English people a fresh start.
ush|2|l4-1|6-8|New England: Sea and Town Meeting — With thin, rocky soil, New Englanders made a living from small farms, the sea and trade, and they ran their towns through town meetings.
ush|2|l4-2|6-8|The Middle Colonies: Grain and Many Peoples — The Middle Colonies grew so much grain that they were called the breadbasket colonies, and they had the most varied mix of peoples in English America.
ush|2|l4-3|6-8|The Southern Colonies: Tobacco, Rice and Indigo — The warm South grew cash crops like tobacco, rice and indigo on plantations that depended more and more on the labor of enslaved Africans.
ush|2|l4-4|6-8|Trade Rules from London — Under mercantilism, England passed Navigation Acts so colonial trade would enrich England, and Atlantic trade routes were deeply tied to slavery.
ush|2|l4-5|6-8|The Middle Passage and Enslaved Lives — Enslaved Africans survived the Middle Passage and harsh slave codes, and they built families, faith and culture that helped them endure.
ush|2|l4-6|6-8|Resistance and the Stono Rebellion — Enslaved people resisted slavery in many ways, and the Stono Rebellion of 1739 led South Carolina to pass even harsher laws.
ush|2|l4-7|6-8|Reading, Reason and Ben Franklin — Many colonists valued reading and learning, and Enlightenment ideas about reason and natural rights spread through books, newspapers and people like Benjamin Franklin.
ush|2|l4-8|6-8|The Great Awakening — The Great Awakening was a wave of religious excitement in the 1730s and 1740s that drew huge crowds, split churches and encouraged people to question authority.
ush|2|l4-9|6-8|The Rights of English Subjects — Colonists believed they had the rights of English subjects, rooted in the Magna Carta and the English Bill of Rights, and they used their assemblies and courts to defend them.
ush|2|l4-10|6-8|Rivals in the Ohio Valley — France and Britain competed for the Ohio River valley, a region Native nations called home, and that rivalry started the French and Indian War in 1754.
ush|2|l4-11|6-8|Britain Wins, New Troubles Begin — Britain won the French and Indian War and took France's lands east of the Mississippi, but victory left Britain deep in debt and angered Native nations.
ush|3|l5-1|6-8|A Line on the Map — After winning the French and Indian War, Britain tried to stop colonists from settling west of the Appalachian Mountains.
ush|3|l5-2|6-8|Parliament Wants Money — To pay its war debts, Britain's Parliament began taxing the colonies directly, starting with the Sugar Act and the Stamp Act.
ush|3|l5-3|6-8|No Taxation Without Representation — Colonists argued that only their own elected assemblies could tax them, and their protests forced Parliament to repeal the Stamp Act.
ush|3|l5-4|6-8|New Taxes, New Boycotts — When Parliament taxed everyday imports in 1767, colonists, including many women, answered with boycotts and homemade goods.
ush|3|l5-5|6-8|Blood on King Street — In 1770, British soldiers fired into a crowd in Boston, killing five people, and Patriots used the event to turn opinion against Britain.
ush|3|l5-6|6-8|Tea in the Harbor — Committees linked the colonies, and when Britain passed the Tea Act, Boston Patriots dumped a shipload of taxed tea into the harbor.
ush|3|l5-7|6-8|Britain Cracks Down — Parliament punished Massachusetts with harsh laws in 1774, and the colonies responded by meeting together in the First Continental Congress.
ush|3|l5-8|6-8|The Shot Heard Round the World — On April 19, 1775, fighting at Lexington and Concord turned the long argument with Britain into a war.
ush|3|l5-9|6-8|One Last Try for Peace — The Second Continental Congress prepared for war while still offering the king a chance at peace, which he rejected.
ush|3|l5-10|6-8|A Pamphlet Changes Minds — Thomas Paine's pamphlet Common Sense convinced many colonists in 1776 that independence was the right choice.
ush|3|l5-11|6-8|Jefferson Writes the Declaration — In July 1776, Congress approved the Declaration of Independence, which explained why the colonies were breaking from Britain.
ush|3|l5-12|6-8|Who Was Left Out? — The Declaration said all men are created equal, but in 1776 those words did not apply to enslaved people, women or Native Americans.
ush|3|l6-1|6-8|Bunker Hill and Boston — The Battle of Bunker Hill showed that colonial soldiers could stand up to the British army, and in 1776 the British left Boston.
ush|3|l6-2|6-8|Patriots, Loyalists and Native Nations — Americans were deeply divided about the war, and Native nations made hard choices to protect their own lands.
ush|3|l6-3|6-8|Retreat, Then Surprise — After losing New York in 1776, Washington's army won surprise victories at Trenton and Princeton that kept the Revolution alive.
ush|3|l6-4|6-8|Victory at Saratoga — The American victory at Saratoga in 1777 was a turning point because it convinced France to join the war.
ush|3|l6-5|6-8|Help From Abroad — France, Spain and volunteers from Europe gave the Americans soldiers, ships, money and skills they badly needed.
ush|3|l6-6|6-8|War on the Sea and in Illinois — Americans fought at sea against the powerful British navy and on the western frontier, where George Rogers Clark captured British posts in the Illinois country.
ush|3|l6-7|6-8|Women in the War — Women kept farms and businesses running, supported the army and sometimes fought, and some began asking for rights of their own.
ush|3|l6-8|6-8|African Americans and the Fight for Freedom — Thousands of African Americans fought in the Revolution, some for the Americans and some for the British, most of them hoping to win their own freedom.
ush|3|l6-9|6-8|The War Moves South — Britain shifted the war to the South, where early British victories gave way to hard-fought American comebacks.
ush|3|l6-10|6-8|Yorktown and the Peace — With French help, Americans trapped a British army at Yorktown in 1781, and the Treaty of Paris of 1783 recognized American independence.
ush|3|l6-11|6-8|What the Revolution Changed — The Americans won through determination, leadership and foreign help, and the Revolution's ideas began to change society, though slowly and unevenly.
ush|3|l7-1|6-8|Writing New Rules — After declaring independence, each state wrote a constitution that limited government power and protected certain rights.
ush|3|l7-2|6-8|The Articles of Confederation — The Articles of Confederation created a weak national government because Americans feared a strong central power.
ush|3|l7-3|6-8|Dividing the Northwest — Two land laws organized the Northwest Territory, including future Illinois, created a path to statehood and banned slavery there.
ush|3|l7-4|6-8|A Government Without Money — Without the power to tax or control trade, the Confederation government could not pay its debts or earn respect from other nations.
ush|3|l7-5|6-8|Farmers in Revolt — Shays's Rebellion, an armed protest by Massachusetts farmers, convinced many leaders that the national government needed to be stronger.
ush|3|l7-6|6-8|Two Plans for a New Government — At the Constitutional Convention, large states backed the Virginia Plan and small states backed the New Jersey Plan.
ush|3|l7-7|6-8|The Great Compromise — The Great Compromise created a Congress with two houses: one based on population and one with equal votes for each state.
ush|3|l7-8|6-8|Bargains Over Slavery — To keep southern states in the union, delegates made compromises that protected slavery in the new Constitution.
ush|3|l7-9|6-8|Choosing a President — Delegates created the office of president and the Electoral College as a compromise over how the nation's leader would be chosen.
ush|3|l7-10|6-8|Dividing and Checking Power — The Constitution divides power among three branches and between the national and state governments so that no one part becomes too strong.
ush|3|l7-11|6-8|The Fight Over Ratification — Federalists and Antifederalists debated the Constitution in every state before it was ratified in 1788.
ush|3|l7-12|6-8|Adding a Bill of Rights — The first ten amendments, the Bill of Rights, were added in 1791 to protect individual freedoms from the national government.
ush|4|l8-1|6-8|The First President Takes Office — As the first president, George Washington knew that almost everything he did would become a model for later leaders.
ush|4|l8-2|6-8|Cabinet, Courts and Rights — Congress created departments to help the president and a system of federal courts to settle legal questions.
ush|4|l8-3|6-8|Counting the People — The first census in 1790 counted about 3.9 million people, including about 700,000 enslaved people.
ush|4|l8-4|6-8|Paying Off the War Debt — Hamilton wanted the national government to pay all the Revolutionary War debts so the new nation would be trusted with money.
ush|4|l8-5|6-8|A Bank for the Nation — The fight over a national bank raised a lasting question: how much power does the Constitution give the national government?
ush|4|l8-6|6-8|The Whiskey Rebellion — When farmers rose up against a tax on whiskey, Washington sent a large army to show that the new government could enforce its laws.
ush|4|l8-7|6-8|Two Parties Form — Disagreements between Hamilton and Jefferson led to the nation's first two political parties.
ush|4|l8-8|6-8|Staying Out of Europe's Wars — Washington kept the young nation neutral in a war between Britain and France, and treaties with Britain and Spain eased tensions.
ush|4|l8-9|6-8|War in the Northwest Territory — After years of fighting, Native nations north of the Ohio River were forced to give up most of what is now Ohio in the Treaty of Greenville.
ush|4|l8-10|6-8|Washington Says Goodbye — In his Farewell Address, Washington warned Americans about political parties, regional divisions and permanent alliances.
ush|4|l8-11|6-8|President Adams and the XYZ Affair — John Adams faced an undeclared war at sea with France but chose peace, even though it hurt him politically.
ush|4|l8-12|6-8|The Alien and Sedition Acts — Laws passed in 1798 limited criticism of the government, and Jefferson and Madison argued that states could challenge them.
ush|4|l9-1|6-8|The Election of 1800 — In 1800 power passed peacefully from one party to another, proving the Constitution could survive a bitter election.
ush|4|l9-2|6-8|Jefferson's Smaller Government — Jefferson cut the size and cost of the national government because he believed a free nation should rest on independent farmers.
ush|4|l9-3|6-8|Marbury v. Madison — In Marbury v. Madison (1803), the Supreme Court claimed the power to strike down laws that break the Constitution.
ush|4|l9-4|6-8|The Louisiana Purchase — In 1803 the United States bought the huge Louisiana Territory from France, doubling the size of the nation.
ush|4|l9-5|6-8|Lewis and Clark's Journey — The Corps of Discovery mapped the West and succeeded because Native nations helped them along the way.
ush|4|l9-6|6-8|Pike Explores the Southwest — Zebulon Pike explored the southern part of the Louisiana Territory and brought back news of Spanish lands in the Southwest.
ush|4|l9-7|6-8|The Barbary Pirates — Jefferson sent the navy to North Africa rather than keep paying for the safety of American ships.
ush|4|l9-8|6-8|Impressment and the Embargo — Jefferson tried to punish Britain and France by stopping trade, but the Embargo Act hurt Americans most of all.
ush|4|l9-9|6-8|Tecumseh Unites the Nations — The Shawnee leader Tecumseh tried to unite many Native nations to stop the loss of their lands.
ush|4|l9-10|6-8|The War Hawks Get Their War — Angry about impressment and British support for Native resistance, Congress declared war on Britain in 1812.
ush|4|l9-11|6-8|Washington Burns, Baltimore Holds — British troops burned the nation's capital in 1814, but the defense of Baltimore inspired the national anthem.
ush|4|l9-12|6-8|New Orleans and the Peace — The War of 1812 ended in a draw, but it left Americans feeling proud and left Native nations without a strong ally.
ush|4|l10-1|6-8|Machines Come to America — The Industrial Revolution reached the United States when Samuel Slater brought British factory ideas to New England.
ush|4|l10-2|6-8|The Lowell Mill Girls — Young women from New England farms went to work in the textile mills of Lowell, Massachusetts, earning wages but facing long, hard days.
ush|4|l10-3|6-8|The National Road and the Steamboat — New roads and steamboats made travel faster and cheaper, tying the West more closely to the East.
ush|4|l10-4|6-8|The Erie Canal — The Erie Canal linked the Great Lakes to New York City, cutting shipping costs and making New York the nation's greatest port.
ush|4|l10-5|6-8|The First Railroads — Railroads began in the 1830s and spread quickly, because trains could go almost anywhere and run all year.
ush|4|l10-6|6-8|The Cotton Gin — The cotton gin made growing cotton very profitable, which spread slavery across the Deep South.
ush|4|l10-7|6-8|The Slave Trade Inside the Nation — After bringing enslaved people from Africa was banned in 1808, a huge trade in enslaved people grew inside the United States.
ush|4|l10-8|6-8|Surviving and Resisting Slavery — Enslaved people kept their families, faith and culture alive and resisted slavery in both quiet and open ways.
ush|4|l10-9|6-8|An Era of Good Feelings — After the War of 1812, national pride grew, and leaders worked to build a stronger, more connected economy.
ush|4|l10-10|6-8|Illinois Becomes a State — Illinois became the 21st state in 1818 as a free state, though slavery and forced servitude still existed there.
ush|4|l10-11|6-8|The Missouri Compromise — The Missouri Compromise of 1820 kept a balance between free and slave states, but it showed how slavery was dividing the nation.
ush|4|l10-12|6-8|The Monroe Doctrine — In 1823 President Monroe warned European nations not to set up new colonies or interfere in the Americas.
ush|5|l11-1|6-8|The Election of 1824 — In 1824 no candidate won a majority of electoral votes, so the House chose John Quincy Adams, and Andrew Jackson's supporters cried foul.
ush|5|l11-2|6-8|Jackson Wins in 1828 — Jackson won in 1828 as a hero of ordinary white men, as more states let white men vote without owning property.
ush|5|l11-3|6-8|Jobs for Supporters — Jackson replaced many government workers with his own supporters, a practice critics called the spoils system.
ush|5|l11-4|6-8|The Nullification Crisis — South Carolina tried to cancel a federal tariff, but Jackson insisted that no state could break away from federal law.
ush|5|l11-5|6-8|The War on the Bank — Jackson saw the national bank as a tool of the rich and destroyed it, which helped his popularity but shook the economy.
ush|5|l11-6|6-8|The Indian Removal Act — The Indian Removal Act of 1830 let the president push Native nations to trade their eastern homelands for land west of the Mississippi.
ush|5|l11-7|6-8|The Cherokee Go to Court — The Cherokee won their case in the Supreme Court, but President Jackson did not enforce the ruling.
ush|5|l11-8|6-8|The Trail of Tears — In 1838 the U.S. Army forced the Cherokee west, and thousands died on the journey called the Trail of Tears.
ush|5|l11-9|6-8|Seminole and Sauk Resistance — The Seminole in Florida and Black Hawk's Sauk in Illinois fought back against removal, at great cost.
ush|5|l11-10|6-8|The Panic of 1837 — Soon after Martin Van Buren became president, a financial panic started a long depression that hurt millions of Americans.
ush|5|l11-11|6-8|The Whigs Take Over — Opponents of Jackson formed the Whig Party and won the White House in 1840 with a lively, image-driven campaign.
ush|5|l12-1|6-8|A Belief in Expansion — Many Americans in the 1840s believed the United States was meant to spread across the continent, an idea called Manifest Destiny.
ush|5|l12-2|6-8|Trappers and Traders — Fur trappers and traders opened paths into the West that settlers would later follow.
ush|5|l12-3|6-8|The Oregon Trail — Hundreds of thousands of people crossed about 2,000 miles on overland trails, facing hard work, disease and danger.
ush|5|l12-4|6-8|The Mormon Journey — Members of the Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints left Illinois to escape violence and built a new home near the Great Salt Lake.
ush|5|l12-5|6-8|Texas Under Mexico — Mexico invited American settlers into Texas, but clashes over laws, slavery and power led to revolt.
ush|5|l12-6|6-8|The Texas Revolution — Texans declared independence in 1836, lost at the Alamo, and won at San Jacinto, creating the Republic of Texas.
ush|5|l12-7|6-8|Polk and the Oregon Deal — President James K. Polk pushed expansion, settling Oregon with Britain by compromise and preparing for conflict with Mexico.
ush|5|l12-8|6-8|War With Mexico — The U.S.-Mexican War (1846–1848) ended with Mexico giving up about half its land to the United States.
ush|5|l12-9|6-8|The Rush for Gold — The discovery of gold in 1848 drew people from around the world and made California a state by 1850.
ush|5|l12-10|6-8|Who Paid the Price — Native Californians, Californios, and Chinese and Latin American miners lost land, rights and lives as American California grew.
ush|5|l13-1|6-8|Newcomers From Ireland and Germany — Millions of immigrants, mostly from Ireland and Germany, arrived between 1830 and 1860 and changed American cities and farms.
ush|5|l13-2|6-8|Fear of Newcomers — Some native-born Americans turned against immigrants, especially Catholics, and formed the Know-Nothing Party.
ush|5|l13-3|6-8|Cities on the Rise — Cities grew very fast before the Civil War, bringing jobs and opportunity but also crowding, disease and fire.
ush|5|l13-4|6-8|Revival and the Push for Change — A wave of religious revivals inspired many Americans to try to improve society, including the fight against alcohol abuse.
ush|5|l13-5|6-8|Schools, Prisons and Hospitals — Reformers such as Horace Mann and Dorothea Dix worked for free public schools and better care for prisoners and people with mental illness.
ush|5|l13-6|6-8|Writers and Artists — American writers and painters created a new national culture that celebrated nature, independence and conscience.
ush|5|l13-7|6-8|Calls for Abolition — Black and white abolitionists demanded an immediate end to slavery, even when it put their lives in danger.
ush|5|l13-8|6-8|Voices of Freedom — Frederick Douglass, Sojourner Truth and the Grimké sisters used their own experiences to show Americans the truth about slavery.
ush|5|l13-9|6-8|The Underground Railroad — Freedom seekers and those who helped them built a secret network of routes to freedom, including routes through Illinois.
ush|5|l13-10|6-8|Roots of a Movement — Women's work in reform, especially abolition, led many to see their own lack of rights and to organize.
ush|5|l13-11|6-8|Seneca Falls, 1848 — At Seneca Falls in 1848, reformers declared that women and men are equal and called for women's right to vote.
ush|6|l14-1|6-8|A Question from the Mexican War — Winning new land from Mexico forced Americans to decide whether slavery could spread into the West.
ush|6|l14-2|6-8|The Compromise of 1850 — The Compromise of 1850 gave each side something it wanted, but it only delayed the conflict over slavery.
ush|6|l14-3|6-8|A Law That Angered the North — The Fugitive Slave Act of 1850 forced Northerners to take part in slavery, and it turned many of them against it.
ush|6|l14-4|6-8|Douglas Opens the Plains — The Kansas-Nebraska Act of 1854 let settlers vote on slavery and wiped out an old line that had kept slavery out of the North.
ush|6|l14-5|6-8|Bleeding Kansas — When settlers on both sides rushed into Kansas, the vote on slavery turned into fraud and violence.
ush|6|l14-6|6-8|A New Party Rises — Anger over the Kansas-Nebraska Act created the Republican Party, which opposed the spread of slavery.
ush|6|l14-7|6-8|The Dred Scott Decision — In 1857, the Supreme Court ruled that Black Americans were not citizens and that Congress could not ban slavery in the territories.
ush|6|l14-8|6-8|Lincoln Meets Douglas — In 1858, the Lincoln-Douglas debates in Illinois laid out the choice facing the nation and made Lincoln a national figure.
ush|6|l14-9|6-8|John Brown at Harpers Ferry — John Brown’s 1859 raid failed, but it convinced many Southerners that the North wanted to destroy their way of life.
ush|6|l14-10|6-8|Four Candidates in 1860 — With the Democrats split, Abraham Lincoln won the 1860 election with only Northern votes.
ush|6|l14-11|6-8|The South Secedes — After Lincoln’s election, seven Southern states left the Union and formed the Confederate States of America.
ush|6|l14-12|6-8|Fort Sumter — When last efforts at compromise failed, Confederate guns fired on Fort Sumter in April 1861 and the Civil War began.
ush|6|l15-1|6-8|The Border States — Four slave states stayed in the Union, and keeping them loyal was one of Lincoln’s first big challenges.
ush|6|l15-2|6-8|Comparing North and South — The North had more people, factories and railroads, but the South had skilled leaders and only needed to defend itself.
ush|6|l15-3|6-8|Leaders and Plans — Each side had very different leaders and plans, and the Union aimed to squeeze the South like a giant snake.
ush|6|l15-4|6-8|A Picnic Turns to Panic — The First Battle of Bull Run in 1861 showed both sides that the war would be long and costly.
ush|6|l15-5|6-8|Iron Ships — In 1862, the first battle between ironclad warships changed naval warfare forever.
ush|6|l15-6|6-8|A Soldier’s Days — Most of a Civil War soldier’s life was boredom, drill, bad food and homesickness, broken by moments of terror.
ush|6|l15-7|6-8|Rifles and Germs — New rifles made battles deadlier, but disease killed even more soldiers than bullets did.
ush|6|l15-8|6-8|Women Go to War — Women on both sides nursed the wounded, organized supplies, ran farms and businesses, and some even fought in disguise.
ush|6|l15-9|6-8|Grant Wins in the West — In 1862, Union victories in the West opened rivers into the South and made Ulysses S. Grant a hero.
ush|6|l15-10|6-8|Lee Takes Command — In the East, Robert E. Lee stopped a slow Union drive toward Richmond and won a string of victories in 1862.
ush|6|l15-11|6-8|The Bloodiest Day — The Battle of Antietam in September 1862 stopped Lee’s invasion and gave Lincoln the chance to change the purpose of the war.
ush|6|l16-1|6-8|Lincoln Moves Toward Freedom — Pressure from enslaved people who escaped, from abolitionists and from the needs of war pushed Lincoln toward ending slavery.
ush|6|l16-2|6-8|The Emancipation Proclamation — The Emancipation Proclamation, effective January 1, 1863, declared enslaved people in rebelling areas free and made the war a fight against slavery.
ush|6|l16-3|6-8|Black Soldiers Fight — About 180,000 Black men served in the Union army, fighting bravely while facing unequal treatment.
ush|6|l16-4|6-8|The Draft and the Riots — Both sides forced men into the army with a draft, and unfair rules led to anger and even violence.
ush|6|l16-5|6-8|Paying for the War — The Union paid for the war with new taxes, loans and paper money, while the Confederacy’s money lost almost all its value.
ush|6|l16-6|6-8|Prisoners of War — Overcrowded prison camps on both sides became places of terrible suffering, especially Andersonville in Georgia.
ush|6|l16-7|6-8|Three Days at Gettysburg — In July 1863, the Union victory at Gettysburg ended Lee’s last invasion of the North.
ush|6|l16-8|6-8|A Short Speech at Gettysburg — In the Gettysburg Address, Lincoln explained in a few words why the war was worth its terrible cost.
ush|6|l16-9|6-8|The Siege of Vicksburg — Grant’s capture of Vicksburg on July 4, 1863, gave the Union control of the Mississippi River and split the Confederacy.
ush|6|l16-10|6-8|Grant and Sherman Close In — In 1864, Grant and Sherman used relentless pressure and destruction to break the South’s ability to fight.
ush|6|l16-11|6-8|The Election and the Surrender — Lincoln won re-election in 1864, and in April 1865 Lee surrendered to Grant at Appomattox Court House.
ush|6|l16-12|6-8|Freedom and Loss — The war ended slavery and saved the Union, but Lincoln was killed and hundreds of thousands of Americans died.
ush|6|l17-1|6-8|Two Presidents’ Plans — Lincoln and then Andrew Johnson wanted to bring Southern states back quickly, with few demands to protect freed people.
ush|6|l17-2|6-8|The Black Codes — Southern states passed Black Codes to control freed people and keep them working almost as if they were still enslaved.
ush|6|l17-3|6-8|Congress Takes Charge — Angry at the Black Codes, Republicans in Congress took control of Reconstruction and put the South under military rule.
ush|6|l17-4|6-8|A President on Trial — In 1868, the House impeached President Johnson, and the Senate came just one vote short of removing him.
ush|6|l17-5|6-8|Citizens and Equal Protection — The 14th Amendment made all people born in the United States citizens and promised them equal protection of the laws.
ush|6|l17-6|6-8|The Vote and Black Leaders — The 15th Amendment protected Black men’s right to vote, and Black Southerners were elected to office for the first time.
ush|6|l17-7|6-8|Families, Churches and Schools — Freedpeople reunited families, built their own churches, and created schools and colleges that still stand today.
ush|6|l17-8|6-8|The Promise of Land — Many freedpeople hoped to own land, but the government took back most land promised to them, leaving them without a way to be independent.
ush|6|l17-9|6-8|Sharecropping — Sharecropping gave freed families work on the land, but it often trapped them in debt and poverty.
ush|6|l17-10|6-8|Terror and Resistance — Groups like the Ku Klux Klan used terror to stop Black Southerners from voting, and the federal government’s efforts to stop them faded.
ush|6|l17-11|6-8|The Compromise of 1877 — A deal to settle the disputed election of 1876 removed the last federal troops from Southern statehouses and ended Reconstruction.
ush|6|l17-12|6-8|Jim Crow and the New South — After Reconstruction, Southern leaders promoted new industry while passing laws that segregated Black Southerners and took away their vote.
ush|7|l18-1|6-8|Mining Booms and Boomtowns — Discoveries of gold and silver drew thousands of people west and created towns that grew fast and often died just as fast.
ush|7|l18-2|6-8|Rails Across the Continent — The first transcontinental railroad, finished in 1869, tied East and West together and was built largely by Chinese and Irish workers.
ush|7|l18-3|6-8|Cattle Drives and Cowboys — After the Civil War, cowboys of many backgrounds drove Texas cattle north to railroad towns, until fences and hard winters ended the open range.
ush|7|l18-4|6-8|Homesteads on the Prairie — The Homestead Act offered free land, but families who farmed the Plains faced a hard, lonely life with few trees and harsh weather.
ush|7|l18-5|6-8|The Exodusters — Thousands of Black Southerners moved to Kansas in the late 1870s, seeking land, safety and freedom after Reconstruction ended.
ush|7|l18-6|6-8|Farmers Fight Back — Farmers facing debt, low prices and high railroad rates joined together in the Grange and later the Populist Party.
ush|7|l18-7|6-8|The People of the Plains — Plains nations built their lives around the horse and the buffalo, and the loss of the herds and their land broke their way of life.
ush|7|l18-8|6-8|Sand Creek and Little Bighorn — Broken treaties led to bloody wars, including the Sand Creek Massacre in 1864 and the Lakota and Cheyenne victory at Little Bighorn in 1876.
ush|7|l18-9|6-8|Chief Joseph and the Nez Perce — In 1877, the Nez Perce tried to escape to Canada rather than lose their homeland, but they were stopped just short of the border.
ush|7|l18-10|6-8|Schools and Land Allotment — Boarding schools and the Dawes Act tried to erase Native cultures and took away most of the land Native nations still held.
ush|7|l18-11|6-8|Wounded Knee and the End of the Frontier — The killing of Lakota people at Wounded Knee in 1890 ended the wars on the Plains, the same year officials said the frontier was gone.
ush|7|l19-1|6-8|Steel, Oil and Electricity — New ways to make steel, refine oil and use electricity powered a huge growth of American industry after the Civil War.
ush|7|l19-2|6-8|Inventors Change Daily Life — Inventors such as Alexander Graham Bell, Thomas Edison and Lewis Latimer created new tools that changed how Americans lived and worked.
ush|7|l19-3|6-8|Giants of Big Business — Business leaders like Andrew Carnegie and John D. Rockefeller built giant companies, earning praise for their gifts and criticism for their methods.
ush|7|l19-4|6-8|A New Wave of Immigrants — Between 1880 and 1914, millions of immigrants, many from southern and eastern Europe, came to the United States for work and freedom.
ush|7|l19-5|6-8|Through the Golden Door — Immigrants from Europe passed through Ellis Island, while many Asian immigrants faced long waits and harsh questioning at Angel Island.
ush|7|l19-6|6-8|Closing the Door to Chinese Immigrants — In 1882, the Chinese Exclusion Act became the first U.S. law to ban immigrants because of their nationality.
ush|7|l19-7|6-8|Chicago Rises from the Ashes — American cities grew at amazing speed, and Chicago rebuilt after the Great Fire of 1871 to become a leader in tall buildings.
ush|7|l19-8|6-8|Helping Hands and Party Bosses — Settlement houses and political machines both helped city newcomers, but machines often traded help for votes and were often corrupt.
ush|7|l19-9|6-8|Long Days and Small Hands — Factory workers, including many children, faced long hours, low pay and danger, so they formed labor unions to demand better conditions.
ush|7|l19-10|6-8|Haymarket and Pullman — Two conflicts in the Chicago area, the Haymarket affair of 1886 and the Pullman Strike of 1894, showed how bitter the fight between workers and owners became.
ush|7|l19-11|6-8|Separate and Unequal — After Reconstruction, Jim Crow laws and the Plessy decision made segregation legal, and Ida B. Wells bravely exposed racial violence.
ush|7|l19-12|6-8|Two Paths Toward Equality — Booker T. Washington and W.E.B. Du Bois disagreed about how Black Americans should win equality, and Du Bois helped found the NAACP in 1909.
ush|7|l20-1|6-8|Reporters Who Dug Up the Truth — Progressive reformers and journalists called muckrakers exposed problems in cities, business and government so that the public would demand change.
ush|7|l20-2|6-8|The Jungle and Safer Food — Upton Sinclair's novel about Chicago's meatpacking plants led Congress to pass the first national food safety laws in 1906.
ush|7|l20-3|6-8|Giving Voters More Power — Progressives changed state and national government so that ordinary voters, not party bosses, had more control.
ush|7|l20-4|6-8|Roosevelt's Square Deal — Theodore Roosevelt used the power of the presidency to control big business and give workers and consumers what he called a Square Deal.
ush|7|l20-5|6-8|Using and Saving the Land — Roosevelt and other conservationists protected millions of acres, but Americans disagreed about whether to use natural resources wisely or leave wild places untouched.
ush|7|l20-6|6-8|Taft, Wilson and New Laws — Presidents Taft and Wilson continued Progressive reform, adding an income tax, the Federal Reserve and stronger rules for business.
ush|7|l20-7|6-8|A Long Road to the Ballot — Women fought for the right to vote for more than 70 years, winning first in western states and in Illinois before the national victory.
ush|7|l20-8|6-8|Winning the 19th Amendment — Using both quiet persuasion and bold protest, suffragists won the 19th Amendment in 1920, though many women of color still faced barriers to voting.
ush|7|l20-9|6-8|Why America Looked Outward — In the late 1800s, the United States began to gain lands overseas, including Alaska and Hawaii, in a push called imperialism.
ush|7|l20-10|6-8|War with Spain — In 1898, the United States fought a short war with Spain that began over Cuba and ended with the U.S. gaining Puerto Rico, Guam and the Philippines.
ush|7|l20-11|6-8|The Philippines and the Debate over Empire — When the U.S. took the Philippines, Filipinos fought for independence, and Americans argued over whether their country should rule other peoples.
ush|7|l20-12|6-8|A Canal and a Big Stick — Theodore Roosevelt helped Panama break from Colombia, built the Panama Canal, and claimed the right for the U.S. to police Latin America.
ush|8|l21-1|6-8|Four Roots of War — Militarism, alliances, imperialism and nationalism turned Europe into a place where one spark could start a huge war.
ush|8|l21-2|6-8|The Spark at Sarajevo — The killing of Austria-Hungary's Archduke Franz Ferdinand in 1914 set off a chain of war declarations across Europe.
ush|8|l21-3|6-8|A New Kind of War — Machine guns, artillery, poison gas and other new weapons led to trench warfare and terrible losses with little gain.
ush|8|l21-4|6-8|Neutral in Name — President Wilson wanted the United States to stay neutral, but trade, loans and old ties pulled Americans toward the Allies.
ush|8|l21-5|6-8|Submarines and a Secret Telegram — German submarine attacks and the Zimmermann Telegram turned many Americans against Germany.
ush|8|l21-6|6-8|Safe for Democracy — In April 1917, Wilson asked Congress for war, saying the fight would help make the world safe for democracy.
ush|8|l21-7|6-8|Raising an Army — Through a draft, the United States built an army of millions and sent about 2 million soldiers to France.
