ush|8|l21-8|6-8|Over There — Fresh American troops helped the Allies stop Germany's last big attack and push forward until the armistice of November 11, 1918.
ush|8|l21-9|6-8|Selling the War — The government raised money, shaped opinion and limited free speech to win support for the war.
ush|8|l21-10|6-8|New Jobs, New Journeys — The war opened factory jobs to women and to African Americans, starting the Great Migration from the South to Northern cities like Chicago.
ush|8|l21-11|6-8|The Deadly Flu of 1918 — A worldwide flu pandemic in 1918 and 1919 killed far more Americans than the war did.
ush|8|l21-12|6-8|Winning the War, Losing the Peace — Wilson hoped for a fair peace and a League of Nations, but the Treaty of Versailles punished Germany and the U.S. Senate rejected the League.
ush|8|l22-1|6-8|A Return to Normal — Tired of war and reform, voters chose Republican presidents who promised calm, low taxes and help for business.
ush|8|l22-2|6-8|The Red Scare — After the Russian Revolution, fear of communism led to raids, arrests and deportations that ignored many people's rights.
ush|8|l22-3|6-8|Closing the Door — New laws in the 1920s set quotas that sharply limited immigration, especially from southern and eastern Europe and Asia.
ush|8|l22-4|6-8|The Klan Returns — A new Ku Klux Klan grew to millions of members in the 1920s, spreading hate against Black Americans, Catholics, Jews and immigrants.
ush|8|l22-5|6-8|Prohibition — The 18th Amendment banned alcohol, but many people broke the law, and illegal liquor helped gangsters grow rich and powerful.
ush|8|l22-6|6-8|Science and Faith on Trial — The 1925 Scopes trial over teaching evolution showed a sharp divide between traditional religious values and modern ideas.
ush|8|l22-7|6-8|Cars and Credit — Mass-produced cars and buying on credit changed how Americans lived, worked and spent money.
ush|8|l22-8|6-8|Radio, Movies and Heroes — Radio, movies and sports created a shared national culture and made celebrities out of athletes and pilots.
ush|8|l22-9|6-8|Flappers and New Freedoms — Young women in the 1920s gained the vote and new freedoms, though most women's lives changed more slowly than the flapper image suggests.
ush|8|l22-10|6-8|Chicago's Black Metropolis — The Great Migration built a thriving Black community on Chicago's South Side, full of businesses, music and political power.
ush|8|l22-11|6-8|The Harlem Renaissance — In the 1920s, Black writers, artists and musicians in Harlem created works that celebrated African American life and pride.
ush|8|l22-12|6-8|Marcus Garvey and Black Pride — Marcus Garvey built a mass movement that urged Black people around the world to take pride in their African heritage and build their own economic power.
ush|8|l23-1|6-8|Cracks Under the Boom — Behind the prosperity of the 1920s were serious weaknesses: struggling farmers, unequal wealth, too much debt and risky stock buying.
ush|8|l23-2|6-8|Black Tuesday — In October 1929, the stock market crashed, wiping out fortunes and shaking Americans' confidence.
ush|8|l23-3|6-8|Banks Fail, Jobs Vanish — Thousands of banks failed and about one in four workers lost their jobs, spreading hardship across the country.
ush|8|l23-4|6-8|Hoover Tries to Respond — President Hoover took some steps to fight the Depression, but he opposed direct federal help to individuals, and many Americans blamed him.
ush|8|l23-5|6-8|Hoovervilles and the Bonus Army — Homeless families built shacktowns called Hoovervilles, and the army's removal of protesting veterans in 1932 turned many Americans against Hoover.
ush|8|l23-6|6-8|The Dust Bowl — Drought and poor farming methods turned the southern Great Plains into a Dust Bowl, driving hundreds of thousands of people west.
ush|8|l23-7|6-8|Roosevelt Takes Charge — Franklin D. Roosevelt won the 1932 election by promising bold action, and he moved fast to restore Americans' confidence.
ush|8|l23-8|6-8|The First Hundred Days — In his first hundred days, Roosevelt and Congress created programs that protected savings, put young people to work and brought power to rural areas.
ush|8|l23-9|6-8|A Second Wave of Reform — The Second New Deal created jobs, Social Security and stronger rights for workers, changes that still shape American life.
ush|8|l23-10|6-8|Women Who Led — Eleanor Roosevelt, Frances Perkins and Mary McLeod Bethune pushed the New Deal to help more people.
ush|8|l23-11|6-8|Uneven Help — The New Deal helped many people, but African Americans, Mexican Americans and Native Americans often received less help or faced unfair treatment.
ush|8|l23-12|6-8|Critics and Legacy — Critics said the New Deal did too much or too little, but many of its programs lasted, and full recovery came only with World War II.
ush|8|l24-1|6-8|Dictators on the March — In the 1920s and 1930s, dictators in Italy, Germany, the Soviet Union and Japan's military leaders built aggressive states that invaded their neighbors.
ush|8|l24-2|6-8|From Neutral to Arsenal — Americans wanted to stay out of the war, but Roosevelt found ways to send help to Britain and other nations fighting the Axis.
ush|8|l24-3|6-8|Pearl Harbor — Japan's surprise attack on Pearl Harbor on December 7, 1941, brought the United States into World War II.
ush|8|l24-4|6-8|Factories, Rations and Rosie — Americans at home turned factories into war plants, lived with rationing and filled millions of jobs, many of them women.
ush|8|l24-5|6-8|Fighting for Freedom Twice — African Americans, Native Americans and Mexican Americans served bravely while still facing discrimination at home.
ush|8|l24-6|6-8|Executive Order 9066 — The forced removal of Japanese Americans was later judged a grave injustice, and in 1988 the U.S. government formally apologized.
ush|8|l24-7|6-8|Turning the Tide — The Allies focused first on defeating Germany, winning key victories in the Atlantic, the Soviet Union, North Africa and Italy.
ush|8|l24-8|6-8|D-Day and Victory — On D-Day, June 6, 1944, the Allies invaded France, and less than a year later, Germany surrendered.
ush|8|l24-9|6-8|The Holocaust — Nazi Germany and its collaborators murdered about six million Jews, along with millions of other people, in the Holocaust.
ush|8|l24-10|6-8|Island by Island — After early Japanese victories, the U.S. won at Midway and then fought island by island toward Japan.
ush|8|l24-11|6-8|The Atomic Bomb — The United States built atomic bombs in secret and dropped two on Japan in August 1945, and Japan surrendered soon after.
ush|8|l24-12|6-8|The Cost and the Peace — World War II was the deadliest war in history, and it left the United States a world superpower that helped create the United Nations.
ush|9|l25-1|6-8|Veterans Come Home — The GI Bill helped millions of veterans go to school and buy homes, but not every veteran got the same help.
ush|9|l25-2|6-8|The Baby Boom — After the war, Americans had babies at record rates, and this baby boom changed schools, homes and businesses for decades.
ush|9|l25-3|6-8|Truman's Fair Deal — President Truman tried to build on the New Deal with his Fair Deal, and he won a surprise election in 1948.
ush|9|l25-4|6-8|Two Superpowers Split — After World War II, the United States and the Soviet Union became rivals in a long struggle called the Cold War.
ush|9|l25-5|6-8|Containment in Action — The U.S. tried to stop communism from spreading with a policy called containment, using aid like the Truman Doctrine and the Marshall Plan.
ush|9|l25-6|6-8|The Berlin Airlift and NATO — When the Soviets blocked the roads into West Berlin, the U.S. and its allies flew in supplies, and soon they formed the NATO alliance.
ush|9|l25-7|6-8|China and the Korean War — After communists won China in 1949, the U.S. fought in Korea to stop communism from spreading, and the war ended in a stalemate.
ush|9|l25-8|6-8|The Red Scare at Home — Fear of communist spies led to a Red Scare in which many Americans were accused without proof and lost their jobs.
ush|9|l25-9|6-8|Living With the Bomb — The U.S. and Soviet Union raced to build more powerful weapons, and fear of nuclear war became part of everyday life.
ush|9|l25-10|6-8|Highways and Suburbs — Under President Eisenhower, new highways and cheap homes helped millions of families move to the suburbs.
ush|9|l25-11|6-8|Cars, TV and Rock and Roll — In the 1950s, many families bought cars and TVs, and teenagers created a new youth culture built around rock and roll.
ush|9|l25-12|6-8|Left Out of the Boom — Millions of Americans, including many Black, Latino and Native American families, did not share in the 1950s boom.
ush|9|l26-1|6-8|Life Under Jim Crow — In the 1940s, Jim Crow laws kept Black Americans separate and unequal in the South, and discrimination followed them to the North.
ush|9|l26-2|6-8|Breaking Barriers — Jackie Robinson and President Truman's order to desegregate the military showed that barriers could fall.
ush|9|l26-3|6-8|Mendez and Brown — Lawyers for Mexican American and Black families won court cases that ruled segregated schools unfair, ending with Brown v. Board of Education in 1954.
ush|9|l26-4|6-8|The Little Rock Nine — When Arkansas's governor used troops to block nine Black students from a high school, President Eisenhower sent the Army to protect them.
ush|9|l26-5|6-8|Emmett Till and a Mother's Choice — The murder of Emmett Till, a Chicago boy, and his mother's brave choice to show the world shocked the nation and stirred the movement.
ush|9|l26-6|6-8|The Montgomery Bus Boycott — When Rosa Parks was arrested for keeping her bus seat, Black residents of Montgomery refused to ride the buses for over a year and won.
ush|9|l26-7|6-8|The Power of Nonviolence — Martin Luther King Jr. and others used nonviolent protest to win public support and force change.
ush|9|l26-8|6-8|Sit-Ins and Freedom Rides — Young people led sit-ins and Freedom Rides that challenged segregation directly, even in the face of violence.
ush|9|l26-9|6-8|Women Who Led — Women like Ella Baker, Fannie Lou Hamer and Daisy Bates were leaders and organizers at the heart of the civil rights movement.
ush|9|l26-10|6-8|Birmingham, 1963 — Shocking images of police attacking young protesters in Birmingham pushed President Kennedy to call for a strong civil rights law.
ush|9|l26-11|6-8|The March on Washington — In August 1963, about 250,000 people marched in Washington, D.C., for jobs and freedom, and King shared his dream of equality.
ush|9|l27-1|6-8|A New Frontier in Space — President Kennedy called Americans to a “New Frontier” and set the goal of landing a person on the Moon.
ush|9|l27-2|6-8|Close Calls in Cuba and Berlin — Crises over Cuba and Berlin brought the U.S. and the Soviet Union to the edge of nuclear war.
ush|9|l27-3|6-8|The Civil Rights Act of 1964 — After Kennedy was assassinated, President Johnson pushed through the Civil Rights Act of 1964, which banned segregation in public places.
ush|9|l27-4|6-8|Selma and the Right to Vote — Marchers in Selma, Alabama, faced violence to demand the vote, leading to the Voting Rights Act of 1965.
ush|9|l27-5|6-8|The Great Society — Johnson's Great Society created programs like Medicare and Head Start to fight poverty, and a new immigration law opened the door wider.
ush|9|l27-6|6-8|Black Power and a Nation in Pain — Some Black leaders called for Black Power and self-defense, cities erupted in unrest, and King was assassinated in 1968.
ush|9|l27-7|6-8|Farmworkers and Latino Rights — César Chávez, Dolores Huerta and Filipino American workers led farmworkers in a nonviolent struggle for fair pay and better conditions.
ush|9|l27-8|6-8|Equal Rights for Women — The women's movement challenged unfair treatment at work and in school and won new laws such as Title IX.
ush|9|l27-9|6-8|Native Nations and Disability Rights — Native Americans demanded respect for their treaties and self-rule, and people with disabilities began a movement for equal access.
ush|9|l27-10|6-8|Why America Went to Vietnam — The U.S. became involved in Vietnam to stop communism from spreading in Southeast Asia, based on the domino theory.
ush|9|l27-11|6-8|Escalation and the Tet Offensive — After the Gulf of Tonkin Resolution, the U.S. sent hundreds of thousands of troops, but the 1968 Tet Offensive made many Americans doubt the war could be won.
ush|9|l27-12|6-8|Protest and the War's End — A growing antiwar movement divided the nation, and President Nixon slowly withdrew U.S. troops, ending American fighting in 1973.
wcs|1|l1-1|k-2|Families Come in Many Sizes — Some families are big. Some families are small. All of them are families.
wcs|1|l1-2|k-2|Cousins, Aunts and Uncles — Your family can reach past your home to cousins, aunts, and uncles.
wcs|1|l1-3|k-2|Grandparents at Home — In many places, grandparents and grandchildren live in one home.
wcs|1|l1-4|k-2|What Grandparents Share — Grandparents often share stories, skills, and care with children.
wcs|1|l1-5|k-2|Chores Help Everyone — When each person does a chore, the whole family is helped.
wcs|1|l1-6|k-2|Every Family Has Its Own Ways — Each family has its own ways, and you can tell about yours.
wcs|1|l2-1|k-2|Apartments in Big Cities — In big cities, many families live in tall buildings called apartments.
wcs|1|l2-2|k-2|Houses on Stilts — Near water, some houses stand up high on poles to stay dry.
wcs|1|l2-3|k-2|Adobe Homes — In hot, dry places, thick mud-brick walls keep a home cool.
wcs|1|l2-4|k-2|The Ger That Moves — Herders in Mongolia live in gers, round homes they can take with them.
wcs|1|l2-5|k-2|Snow-Block Shelters — Long ago, Inuit hunters built warm shelters out of snow blocks.
wcs|1|l2-6|k-2|Farmhouses in Europe — Farm families build homes that fit their fields, animals, and weather.
wcs|1|l2-7|k-2|What Every Home Needs — Every home gives shelter, warmth, and water, and people who care.
wcs|10|l19-1|6-8|A City on a Lake — The Mexica built Tenochtitlan on an island in Lake Texcoco and fed it with farm plots built on the water.
wcs|10|l19-2|6-8|Nobles and Commoners — Aztec society was divided mainly into nobles, called pipiltin, and commoners, called macehualtin, each with different duties.
wcs|10|l19-3|6-8|Merchants and Enslaved People — Pochteca merchants held a special place in Aztec society, and enslaved people had some rights their children could build on.
wcs|10|l19-4|6-8|Kings and City-States — The Maya were never one empire; they lived in many city-states, each led by a holy king.
wcs|10|l19-5|6-8|Writing, Numbers and Time — The Maya created a full writing system, a number system with zero and careful calendars.
wcs|10|l19-6|6-8|The Sapa Inca and Cusco — The Inca built the largest empire in the Americas, ruled from Cusco by the Sapa Inca.
wcs|10|l19-7|6-8|Ayllu and Mit'a — Inca life rested on the ayllu, a kin group, and on mit'a, turns of labor owed to the state.
wcs|10|l19-8|6-8|Roads, Runners and Quipu — A vast road system, relay runners and knotted cords called quipu held the Inca Empire together.
wcs|10|l19-9|6-8|Great Achievements — The Aztec, Maya and Inca each built lasting achievements in farming, science, building and government.
wcs|10|l19-10|6-8|Hierarchies and Hard Truths — These societies had strict hierarchies, tribute and war, and a fair history names both the achievements and the costs.
wcs|10|l20-1|6-8|Spain and Portugal Divide the Americas — After 1492, Spain and Portugal claimed most of Central and South America and set up colonies ruled from Europe.
wcs|10|l20-2|6-8|The Encomienda — The encomienda gave Spanish colonists the right to Indigenous people's labor and tribute, and it led to terrible abuse.
wcs|10|l20-3|6-8|Voices Against Abuse — Some Spaniards, like Bartolome de las Casas, argued against the abuse of Indigenous people, and the crown passed laws to limit it.
wcs|10|l20-4|6-8|Peninsulares and Criollos — In Spanish America, people born in Spain held top posts, and American-born Spaniards resented being ranked below them.
wcs|10|l20-5|6-8|Mixed Societies — Colonial Latin America grew into mixed societies of Spanish, Indigenous and African people, and the law ranked these groups.
wcs|10|l20-6|6-8|The Casta Paintings — Casta paintings of the 1700s showed an idealized ranking of mixed families, and historians read them as ideas, not snapshots.
wcs|10|l20-7|6-8|Twelve Million People — From the 1500s to the 1800s, about 12.5 million Africans were forced onto ships bound for the Americas.
wcs|10|l20-8|6-8|Slavery in Brazil and the Caribbean — Sugar plantations in Brazil and the Caribbean depended on enslaved Africans, and the work was so deadly that owners kept buying more captives.
wcs|10|l20-9|6-8|Slavery in the United States — Fewer captives came to North America, but slavery there grew through births to about four million people by 1860.
wcs|10|l20-10|6-8|Maroons and Everyday Resistance — Enslaved people resisted in every colony, from quiet daily acts to whole free communities of runaways called maroons.
wcs|10|l20-11|6-8|The Haitian Revolution — From 1791 to 1804, enslaved people in Saint-Domingue overthrew slavery and French rule and founded Haiti.
wcs|10|l20-12|6-8|Abolition and Its Legacies — Slavery was abolished across the Americas between the 1790s and 1888, but its effects on inequality lasted long after.
wcs|11|l21-1|6-8|The Three Estates Meet — In 1789 the king called the Estates-General, and the commoners broke away to form a National Assembly.
wcs|11|l21-2|6-8|Seats Become Sides — Deputies who backed the king sat on the right and those who wanted change sat on the left.
wcs|11|l21-3|6-8|How Two Words Spread — Left and right started as seats in France and became words for political ideas around the world.
wcs|11|l21-4|6-8|The Left: Equality and Change — People on the left usually stress equality and are more open to changing how society works.
wcs|11|l21-5|6-8|The Right: Tradition, Order and Liberty — People on the right usually stress tradition and order, and often want liberty from too much government.
wcs|11|l21-6|6-8|Meanings Change by Place and Time — Left and right do not mean exactly the same thing in every country or every era.
wcs|11|l21-7|6-8|The Center — The center is the middle ground, where people mix ideas from left and right.
wcs|11|l21-8|6-8|A Line Is a Model — The left-right line is a simple model that helps us sort ideas, but it leaves a lot out.
wcs|11|l21-9|6-8|Two Lines Instead of One — Some thinkers add a second line to show views about personal freedom as well as money.
wcs|11|l21-10|6-8|Using the Labels Fairly — Use left and right to understand ideas, not to judge or stereotype people.
wcs|11|l22-1|6-8|Absolute Monarchy — In an absolute monarchy, one king or queen holds nearly all power and answers to no elected body.
wcs|11|l22-2|6-8|Constitutional Monarchy — In a constitutional monarchy, the king or queen is head of state, but elected lawmakers make the laws.
wcs|11|l22-3|6-8|Monarchs in Today's World — Only a small share of countries still have a monarch, and most of those monarchs have limited power.
wcs|11|l22-4|6-8|Direct Democracy — In a direct democracy, citizens vote on laws themselves instead of choosing others to do it.
wcs|11|l22-5|6-8|Representatives and Republics — In a representative democracy, people elect lawmakers to make decisions for them.
wcs|11|l22-6|6-8|Checks and Balances — Checks and balances split power so that no one part of government can rule alone.
wcs|11|l22-7|6-8|Free and Fair Elections — A free and fair election gives real choices, a secret vote, an honest count and a peaceful handover.
wcs|11|l22-8|6-8|One-Party States — In a one-party state, only one party may hold power, and other parties are banned or powerless.
wcs|11|l22-9|6-8|Military Rule — Military rule happens when the armed forces take over and govern a country.
wcs|11|l22-10|6-8|Dictators of the 1900s — In the 1900s, dictators on both the far left and the far right took total control and caused enormous suffering.
wcs|12|l23-1|9-10|Locke and Natural Rights — John Locke argued that people are born with rights to life, liberty and property, and that governments exist to protect those rights.
wcs|12|l23-2|9-10|Adam Smith and Free Markets — Adam Smith argued that free trade and competition, not government control, usually make a nation richer.
wcs|12|l23-3|9-10|Mill and the Harm Principle — John Stuart Mill argued that society may limit a person's freedom only to prevent harm to others.
wcs|12|l23-4|9-10|The Welfare State — Modern liberals argue that government should help people through programs like pensions and health care so they can use their freedom.
wcs|12|l23-5|9-10|Burke and Gradual Change — Edmund Burke argued that societies should change slowly and keep the wisdom stored in long-standing traditions and institutions.
wcs|12|l23-6|9-10|Conservatism and Its Critics — Conservatives today value tradition, family, faith and order, while critics argue that some traditions protect unfairness.
wcs|12|l23-7|9-10|Christian Democracy in Europe — Christian democratic parties in Europe mix support for markets with social programs, guided by Christian ideas about human dignity.
wcs|12|l23-8|9-10|Libertarianism — Libertarians want government kept very small so individuals have the widest possible freedom in both personal and economic life.
wcs|12|l23-9|9-10|The Center — Centrists borrow ideas from both left and right and often seek compromise, though critics say the middle can lack clear direction.
wcs|12|l23-10|9-10|Liberal in America and Europe — In the United States 'liberal' usually means favoring more government help, while in much of Europe it often means favoring markets and limited government.
wcs|12|l23-11|9-10|Reading Labels Fairly — Political labels are shortcuts, so a fair thinker asks what a person actually believes before judging.
wcs|12|l24-1|9-10|What Socialism Means — Socialism is the idea that major industries and wealth should be owned or controlled by the public or the community rather than by private owners.
wcs|12|l24-2|9-10|Marx and Class Struggle — Karl Marx argued that history is driven by conflict between classes and predicted that workers would overthrow capitalism.
wcs|12|l24-3|9-10|Communist States and Their Record — Twentieth-century communist states claimed Marx's ideas, ruled as one-party dictatorships and caused mass suffering, while some people in them also gained schooling and jobs.
wcs|12|l24-4|9-10|Democratic Socialism and Social Democracy — Democratic socialists and social democrats both work through elections, but social democrats keep a market economy while democratic socialists want to move further from capitalism.
wcs|12|l24-5|9-10|The Nordic Model — Sweden, Denmark and Norway combine market economies and private business with high taxes and large welfare states.
wcs|12|l24-6|9-10|Weighing the Nordic Model — People disagree about whether the Nordic model could work elsewhere and whether its benefits are worth its costs.
wcs|12|l24-7|9-10|Nations Uniting — In the 1800s nationalism helped unite Italians and Germans, each into a single country.
wcs|12|l24-8|9-10|Independence Movements — In the 1900s nationalism powered independence movements in India and across Africa that ended colonial rule.
wcs|12|l24-9|9-10|When Nationalism Turns Extreme — Extreme nationalism, as in Fascist Italy and Nazi Germany, treated other peoples as enemies and led to war and genocide.
wcs|12|l24-10|9-10|Populism Left and Right — Scholars describe populism as dividing society into a pure people and a corrupt elite, and it can appear on the left or the right.
wcs|12|l24-11|9-10|Weighing Populism — Scholars disagree about whether populism mainly strengthens democracy by voicing ignored people or weakens it by attacking checks on power.
wcs|13|l25-1|9-10|Marx's Promise — Karl Marx argued that history is a struggle between classes and that workers would one day overthrow owners and share all property.
wcs|13|l25-2|9-10|Lenin and the Revolution of 1917 — In 1917 Lenin's Bolsheviks seized power in Russia and began building a state run by one party in the name of the workers.
wcs|13|l25-3|9-10|One Party and Collective Farms — Under Stalin, one party controlled all of Soviet life and forced peasants onto collective farms at a terrible cost.
wcs|13|l25-4|9-10|The Holodomor — In 1932 and 1933 Soviet grain policies caused a famine in Ukraine that killed millions of people.
wcs|13|l25-5|9-10|The Gulag and the Great Terror — Stalin's regime sent millions to labor camps and, in 1937 and 1938, executed hundreds of thousands of people it called enemies.
wcs|13|l25-6|9-10|Mao and the Great Leap Forward — Mao Zedong's Great Leap Forward tried to transform China overnight and caused the deadliest famine in recorded history.
wcs|13|l25-7|9-10|The Cultural Revolution — From 1966 Mao urged young Red Guards to attack teachers, officials and old traditions, throwing China into a decade of turmoil.
wcs|13|l25-8|9-10|The Khmer Rouge in Cambodia — From 1975 to 1979 the Khmer Rouge emptied Cambodia's cities and killed or starved about 1.5 to 2 million people.
wcs|13|l25-9|9-10|Cuba and North Korea — Communist governments still rule in Cuba and North Korea, each with its own history of promises and repression.
wcs|13|l25-10|9-10|Why People Were Drawn to It — Communism attracted people with real hopes for equality, land and dignity, even as its record in power turned out very differently.
wcs|13|l25-11|9-10|The Wall Falls — In 1989 the Berlin Wall fell and Eastern European communist governments collapsed; in 1991 the Soviet Union itself broke apart.
wcs|13|l26-1|9-10|The March on Rome — Benito Mussolini used street violence and the fear of revolution to take power in Italy in 1922 and build the first fascist state.
wcs|13|l26-2|9-10|The Leader and the Total State — Italian Fascism built a cult around Mussolini, demanded that all of life serve the state, and used violence to silence critics.
wcs|13|l26-3|9-10|Why Germans Turned to Hitler — National humiliation, economic disaster and fear of communism helped Adolf Hitler's Nazi Party grow from a fringe group to Germany's largest party.
wcs|13|l26-4|9-10|Destroying a Democracy — Within months in 1933, the Nazis used emergency powers, terror and new laws to turn a democracy into a dictatorship.
wcs|13|l26-5|9-10|Racial Ideology and Antisemitism — Nazism rested on a false racial ideology that treated Jews as enemies and stripped them of rights step by step.
wcs|13|l26-6|9-10|Genocide Across Europe — During World War II, Nazi Germany and its collaborators murdered about six million Jews, along with millions of other victims.
wcs|13|l26-7|9-10|Resistance and Rescue — Even under terror, some people resisted and rescued, showing that choices remained possible.
wcs|13|l26-8|9-10|Franco's Spain — General Francisco Franco won Spain's civil war with help from Hitler and Mussolini and ruled as a dictator until 1975.
wcs|13|l26-9|9-10|Imperial Japan's Militarism — In the 1930s Japan's military gained control of the government and launched wars of conquest across Asia and the Pacific.
wcs|13|l26-10|9-10|Totalitarianism: Where the Extremes Meet — Hannah Arendt argued in 1951 that Nazi Germany and Stalin's USSR shared a new kind of rule, totalitarianism, built on ideology and terror.
wcs|14|l27-1|9-10|Income Versus Wealth — Income is money coming in over time, while wealth is what a person owns minus what they owe, and wealth is usually far more unequal.
wcs|14|l27-2|9-10|The Gini Coefficient — The Gini coefficient is one number, from 0 to 1, that sums up how evenly income is shared in a country.
wcs|14|l27-3|9-10|Poverty Lines and Living Standards — Poverty can be measured against a fixed line, like the World Bank's line for extreme poverty, or against what is normal in one country.
wcs|14|l27-4|9-10|What Social Mobility Means — Social mobility is how easily people move up or down the ladder of income and status, especially compared with their parents.
wcs|14|l27-5|9-10|The Great Gatsby Curve — Economists describe a pattern called the Great Gatsby curve: countries with more inequality tend to have less mobility between generations.
wcs|14|l27-6|9-10|Education as a Ladder — Education is one of the strongest ladders for moving up, but unequal schools can also pass advantages down.
wcs|14|l27-7|9-10|Class in Britain — In the United Kingdom, class has long been signaled by accent and schooling, and a small group from private schools still holds many top jobs.
wcs|14|l27-8|9-10|India's Reservations Debate — India reserves places in colleges, government jobs and legislatures for groups that faced caste discrimination, and people still debate how the system should work.
wcs|14|l27-9|9-10|China's Great Climb — After reforms began in 1978, China's economy grew fast and hundreds of millions of people left extreme poverty, while new gaps also opened.
wcs|14|l27-10|9-10|Explanations From Left and Right — People on the left and right often explain inequality differently, and each side points to real evidence.
wcs|14|l27-11|9-10|Tools Countries Use — Countries use taxes, public services and growth policies to shape inequality and mobility, and each tool has supporters and critics.
wcs|14|l28-1|9-10|The Korean Wave and Anime — Popular culture from South Korea and Japan now reaches fans around the world, a sign of cultural globalization.
wcs|14|l28-2|9-10|Bollywood and Reggaeton — Bollywood films and reggaeton music show how a local style can grow from its roots into a global one.
wcs|14|l28-3|9-10|Food That Travels — Food is one of the oldest ways cultures spread, and dishes change as they move to new places.
wcs|14|l28-4|9-10|Why People Migrate — People migrate because of push factors that drive them away and pull factors that draw them to a new place.
wcs|14|l28-5|9-10|Assimilation, Multiculturalism, Integration — Countries choose different policies for newcomers, from assimilation to multiculturalism to integration, and each has supporters and critics.
wcs|14|l28-6|9-10|Debates About Migration — People disagree about how much migration a country should allow and how, and each side raises real concerns.

wcs|14|l28-8|9-10|Indigenous Peoples Today — Indigenous peoples around the world are working to protect their lands, languages and self-government, with growing recognition of their rights.
wcs|14|l28-9|9-10|Exchange or Appropriation? — People argue about where cultural exchange ends and cultural appropriation begins, especially when sacred symbols or profit are involved.

wcs|15|l29-2|11-12|What a Line Leaves Out — A single line mixes up two different questions: how much the state should run the economy and how much it should control personal life.
wcs|15|l29-3|11-12|A Grid With Two Axes — Two-axis models place ideas on a grid, with economics on one line and freedom versus control on the other.

wcs|15|l29-9|11-12|Japan: Stability and Consensus — Japan's long rule by one conservative party has mixed market economics with close ties between government, business and workers.
wcs|15|l29-10|11-12|Models, Not Labels for People — Political models help us compare ideas, but they should never be used to sort or stereotype real people.
wcs|15|l30-1|11-12|Huntington's Three Waves — Political scientist Samuel Huntington described democracy spreading in waves, each followed by a partial pullback.
wcs|15|l30-2|11-12|Spain and Portugal — In the 1970s Portugal and Spain left long dictatorships behind, one through a military revolt and one through negotiated reform.

wcs|15|l30-6|11-12|Eastern Europe, 1989 — In 1989 communist governments across Eastern Europe fell, mostly without war, opening the way to elections and new constitutions.
wcs|15|l30-7|11-12|Chile Votes No — Chile returned to democracy after voters rejected Pinochet in a 1988 vote, and an elected president took office in 1990.
wcs|15|l30-8|11-12|What Backsliding Means — Political scientists use democratic backsliding for the slow weakening of democracy by elected leaders, rather than a sudden coup.
wcs|15|l30-9|11-12|Warning Lights — Independent courts, a free press and the rule of law act as warning lights; when they dim, democracy may be weakening.
wcs|15|l30-10|11-12|Backsliding on Left and Right — Scholars describe democratic backsliding under governments of both the left and the right, and those governments dispute the charge.
wcs|16|l31-1|11-12|What Propaganda Is — Propaganda is organized communication that tries to shape what large numbers of people believe and do, often by working on feelings instead of evidence.
wcs|16|l31-2|11-12|The Enemy Image — Propaganda often builds an enemy image, turning a whole group into a single threat that explains every problem.
wcs|16|l31-3|11-12|The Big Lie and Repetition — Propaganda relies on repetition, and a large, bold falsehood repeated often enough can come to feel true.
wcs|16|l31-4|11-12|Fear and the Bandwagon — Propaganda uses fear to rush people into action and the bandwagon to make them feel everyone already agrees.
wcs|16|l31-5|11-12|The Cult of Personality — A cult of personality turns a leader into a near-perfect hero whom citizens are expected to love and never question.
wcs|16|l31-6|11-12|Soviet and Nazi Posters Compared — Soviet and Nazi posters promoted opposite ideologies but used many of the same visual techniques.
wcs|16|l31-7|11-12|Propaganda in Democracies — Democracies at war have also used propaganda, which raises hard questions about when persuasion by a free government goes too far.
wcs|16|l31-8|11-12|Source, Purpose and Evidence — Three questions help with any message: who made it, why they made it and what evidence supports it.
wcs|16|l31-9|11-12|Lateral Reading — Professional fact-checkers judge a website by leaving it and seeing what other trusted sources say about it.
wcs|16|l31-10|11-12|Algorithms and Echo Chambers — Online feeds are ranked by algorithms, and scholars debate how much they trap people in echo chambers.
wcs|16|l31-11|11-12|Check Before You Share — A few quick moves, such as stopping, checking the source and finding better coverage, help stop false claims from spreading.

wcs|16|l32-5|11-12|The Red Brigades in Italy — The Red Brigades, a far-left group, used kidnapping and murder in 1970s Italy and helped turn public opinion against political violence.

wcs|16|l32-7|11-12|The Shining Path in Peru — The Shining Path, a Maoist movement, waged a brutal war in Peru from 1980, and its violence and the state's response cost tens of thousands of lives.

wcs|16|l32-10|11-12|Finding a Way Out — People do leave extremist movements, often helped by relationships, doubts and programs run by former members.
wcs|16|l32-11|11-12|Disagreeing Well — Citizens can disagree strongly and still respect each other by listening, arguing with evidence and describing the other side fairly.

wcs|17|l33-10|11-12|Comparing Without Stereotypes — A fair comparison uses evidence about patterns, names its sources and remembers that individuals differ from the average.
wcs|17|l34-1|11-12|Choosing a Strong Question — A strong capstone question is open, arguable and researchable, so reasonable people could answer it differently using evidence.

wcs|17|l34-5|11-12|The Strongest Case From the Left — Thinkers on the left often argue that opportunity grows when government reduces inequality and invests in schools, health and families.
wcs|17|l34-6|11-12|The Strongest Case From the Right — Thinkers on the right often argue that opportunity grows from economic growth, strong families and communities, and personal responsibility.
wcs|17|l34-7|11-12|Knowing the Other Side — You understand your own position only when you can state the strongest opposing case as well as its supporters can.

wcs|17|l34-9|11-12|Answering Counterarguments — A strong argument names the best objection to its claim and answers it honestly, by rebutting it, limiting the claim or conceding the point.
wcs|17|l34-10|11-12|Presenting and Discussing Respectfully — Sharing your argument well means speaking clearly, listening fully and disagreeing with ideas without attacking people.

wcs|5|l9-1|3-5|What Is a Rank? — Long ago, in many places, the family you were born into set your rank in life.
wcs|5|l9-2|3-5|Kings and Queens — A king or queen ruled a kingdom, and the crown usually passed to their child.
wcs|5|l9-3|3-5|Nobles and Knights — Nobles got land from the king and promised loyalty and soldiers in return.
wcs|5|l9-4|3-5|Peasants and Serfs — Most people were peasants who farmed, and many serfs could not leave the land they worked.
wcs|5|l9-5|3-5|Emperor, Shogun and Samurai — In Japan, samurai warriors served powerful lords, and people were sorted into ranks by birth.
wcs|5|l9-6|3-5|Emperor and Scholars in China — In China, scholars who passed hard tests could become officials who helped the emperor rule.
wcs|5|l9-7|3-5|Aztec Rulers and Commoners — The Aztec had a ruler, nobles and commoners, and most people farmed or worked with their hands.
wcs|5|l9-8|3-5|The Inca and Shared Work — The Inca ruler was at the top, and families paid their share by working for the state.
wcs|5|l9-9|3-5|Peasants Push Back — After a terrible plague, workers were scarce, and English peasants demanded more freedom.
wcs|5|l9-10|3-5|Old Ranks Begin to Fade — Over hundreds of years, many countries ended ranks set by birth and gave more people rights.
wcs|5|l10-1|3-5|Income and Wealth — Income is money you get over time; wealth is everything you own, minus what you owe.
wcs|5|l10-2|3-5|Saving and Owning — Saving money and owning things like land or a business can help wealth grow over time.
wcs|5|l10-3|3-5|Training and Pay — Jobs that need more training or school often pay more, on average.
wcs|5|l10-4|3-5|Luck and Where You Are Born — Luck and the place and family you are born into also shape how much people have.
wcs|5|l10-5|3-5|Being Poor Is Not Who You Are — Being poor means having too little money for needs; it is not a sign of a person's worth.
wcs|5|l10-6|3-5|Food Banks and Neighbors — Food banks and other groups collect food and share it with people who need it.
wcs|5|l10-7|3-5|Schools and Libraries for All — Public schools and libraries give everyone a chance to learn, no matter how much money they have.
wcs|5|l10-8|3-5|Rich and Poor Countries — Countries differ a lot in wealth, and the share of people in extreme poverty has fallen over time.
wcs|5|l10-9|3-5|What Is Fair? — Opportunity means having a real chance, and people have different ideas about what makes chances fair.
wcs|5|l10-10|3-5|Different Ideas About Helping — People disagree about who should help most: families, charities, businesses or the government.
wcs|6|l11-1|3-5|Making the Rules — A government is the group that makes and carries out the rules, called laws, for a place.
wcs|6|l11-2|3-5|Keeping People Safe — Governments keep people safe with police, firefighters and courts.
wcs|6|l11-3|3-5|Roads, Schools and Taxes — Governments use tax money to pay for things everyone shares, like roads and schools.
wcs|6|l11-4|3-5|Ruled by a King or Queen — In a monarchy, a king or queen rules, and the job usually passes down in one family.
wcs|6|l11-5|3-5|The People Choose — In a democracy, the people choose their leaders or their laws by voting.
wcs|6|l11-6|3-5|Power Without Fair Votes — In a dictatorship, one person or a small group holds power without fair elections.
wcs|6|l11-7|3-5|Athens Tries Something New — Ancient Athens was one of the first democracies, but only some people could vote.
wcs|6|l11-8|3-5|Choosing Representatives — In a representative democracy, people elect a smaller group to make laws for them.
wcs|6|l11-9|3-5|Kings and Queens Today — In a constitutional monarchy, a king or queen remains, but elected leaders make the laws.
wcs|6|l11-10|3-5|Countries With No King — A republic has no monarch; its leaders are chosen, often by the people's votes.
wcs|6|l12-1|3-5|Speaking and Believing Freely — Freedom of speech and freedom of religion let people share ideas and practice their faith.
wcs|6|l12-2|3-5|A Fair Trial — A fair trial means a person accused of a crime gets a fair chance to be heard before a judge or jury.
wcs|6|l12-3|3-5|The Right to Vote — Voting is a right that has slowly grown to include more and more people.
wcs|6|l12-4|3-5|Following the Law — A responsibility is a duty; obeying laws is a duty that keeps everyone safe.
wcs|6|l12-5|3-5|Serving on a Jury — Jury duty is a responsibility that makes the right to a fair trial possible.
wcs|6|l12-6|3-5|Paying Taxes and Voting — Paying taxes and voting are ways citizens help run their community.
wcs|6|l12-7|3-5|A Promise for the World — In 1948, the United Nations adopted the Universal Declaration of Human Rights, led by Eleanor Roosevelt.
wcs|6|l12-8|3-5|Different Countries, Different Ways — Countries protect rights in different ways, through constitutions, laws and courts.
wcs|6|l12-9|3-5|Disagreeing With Respect — In a free country, people disagree often, and they can do it with respect.
wcs|6|l12-10|3-5|Gandhi and King — Gandhi in India and Martin Luther King Jr. in the U.S. used peaceful protest to win rights.
wcs|6|l12-11|3-5|Nelson Mandela — Nelson Mandela helped end apartheid and became South Africa's first president elected by all races.
wcs|7|l13-1|3-5|Carnival in Brazil — Carnival in Brazil is a huge festival of music, dancing and costumes held just before Lent.
wcs|7|l13-2|3-5|Lunar New Year in China — In China, the Spring Festival welcomes the new year of the moon calendar with family, food and the color red.
wcs|7|l13-3|3-5|Seollal in Korea — Korea celebrates Lunar New Year as Seollal, with bows of respect to elders and a bowl of rice cake soup.
wcs|7|l13-4|3-5|Diwali, the Festival of Lights — Diwali is a festival of lights celebrated in India and around the world by Hindus, Sikhs and Jains.
wcs|7|l13-5|3-5|Día de los Muertos in Mexico — On Día de los Muertos, families in Mexico remember loved ones who have died, with altars, flowers and food.
wcs|7|l13-6|3-5|Oktoberfest in Germany — Oktoberfest in Munich began with a royal wedding in 1810 and grew into a giant fair of food, music and rides.
wcs|7|l13-7|3-5|Cherry Blossom Season in Japan — In spring, people in Japan gather under blooming cherry trees for hanami, a custom of enjoying the flowers.
wcs|7|l13-8|3-5|Independence Days — Many countries celebrate the day they became free to govern themselves.
wcs|7|l13-9|3-5|Four Reasons to Celebrate — Most festivals celebrate the harvest, history, family or faith, and many celebrate more than one.
wcs|7|l13-10|3-5|Chicago's Parades and Festivals — Chicago's neighborhoods hold parades and festivals that share the cultures of the people who live there.
wcs|7|l14-1|3-5|Mariachi from Mexico — Mariachi is a kind of Mexican music played by a group with violins, trumpets and guitars.
wcs|7|l14-2|3-5|Flamenco from Spain — Flamenco is a music and dance from southern Spain, shaped strongly by the Roma people of Andalusia.
wcs|7|l14-3|3-5|K-pop from South Korea — K-pop is popular music from South Korea that mixes many styles and is now loved around the world.
wcs|7|l14-4|3-5|The Sitar and the Talking Drum — The Indian sitar and the West African talking drum are instruments with special sounds and long histories.
wcs|7|l14-5|3-5|Soccer Around the World — Soccer, called football in most countries, is the world's most popular sport and is easy to play almost anywhere.
wcs|7|l14-6|3-5|Cricket and Baseball — Cricket is a giant sport in India, and baseball is loved in Japan and the Dominican Republic.
wcs|7|l14-7|3-5|Chess from India and Persia — Chess began in India as chaturanga, grew in Persia, and spread around the world.
wcs|7|l14-8|3-5|Anansi the Spider — Anansi is a clever spider from the folktales of the Akan people of Ghana, and his stories traveled to the Americas.
wcs|7|l14-9|3-5|The Brothers Grimm — Jacob and Wilhelm Grimm collected German folktales and published them in 1812.
wcs|7|l14-10|3-5|The Monkey King — The Monkey King is the bold, magical hero of Journey to the West, a famous Chinese novel.
wcs|7|l14-11|3-5|How Culture Travels and Blends — Music, games and stories spread when people move, trade and share, and they often blend into something new.
wcs|8|l15-1|6-8|Oaths, Lords and Vassals — Feudalism was a system where land was given in return for loyalty and military service.
wcs|8|l15-2|6-8|Life on the Manor — Most medieval people were peasants who farmed a lord's manor, and many were serfs tied to the land.
wcs|8|l15-3|6-8|The Church at the Center — The Catholic Church shaped daily life, owned great amounts of land, and collected a tithe from almost everyone.
wcs|8|l15-4|6-8|Those Who Pray, Fight and Work — Medieval writers described society as three orders, or estates: clergy, nobles and commoners.
wcs|8|l15-5|6-8|The Black Death — The plague of 1347–51 killed a huge share of Europe's people and left the survivors in a changed world.
wcs|8|l15-6|6-8|Workers After the Plague — With so many dead, surviving workers asked for higher pay, and lords tried hard to stop them.
wcs|8|l15-7|6-8|Magna Carta — In 1215 English barons forced King John to accept Magna Carta, which said even a king must follow the law.
wcs|8|l15-8|6-8|Towns, Guilds and Merchants — Growing towns gave merchants and craftsmen a new kind of life outside the manor, organized by guilds.
wcs|8|l15-9|6-8|A Kingdom in Crisis — By 1789 France was deep in debt, bread was expensive, and the Third Estate paid most of the taxes.
wcs|8|l15-10|6-8|The Old Order Falls — In 1789 the National Assembly ended noble privileges and declared that people are born free and equal in rights.
wcs|8|l15-11|6-8|Hope and Terror — The Revolution ended legal ranks, but it also turned violent, and people still argue about its meaning.
wcs|8|l16-1|6-8|Why Britain Went First — The Industrial Revolution began in Britain from about the 1760s, helped by coal, capital, trade and new inventions.
wcs|8|l16-2|6-8|The Factory System — Factories brought workers and machines together under one roof, run by the clock.
wcs|8|l16-3|6-8|Crowded Cities — Factories drew millions of people into fast-growing cities that were often crowded and unhealthy.
wcs|8|l16-4|6-8|Children at Work — Children worked long hours in mills and mines, and their testimony helped change public opinion.
wcs|8|l16-5|6-8|The Working Class — Factory workers who earned wages and owned no property became a new, large working class.
wcs|8|l16-6|6-8|The Rising Middle Class — Factory owners, merchants and professionals formed a growing middle class with new wealth and influence.
wcs|8|l16-7|6-8|Trade Unions — Workers formed trade unions to bargain together for better pay and conditions, despite early bans.
wcs|8|l16-8|6-8|The Factory Acts — Britain passed Factory Acts that limited children's hours and sent inspectors to enforce the rules.
wcs|8|l16-9|6-8|Marx and Engels — Karl Marx and Friedrich Engels argued in 1848 that history is a struggle between classes and that workers would overthrow capitalism.
wcs|8|l16-10|6-8|Marx's Critics and the Record — Critics say Marx's predictions failed and that states built in his name caused mass suffering; supporters defend parts of his analysis.
wcs|8|l16-11|6-8|Adam Smith and Free Markets — Adam Smith argued in 1776 that free markets and the division of labor create wealth, while also warning about their costs.
wcs|8|l16-12|6-8|Bismarck's Welfare State — In the 1880s Germany under Otto von Bismarck created the first national social insurance for workers.
wcs|9|l17-1|6-8|Four Groups in Old Texts — Ancient Hindu texts described society as four broad groups called varnas.
wcs|9|l17-2|6-8|Jati: Thousands of Birth Groups — In daily life, people belonged to a jati, a local birth group tied to work and marriage.
wcs|9|l17-3|6-8|Outside the Four Groups — Some communities were pushed below the four varnas and treated as untouchable.
wcs|9|l17-4|6-8|Voices Inside Hinduism — Many Hindu poets, saints and reformers taught that devotion to God matters more than birth.
wcs|9|l17-5|6-8|Sikh, Muslim and Christian Responses — Sikhism, Islam and Christianity taught equality before God, though caste habits often lingered.
wcs|9|l17-6|6-8|The Buddha and Later Buddhists — The Buddha taught that actions, not birth, make a person worthy, and Ambedkar later turned to Buddhism.
wcs|9|l17-7|6-8|Ambedkar's Struggle — Ambedkar led Dalits in peaceful protests to claim rights such as water and temple entry.
wcs|9|l17-8|6-8|Writing the Constitution — India's Constitution of 1950, drafted under Ambedkar's lead, abolished untouchability.
wcs|9|l17-9|6-8|Reservations in Schools and Jobs — India reserves a share of college seats, government jobs and elected seats for groups held back by caste.
wcs|9|l17-10|6-8|Change, Hope and Unfinished Work — Caste has weakened in many areas of life, but it still shapes marriage, work and politics for many.
wcs|9|l18-1|6-8|Four Kinds of People — Chinese thinkers ranked society as scholars, farmers, artisans and merchants.
wcs|9|l18-2|6-8|The Great Examinations — China chose many officials through hard written exams, a path upward that lasted until 1905.
wcs|9|l18-3|6-8|A Ladder That Was Hard to Climb — The exams opened a path up in theory, but wealth made it much easier to climb.
wcs|9|l18-4|6-8|Peace and Order After War — The Tokugawa shoguns ended a long age of war and ruled Japan from about 1603 to 1868.
wcs|9|l18-5|6-8|Samurai, Farmers, Artisans, Merchants — Tokugawa Japan used a four-rank order with samurai on top and merchants at the bottom.
wcs|9|l18-6|6-8|When Money Outgrew Rank — Merchants grew rich in Tokugawa cities while many samurai fell into debt.
wcs|9|l18-7|6-8|A New Government in 1868 — In 1868 reformers restored the emperor's rule and began to rebuild Japan quickly.
wcs|9|l18-8|6-8|The End of the Samurai Class — Meiji reforms abolished samurai privileges and made all men equal subjects under one law.
wcs|9|l18-9|6-8|Korea's Yangban — In Joseon Korea, the yangban elite gained office through exams, while other classes stood below them.
wcs|9|l18-10|6-8|How Rank Shaped Daily Life — Rank showed in clothing, speech, homes and manners across East Asia.
eco|1|l1-1|k-2|Needs Keep Us Well — A need is something you must have to live and stay well.
eco|1|l1-2|k-2|Wants Are Nice to Have — A want is something nice to have, but you can live without it.
eco|1|l1-3|k-2|Two Piles — We can sort things into a needs pile and a wants pile.
eco|1|l1-4|k-2|Is It a Need or a Want? — Some things can be a need or a want, so we think it through.
eco|1|l1-5|k-2|Choose One, Give One Up — When you cannot have both, you choose one and give the other up.
eco|1|l1-6|k-2|Not Enough for All — Scarce means there is not enough for everyone who wants it.
eco|1|l1-7|k-2|The Class Coin Jar — A group can vote to choose one thing to buy together.
eco|1|l2-1|k-2|What a Good Is — A good is a thing you can hold, like an apple, a book or shoes.
eco|1|l2-2|k-2|People Who Make Goods — Some workers make goods for other people to use.
eco|1|l2-3|k-2|What a Service Is — A service is work someone does for you.
eco|1|l2-4|k-2|Good or Service? — Ask: can I hold it? If yes, it is a good. If it is work, it is a service.
eco|1|l2-5|k-2|Work and Pay — A job is work someone is paid money to do.
eco|1|l2-6|k-2|What a School Buys and Hires — A school buys goods and pays people for services.
eco|10|l19-1|6-8|GDP: The Size of the Economy — GDP is the total value of all final goods and services made in a country in one year.
eco|10|l19-2|6-8|Growth, Recession and the Cycle — The economy grows most years, but sometimes it shrinks for a while; that pattern is called the business cycle.
eco|10|l19-3|6-8|Real GDP versus Nominal GDP — Real GDP removes the effect of rising prices, so it shows whether we really made more.
eco|10|l19-4|6-8|Inflation and the Dollar — Inflation is a rise in the general level of prices, and it means each dollar buys less.
eco|10|l19-5|6-8|How Prices Are Measured — The Consumer Price Index tracks the cost of a fixed basket of things households buy.
eco|10|l19-6|6-8|Who Counts as Unemployed — A person is unemployed only if they have no job, can work, and are looking for work.
eco|10|l19-7|6-8|Three Kinds of Taxes — Governments collect money mostly through taxes on income, on sales and on property.
eco|10|l19-8|6-8|Where Federal Money Goes — Most federal spending goes to a few big items: Social Security, health programs, defense and interest on debt.
eco|10|l19-9|6-8|Who Pays What Share — A tax can take a bigger share, the same share or a smaller share of income as income rises.
eco|10|l19-10|6-8|The Federal Reserve — The Federal Reserve watches prices and jobs and sets a key interest rate to keep the economy steady.
eco|10|l20-1|6-8|Imports and Exports — An export is something sold to another country; an import is something bought from another country.
eco|10|l20-2|6-8|The Trade Balance — The trade balance is exports minus imports; a negative balance is a trade deficit.
eco|10|l20-3|6-8|Comparative Advantage — Two countries both gain when each makes what it gives up the least to make, then trades.
eco|10|l20-4|6-8|Tariffs and Who Pays — A tariff is a tax on imports; much of its cost usually shows up in higher prices for buyers at home.
eco|10|l20-5|6-8|Exchange Rates on a Trip — An exchange rate is the price of one country's money in another country's money.
eco|10|l20-6|6-8|A Strong or a Weak Dollar — When the dollar gets stronger, imports and trips abroad get cheaper for Americans, and U.S. exports cost more for others.
eco|10|l20-7|6-8|Supply Chains: One Phone — A supply chain is every step and place that goods pass through, from raw materials to the buyer.
eco|10|l20-8|6-8|Why Some Countries Are Richer — Countries grow richer mostly by making more per worker, helped by education, health, good institutions and trade.
eco|10|l20-9|6-8|Chicago: Where Trade Meets — Chicago grew as a trade hub because railroads, waterways, farms and markets all met there.
eco|10|l20-10|6-8|The Economic Way of Thinking — Economists look at choices, costs, incentives and trade-offs to understand people, markets and nations.
eco|11|l21-1|9-10|Wants Outrun Resources — Scarcity means that wants are unlimited while the resources to satisfy them are limited, so every person and every society must choose.
eco|11|l21-2|9-10|Scarcity Is Not a Shortage — A shortage is temporary and exists at a particular price; scarcity is permanent and applies to almost every good.
eco|11|l21-3|9-10|No Such Thing as a Free Lunch — Almost every good uses scarce resources, so someone pays for it even when the price to you is zero.
eco|11|l21-4|9-10|Land and Labor — Every good is made from four kinds of resources called the factors of production; land and labor are the first two.
eco|11|l21-5|9-10|Capital and Entrepreneurship — Capital is the tools and buildings made to produce other things, and entrepreneurs are the people who combine all the factors and take the risk.
eco|11|l21-6|9-10|More From the Same — Productivity is output per unit of input; raising it is the main way a society gets more from the same scarce resources.
eco|11|l21-7|9-10|What, How and for Whom — Because of scarcity, every economy must answer three questions: what to produce, how to produce it, and who gets it.
eco|11|l21-8|9-10|Tradition and Command — A traditional economy answers the three questions by custom; a command economy answers them through a central authority that plans and gives orders.
eco|11|l21-9|9-10|Markets and Mixed Economies — A market economy answers the three questions through prices set by buyers and sellers; every real economy, including the United States, is a mix.
eco|11|l21-10|9-10|Incentives Matter — An incentive is a reward or penalty that changes behavior; economists predict that people respond to incentives, sometimes in ways nobody intended.
eco|11|l21-11|9-10|The Economic Way of Thinking — Economists assume people weigh costs and benefits, build simple models to isolate one cause at a time, and separate what is from what ought to be.
eco|11|l22-1|9-10|Every Choice Is a Trade-Off — Because resources are scarce, choosing one option always means giving up others; that exchange is called a trade-off.
eco|11|l22-2|9-10|The Next-Best Alternative — Opportunity cost is the value of the single next-best alternative you give up, not a list of everything you did not choose.
eco|11|l22-3|9-10|Costs You Pay and Costs You Give Up — Explicit costs are the money you pay out; implicit costs are the value of what you give up; both count when you judge whether a choice paid off.
eco|11|l22-4|9-10|A Table of Possibilities — A production possibilities curve shows every combination of two goods an economy can make with all its resources fully used; points on it are efficient.
eco|11|l22-5|9-10|Why the Curve Bows Outward — Because resources are not equally good at making everything, each extra unit of a good costs more of the other good; this bends the curve outward.
eco|11|l22-6|9-10|Shifting the Curve Outward — Economic growth moves the whole curve outward; it comes from more or better resources and technology, and today's choices between capital and consumer goods shape it.
eco|11|l22-7|9-10|One More Hour, One More Unit — Most decisions are about a little more or a little less; keep going as long as the marginal benefit of the next step is at least its marginal cost.
eco|11|l22-8|9-10|Sunk Costs Stay Sunk — A sunk cost has already been paid and cannot be recovered, so it should not affect a decision about what to do next.
eco|11|l22-9|9-10|Weighing a Real Decision — Cost-benefit analysis lists every cost, including opportunity costs, and every benefit of a choice, puts dollar values on what it can, and compares them.
eco|11|l22-10|9-10|Why People Specialize — People and places gain by specializing in what they do at the lowest opportunity cost, even when someone else is better at everything.
eco|11|l22-11|9-10|Trade Makes Both Sides Better Off — In a voluntary trade both sides expect to gain, because each values what it gets more than what it gives up; trade creates value rather than just moving it.
eco|11|l22-12|9-10|Investing in Your Own Skills — Education and training cost tuition plus the pay you give up, and they can raise what you earn for years; weigh the full cost against the expected gain.
eco|12|l23-1|9-10|The Law of Demand — When the price of a good rises and nothing else changes, people buy less of it; when the price falls, they buy more.
eco|12|l23-2|9-10|Why Buyers Pull Back — Demand slopes down for three reasons: buyers switch to substitutes, their money stretches less far, and each extra unit is worth less to them.
eco|12|l23-3|9-10|Adding Up the Buyers — Market demand is found by adding every buyer's quantity demanded at each price, one price at a time.
eco|12|l23-4|9-10|The Law of Supply — When the price of a good rises and nothing else changes, sellers offer more of it; when the price falls, they offer less.
eco|12|l23-5|9-10|Why Sellers Offer More — Sellers offer more at higher prices because each extra unit costs more to make, and a higher price makes those costly units worth producing.
eco|12|l23-6|9-10|Along the Curve or the Whole Curve — A change in the good's own price moves you along the curve; a change in anything else shifts the whole curve.
eco|12|l23-7|9-10|Income: Normal and Inferior Goods — More income raises demand for normal goods and lowers demand for inferior goods.
eco|12|l23-8|9-10|Substitutes and Complements — A rise in the price of a substitute shifts demand right; a rise in the price of a complement shifts demand left.
eco|12|l23-9|9-10|Tastes, Expectations and Buyers — Changes in tastes, in what buyers expect, and in the number of buyers all shift the demand curve.
eco|12|l23-10|9-10|Input Costs and Technology — Higher input costs shift supply left; better technology shifts supply right.
eco|12|l23-11|9-10|Taxes, Subsidies, Expectations and Sellers — Taxes and subsidies, sellers' expectations, and the number of sellers also shift the supply curve.
eco|12|l23-12|9-10|One Block, Both Curves — To read any market event, name which curve moved, which way, and which shifter did it.
eco|12|l24-1|9-10|Finding the Meeting Point — Equilibrium is the one price at which quantity demanded equals quantity supplied, and a market tends to move toward it.
eco|12|l24-2|9-10|Shortage: Price Too Low — When the price is below equilibrium, buyers want more than sellers offer, and the shortage pushes the price up.
eco|12|l24-3|9-10|Surplus: Price Too High — When the price is above equilibrium, sellers offer more than buyers want, and the surplus pushes the price down.
eco|12|l24-4|9-10|Demand Shifts: Two Cases — A rightward demand shift raises both price and quantity; a leftward demand shift lowers both.
eco|12|l24-5|9-10|Supply Shifts: Two Cases — A leftward supply shift raises price and lowers quantity; a rightward supply shift lowers price and raises quantity.
eco|12|l24-6|9-10|Both Curves Move at Once — When demand and supply both shift, one of price or quantity is certain and the other depends on which shift is bigger.
eco|12|l24-7|9-10|Measuring the Response — Price elasticity of demand is the percent change in quantity demanded divided by the percent change in price.
eco|12|l24-8|9-10|What Makes Demand Elastic — Demand is more elastic when a good has close substitutes, takes a big share of the budget, is a luxury rather than a necessity, and when buyers have time to adjust.
eco|12|l24-9|9-10|Elasticity and Total Revenue — Raising the price lowers total revenue when demand is elastic and raises it when demand is inelastic.
eco|12|l24-10|9-10|Elasticity of Supply — Price elasticity of supply measures how much sellers change quantity supplied when the price changes, and it depends mostly on time.
eco|12|l24-11|9-10|Elasticity in Everyday Decisions — Knowing whether demand is elastic or inelastic predicts how buyers, sellers and governments will fare when a price changes.
eco|12|l24-12|9-10|The Ticket Puzzle Solved — A concert ticket priced below equilibrium with a fixed supply creates a shortage, and the resale market is where the price rises to clear it.
eco|13|l25-1|9-10|Three Jobs of a Price — A market price does three jobs at once: it signals, it rations and it motivates.
eco|13|l25-2|9-10|A Market That Clears — Left alone, a price moves toward the level where quantity demanded equals quantity supplied, so there is no shortage and no surplus.
eco|13|l25-3|9-10|A Ceiling That Binds — A price ceiling set below equilibrium creates a shortage; a ceiling set above equilibrium does nothing.
eco|13|l25-4|9-10|Rent Control — Rent control is a price ceiling on apartments: it helps tenants who already have a lease and tends to shrink the supply of housing over time.
eco|13|l25-5|9-10|Gas Lines, Explained — The 1970s gasoline lines were a shortage created by a binding price ceiling on top of real scarcity.
eco|13|l25-6|9-10|A Floor That Binds — A price floor set above equilibrium creates a surplus; a floor set below equilibrium does nothing.
eco|13|l25-7|9-10|The Minimum Wage Debate — The minimum wage is a price floor on labor; the simple model predicts fewer jobs, and economists disagree about how large that effect is in practice.
eco|13|l25-8|9-10|Farm Price Supports — When the government guarantees a crop price above the market, it must buy or store the surplus, and taxpayers pay for it.
eco|13|l25-9|9-10|Rationing Without Prices — When a price cannot ration, something else will: waiting, lotteries, coupons or connections, each with its own costs.
eco|13|l25-10|9-10|Black Markets — A binding price control pushes trades outside the law, where prices are higher, quality is unchecked and buyers have no protection.
eco|13|l25-11|9-10|Taxes, Subsidies and Who Pays — Whoever the law names to pay a tax, the real burden is shared by buyers and sellers, and it falls more heavily on whichever side is less able to walk away.
eco|13|l26-1|9-10|What Market Failure Means — A market fails when prices leave out a cost or benefit, so buyers and sellers produce too much or too little of something.
eco|13|l26-2|9-10|Costs That Land on Others — A negative externality makes the market produce too much, because the seller's cost is lower than the true cost to society.
eco|13|l26-3|9-10|Benefits That Spill Over — A positive externality makes the market produce too little, because buyers count only their own benefit and not the benefit to others.
eco|13|l26-4|9-10|Taxes, Rules and Lawsuits — Governments correct externalities with taxes that put the missing cost into the price, with regulations that set limits, or with courts that enforce property rights.
eco|13|l26-5|9-10|Tradable Permits — A cap-and-trade system fixes the total amount of pollution and lets firms buy and sell the right to pollute, so cleanup happens where it is cheapest.
eco|13|l26-6|9-10|Non-Rival and Non-Excludable — A public good is one that many can use at once and that no one can be kept from using, which is why private sellers cannot make money providing it.
eco|13|l26-7|9-10|The Free-Rider Problem — When people can enjoy a good without paying, many will not pay, and the good goes underprovided unless everyone is required to contribute.
eco|13|l26-8|9-10|The Tragedy of the Commons — A resource that anyone may use and nobody owns gets overused, because each user ignores the cost their use imposes on everyone else.
eco|13|l26-9|9-10|When One Side Knows More — When sellers know more than buyers, buyers pay less for everything, the best goods leave the market, and quality collapses unless information can be shared.
eco|13|l26-10|9-10|What Governments Do in a Market Economy — In a mixed economy, government sets the rules, provides public goods, corrects externalities, protects competition, aids the poor and steadies the whole economy.
eco|13|l26-11|9-10|The Limits of Government — Government can fail too: it lacks information, responds to lobbying and elections, and its rules can produce results nobody intended.
eco|14|l27-1|9-10|Revenue Is Not Profit — Profit is total revenue minus total cost, and a business can bring in a lot of money and still lose.
eco|14|l27-2|9-10|Fixed and Variable Costs — Fixed costs stay the same no matter how much you produce; variable costs rise with every unit you make.
eco|14|l27-3|9-10|The Owner's Hidden Cost — Economic profit subtracts the owner's opportunity cost as well as the bills, so it is smaller than the profit on the napkin.
eco|14|l27-4|9-10|Total, Average and Marginal Cost — Average cost is total cost divided by output; marginal cost is the extra cost of one more unit, and it drives decisions.
eco|14|l27-5|9-10|Diminishing Returns — When you keep adding workers to a fixed space, each new worker eventually adds less output than the one before.
eco|14|l27-6|9-10|Economies of Scale — In the long run a bigger operation can often produce each unit more cheaply, up to a point where it becomes too big to manage.
eco|14|l27-7|9-10|Marginal Revenue Equals Marginal Cost — Produce every unit whose marginal revenue is at least its marginal cost, and stop where the two are equal.
eco|14|l27-8|9-10|Break-Even and Shutdown — A firm breaks even when revenue covers all costs, and it should stay open in a bad month as long as revenue covers its variable costs.
eco|14|l27-9|9-10|Sole Proprietors and Partners — A sole proprietorship is easy to start and keeps all the profit, but the owner risks everything; a partnership shares both.
eco|14|l27-10|9-10|Corporations and LLCs — A corporation is a legal person separate from its owners, which limits their risk and lets it raise money by selling stock.
eco|14|l27-11|9-10|Entrepreneurs and Risk — An entrepreneur combines resources, takes on risk, and is paid in profit if the idea works and loss if it does not.
eco|14|l27-12|9-10|A Chicago Print Shop, Start to Finish — Every idea in this chapter can be applied in order to one real-feeling business to decide whether to grow.
eco|14|l28-1|9-10|Three Questions About Any Market — Count the sellers, check how hard it is to get in, and ask whether buyers can leave; the answers tell you how much power sellers have over price.
eco|14|l28-2|9-10|Perfect Competition — When many sellers offer an identical product and anyone can enter, each seller must take the market price and profits are pushed toward normal.
eco|14|l28-3|9-10|Walls Around a Market — Barriers to entry such as huge startup costs, patents, licenses and control of a resource let a few firms keep high profits for a long time.
eco|14|l28-4|9-10|Monopolistic Competition — Many sellers offering slightly different products each have a little power over price, but easy entry keeps long-run profits near normal.
eco|14|l28-5|9-10|Brands and Advertising — Firms advertise to set their products apart; an ad pays only if the extra sales bring in more than it costs, and ads can inform or merely persuade.
eco|14|l28-6|9-10|Oligopoly: A Few Big Players — In an oligopoly a few large firms dominate, so each must guess how its rivals will react, which can lead to price wars or to cooperation that the law forbids.
eco|14|l28-7|9-10|Monopoly and Price Setting — A monopoly is the only seller with no close substitutes, so it chooses its price, but demand still limits it, and it sells less at a higher price than a competitive market would.
eco|14|l28-8|9-10|Natural Monopoly and Regulation — When one firm can serve a whole market more cheaply than two, the market is a natural monopoly, and governments usually regulate its prices or run it themselves.
eco|14|l28-9|9-10|Antitrust and Standard Oil — Antitrust laws, starting with the Sherman Act of 1890, forbid price fixing and unfair efforts to gain or keep a monopoly.
eco|14|l28-10|9-10|Mergers and the Chicago School — Regulators judge mergers mainly by their effect on consumers' prices and choices, a standard shaped by Chicago economists and still debated today.
eco|14|l28-11|9-10|Labor Markets and Unions — Wages are the price of labor, set by the supply and demand for workers, and unions and employer power can push them up or down.
eco|15|l29-1|11-12|Final Goods, Not Every Sale — GDP is the market value of all final goods and services produced within a country in a year, and it counts each thing only once.
eco|15|l29-2|11-12|Inside the Borders, In This Year — GDP counts production that happens inside the country during the period, and it leaves out used goods, pure financial trades and transfer payments.
eco|15|l29-3|11-12|Spending Equals Income — Every dollar spent on final goods is a dollar of income to someone, so GDP can be measured by adding up spending or by adding up income.
eco|15|l29-4|11-12|Consumption, Investment, Government — The expenditure approach sorts spending by who does it: households (C), businesses building capacity (I), government buying goods and services (G), and foreigners (net exports).
eco|15|l29-5|11-12|Net Exports and the Full Formula — Net exports (exports minus imports) complete the formula, and a worked example shows why imports are subtracted rather than ignored.
eco|15|l29-6|11-12|The U.S. Numbers, Rounded — U.S. GDP is measured in trillions of dollars, and its rounded shares show a consumer-driven economy with a small trade deficit.
eco|15|l29-7|11-12|When Prices Rise but Output Does Not — Nominal GDP can grow just because prices rose, so economists compute real GDP using one fixed set of prices.
eco|15|l29-8|11-12|The Price Index and the Deflator — A price index turns nominal GDP into real GDP with one division: real GDP = nominal GDP divided by the index, times 100.
eco|15|l29-9|11-12|Growth Rates and the Long Run — Economic growth is the percent change in real GDP, and small yearly rates add up to large differences over decades.
eco|15|l29-10|11-12|GDP per Person and Comparing Countries — GDP per capita divides output by population, which is the fair way to compare living standards between countries or across time.
eco|15|l29-11|11-12|The Invisible Economy — GDP misses unpaid work, the underground economy and leisure, so it can rise or fall for reasons that have nothing to do with how well people live.
eco|15|l29-12|11-12|The Environment and Who Gets What — GDP does not subtract environmental damage and says nothing about how output is distributed, so a rising GDP does not guarantee a better life for most people.
eco|15|l29-13|11-12|Beyond GDP: Other Yardsticks — Economists supplement GDP with measures of health, education, income spread and environmental cost, using each number for the question it actually answers.
eco|15|l30-1|11-12|Building a Price Index — The CPI tracks the cost of a fixed market basket of goods and services, compared with a base year set equal to 100.
eco|15|l30-2|11-12|The Inflation Rate — The inflation rate is the percent change in the price index from one period to the next, not the difference in index points.
eco|15|l30-3|11-12|Where the CPI Misses — Because the basket is fixed and quality keeps changing, the CPI can overstate the true rise in living costs, and no family's own inflation matches it exactly.
eco|15|l30-4|11-12|Purchasing Power and Real Wages — To compare dollars from different years, adjust with the CPI; a real wage rises only when pay grows faster than prices.
eco|15|l30-5|11-12|Winners and Losers From Inflation — Unexpected inflation moves buying power from lenders to borrowers and from people on fixed incomes to people whose pay keeps up.
eco|15|l30-6|11-12|Deflation and Runaway Inflation — Falling prices sound good but can deepen a slump, and very rapid inflation, usually caused by printing money, can wreck an economy.
eco|15|l30-7|11-12|Who Is in the Labor Force — Each month the government sorts adults into employed, unemployed and not in the labor force; only people without work who are looking count as unemployed.
eco|15|l30-8|11-12|The Unemployment Rate — The unemployment rate is the number unemployed divided by the labor force, times 100, not divided by the whole adult population.
eco|15|l30-9|11-12|Discouraged Workers and Participation — The participation rate shows what share of adults are working or looking, and discouraged workers can make unemployment look lower than it feels.
eco|15|l30-10|11-12|Between Jobs and Out of Season — Some unemployment is normal: people between jobs (frictional) and people whose work follows the calendar (seasonal).
eco|15|l30-11|11-12|Mismatch and Recession — Structural unemployment comes from a mismatch between workers and the jobs available; cyclical unemployment comes from a weak economy.
eco|15|l30-12|11-12|Full Employment and the Natural Rate — Full employment does not mean zero unemployment; it means only frictional and structural unemployment remain, at the natural rate.
eco|16|l31-1|11-12|Four Phases, One Pattern — The business cycle is the repeating rise and fall of total output around its long-run trend, and it has four named phases: expansion, peak, contraction and trough.
eco|16|l31-2|11-12|What Counts as a Recession — A recession is a broad, significant and lasting decline in economic activity; in the United States a committee of economists dates the peaks and troughs after the fact.
eco|16|l31-3|11-12|Reading a Downturn in Numbers — Quarterly growth rates, job counts and the unemployment rate move together in a downturn, and reading them side by side shows how far and how fast the economy is falling.
eco|16|l31-4|11-12|Leading Indicators — Leading indicators are measures that tend to turn before the whole economy does, so they give early but imperfect warning of the next phase.
eco|16|l31-5|11-12|Coincident and Lagging Indicators — Coincident indicators move with the economy and confirm where it is now; lagging indicators turn afterward and confirm that a turn really happened.
eco|16|l31-6|11-12|Aggregate Demand and Supply — Aggregate demand is total spending on a country's output at each price level, aggregate supply is total production, and shifts in either one move output and prices for the whole economy.
eco|16|l31-7|11-12|Causes of Booms and Busts — Recessions and booms start from shocks, such as a collapse in spending, a jump in costs, a financial panic or a policy change, and they spread because one person's spending is another person's income.
eco|16|l31-8|11-12|The Great Depression — From 1929 to 1933 U.S. output fell by roughly a quarter and unemployment reached about 25%, the deepest contraction in the country's history, driven by collapsing spending and a wave of bank failures.
eco|16|l31-9|11-12|The Recession of 2007 to 2009 — A housing boom financed by risky lending collapsed into a financial crisis, producing the deepest U.S. recession since the 1930s, with unemployment reaching about 10%.
eco|16|l31-10|11-12|The 2020 Recession — The COVID-19 recession of 2020 was the shortest on record, only two months, but it was also the sharpest, with unemployment jumping to nearly 15% in a single month before a fast partial recovery.
eco|16|l31-11|11-12|Productivity and Capital — Over decades, what makes a country richer is not the business cycle but growth in productivity, the output each worker produces in an hour, which rises mainly through more and better capital.
eco|16|l31-12|11-12|Education and Technology — Human capital and new technology are what keep productivity rising after simple additions of machines run out, and they are the main reason living standards keep climbing over generations.
eco|16|l32-1|11-12|Fiscal Policy Defined — Fiscal policy is the use of federal taxes and spending to influence the whole economy, and it is made by Congress and the President through law.
eco|16|l32-2|11-12|Expansionary and Contractionary Policy — Expansionary fiscal policy raises spending or cuts taxes to fight a recession; contractionary policy cuts spending or raises taxes to cool an overheating economy.
eco|16|l32-3|11-12|Automatic Stabilizers — Automatic stabilizers, such as unemployment insurance and progressive income taxes, push the budget toward deficit in recessions and toward surplus in booms without any new law.
eco|16|l32-4|11-12|The Spending Multiplier — Because one person's spending is another's income, a new dollar of government spending can raise total income by more than a dollar; the simple multiplier is 1 ÷ (1 − MPC).
eco|16|l32-5|11-12|Tax Cuts, Transfers and Crowding Out — Tax cuts and transfer payments usually raise spending less than direct government purchases, and government borrowing can crowd out some private investment.
eco|16|l32-6|11-12|Lags and Politics — Fiscal policy acts with delays: time to see a problem, time to pass a law, and time for the money to reach the economy, and politics shapes each step.
eco|16|l32-7|11-12|Where the Money Comes From — Most federal revenue comes from individual income taxes and payroll taxes; corporate income taxes and smaller sources supply the rest.
eco|16|l32-8|11-12|Where the Money Goes — Most federal spending is mandatory, set by existing law for programs like Social Security and Medicare, plus interest on the debt; only about a quarter is decided each year.
eco|16|l32-9|11-12|Deficits and the National Debt — A deficit is one year's gap between spending and revenue; the national debt is the total borrowed over all the years that has not been paid back.
eco|16|l32-10|11-12|The Debate Over Debt — Economists agree that some borrowing is useful and that debt growing faster than the economy forever is not; they disagree about how much is too much and how fast to reduce it.
eco|16|l32-11|11-12|Balanced Budgets in the States — Almost every state must balance its operating budget each year, so states often cut spending or raise taxes in recessions, working against federal stimulus.
