eco|16|l32-12|11-12|Illinois's Budget — Illinois funds its budget mainly with a flat income tax, sales taxes and federal aid, and its biggest long-run challenge is paying for pensions it promised in the past.
eco|17|l33-1|11-12|The Three Jobs of Money — Anything that works as a medium of exchange, a unit of account and a store of value is money, whatever it is made of.
eco|17|l33-2|11-12|Commodity, Representative and Fiat — Money has taken three forms in history: a valuable thing itself, a claim on a valuable thing, and a token that is money because the government says so and people accept it.
eco|17|l33-3|11-12|What Makes Money Good — Good money is durable, portable, divisible, uniform, limited in supply and widely accepted; the dollar's supply is limited by the Federal Reserve, not by a metal.
eco|17|l33-4|11-12|Deposits, Loans and Reserves — A bank takes deposits, keeps a fraction as reserves, lends out the rest, and earns the gap between the interest it charges and the interest it pays.
eco|17|l33-5|11-12|How Loans Create Money — When banks lend out deposits and the loans are redeposited, the money supply grows by up to 1 divided by the reserve ratio; this is the money multiplier.
eco|17|l33-6|11-12|Bank Runs and the FDIC — Because banks hold only a fraction of deposits, a loss of trust can cause a run; federal deposit insurance up to $250,000 per depositor removes the reason to run.
eco|17|l33-7|11-12|Simple Interest — Simple interest equals principal times rate times time, and it is charged only on the original amount borrowed.
eco|17|l33-8|11-12|Compound Interest — Compound interest pays interest on past interest, so a balance grows faster and faster; time matters more than almost anything else.
eco|17|l33-9|11-12|APR and Your Credit Score — The APR lets you compare loans on one yearly scale, and your credit score, built mostly from paying on time and owing little, sets which APR you are offered.
eco|17|l33-10|11-12|Saving Versus Investing — Saving keeps money safe and available for near-term needs; investing accepts risk in exchange for a higher expected return over long periods.
eco|17|l33-11|11-12|Putting It All Together — Money, banks, interest and investing form one system: savers supply funds, banks and markets move them to borrowers, and interest rates are the price that balances the two.
eco|17|l34-1|11-12|Why the Fed Exists — Congress created the Federal Reserve in 1913 to stop banking panics, and today it runs monetary policy, supervises banks, keeps payments moving and serves as the bank for banks.
eco|17|l34-2|11-12|Governors, Banks and the FOMC — The Fed has a seven-member Board of Governors in Washington, twelve regional Reserve Banks including Chicago, and a twelve-vote committee, the FOMC, that sets monetary policy.
eco|17|l34-3|11-12|Two Goals and One Number — Congress gave the Fed a dual mandate, maximum employment and stable prices, and since 2012 the Fed has defined stable prices as 2% inflation a year.
eco|17|l34-4|11-12|The Federal Funds Rate — The FOMC sets a target range for the federal funds rate, the overnight rate banks charge each other, and that rate anchors other short-term interest rates.
eco|17|l34-5|11-12|Paying Interest on Reserves — Since 2008 the Fed has paid interest on the reserves banks hold, and moving that rate up or down is now its main way of steering the federal funds rate.
eco|17|l34-6|11-12|Open Market Operations — When the Fed buys government bonds it adds reserves to the banking system and pushes interest rates down; when it sells or lets bonds run off, it does the reverse.
eco|17|l34-7|11-12|The Discount Window and Reserve Rules — Banks can borrow directly from the Fed at the discount rate, and the Fed once required banks to hold a set share of deposits as reserves, a tool it set to zero in 2020.
eco|17|l34-8|11-12|How a Rate Change Travels — A change in the Fed's target moves bank funding costs first, then card and loan rates, then spending, hiring and prices, with each step weaker and slower than the last.
eco|17|l34-9|11-12|Easing and Tightening — The Fed lowers rates to support spending and jobs when the economy is weak and raises them to cool spending when inflation runs above target; what matters is the real rate after inflation.
eco|17|l34-10|11-12|Lags, Limits and Shocks — Monetary policy works with long delays, runs out of room when rates hit zero, and faces hard choices when supply shocks push inflation up and output down together.
eco|17|l34-11|11-12|The Fed in 2008 and 2020 — In the 2008 financial crisis and the 2020 pandemic, the Fed cut rates to near zero, bought trillions in bonds and lent far beyond banks, and economists still debate the costs.
eco|17|l34-12|11-12|Independence and Accountability — Congress shielded the Fed from day-to-day politics so it can make unpopular choices, while requiring it to explain itself in public and to answer to Congress.
eco|18|l35-1|11-12|Absolute Advantage — A producer has an absolute advantage when it can make more of a good with the same resources than another producer can.
eco|18|l35-2|11-12|Comparative Advantage — A producer has a comparative advantage in the good it can make at the lowest opportunity cost, and that is what decides who should make what.
eco|18|l35-3|11-12|Gains From Trade — When each country specializes in its comparative advantage and trades, both end up with more than they could make alone.
eco|18|l35-4|11-12|Exports, Imports and the Balance — Exports are goods and services sold to other countries, imports are those bought from them, and the trade balance is exports minus imports.
eco|18|l35-5|11-12|What a Trade Deficit Means — A trade deficit means a country buys more from abroad than it sells, and the difference is matched by foreign investment flowing in; it is not a scoreboard of winning or losing.
eco|18|l35-6|11-12|Illinois in the World Market — Illinois sells farm products, machinery and chemicals to the world and depends on rivers, rails and ports to do it.
eco|18|l35-7|11-12|How a Tariff Works — A tariff raises the price of imports, which helps domestic producers and the treasury but costs consumers more than those two gain.
eco|18|l35-8|11-12|Quotas and Other Barriers — A quota limits the quantity imported instead of taxing it, raising the price like a tariff but sending the extra money to whoever holds the import rights.
eco|18|l35-9|11-12|The Case for Protection — Supporters of trade barriers argue they protect new industries, national security and jobs, and some of these arguments have real weight in particular cases.
eco|18|l35-10|11-12|The Case Against Protection — Opponents of trade barriers point to higher prices for consumers, costs to industries that use imports, retaliation by other countries and the history of tariff wars.
eco|18|l35-11|11-12|Trade Agreements — Trade agreements are treaties in which countries lower barriers together, so each side gets market access in exchange for giving it.
eco|18|l35-12|11-12|The World Trade Organization — The WTO is the forum where most countries set shared trade rules and settle disputes, with real influence but limited power to enforce.
eco|18|l36-1|11-12|What an Exchange Rate Is — An exchange rate is the price of one currency in terms of another, and when the dollar strengthens, foreign goods and trips get cheaper for Americans.
eco|18|l36-2|11-12|Exporters and Importers — A stronger dollar helps American importers and consumers of foreign goods but hurts American exporters, and a weaker dollar does the reverse.
eco|18|l36-3|11-12|Why Exchange Rates Move — A currency's price is set by supply and demand in the foreign exchange market, driven by trade, interest rates and expectations.
eco|18|l36-4|11-12|Progressive, Regressive, Proportional — A tax is progressive, regressive or proportional depending on whether the share of income it takes rises, falls or stays the same as income rises.
eco|18|l36-5|11-12|Income and Payroll Taxes — Income taxes fund general government and rise with income in steps; payroll taxes fund Social Security and Medicare and come straight out of each paycheck.
eco|18|l36-6|11-12|Sales and Property Taxes — Sales taxes fund states and cities through spending, and property taxes fund schools and local services through the value of land and buildings.
eco|18|l36-7|11-12|Measuring Development — Economists compare countries by GDP per capita adjusted for prices, plus health and schooling, and the gaps between rich and poor countries are enormous.
eco|18|l36-8|11-12|Why Some Countries Grow — Countries grow when they build capital, educate and keep healthy their workers, adopt technology and trade, and the institutions that protect property and enforce contracts make all of that possible.
eco|18|l36-9|11-12|Poverty and Inequality — Poverty is measured against a line of minimum income and inequality by how income is spread across groups, and they are different problems with different remedies.
eco|18|l36-10|11-12|The Course in One Page — Every topic in this course comes back to a few habits: scarcity forces choices, choices have opportunity costs, people respond to incentives, and markets coordinate through prices.
eco|18|l36-11|11-12|Applying the Model — Analyzing a real decision means naming the opportunity cost, tracing incentives, checking which way prices move and stating the trade-off before choosing.
eco|2|l3-1|k-2|A Trade Needs Two Yeses — A trade happens when both people agree to swap.
eco|2|l3-2|k-2|When Trading Gets Stuck — Trading is hard when the other person does not want what you have.
eco|2|l3-3|k-2|Why We Use Money — Money is something everyone agrees to take in a trade.
eco|2|l3-4|k-2|Prices Tell the Cost — A price tells how much money a thing costs.
eco|2|l3-5|k-2|Coins and What They Are Worth — Each coin is worth a set number of cents.
eco|2|l3-6|k-2|Paying and Getting Change — If you pay more than the price, you get change back.
eco|2|l3-7|k-2|Earning Money by Working — People earn money by doing work.
eco|2|l4-1|k-2|What Saving Means — Saving means keeping money now to use later.
eco|2|l4-2|k-2|Making a Saving Plan — A plan tells you how many weeks to save.
eco|2|l4-3|k-2|Spending Money — Spending means using money to buy something.
eco|2|l4-4|k-2|Save or Spend? You Choose — When you spend now, you give up saving for later.
eco|2|l4-5|k-2|Sharing to Help Others — Sharing means giving some money to help others.
eco|2|l4-6|k-2|A Bank Keeps Money Safe — A bank is a safe place to keep money.
eco|3|l5-1|k-2|Producers Make Things — A producer makes a good or gives a service.
eco|3|l5-2|k-2|Consumers Use Things — A consumer buys or uses goods and services.
eco|3|l5-3|k-2|Farmer, Mill and Bakery — Bread is made in steps, and each step has a worker.
eco|3|l5-4|k-2|Trucks, Trains and Stores — Trucks and trains carry goods, and stores sell them.
eco|3|l5-5|k-2|Illinois Farms — Illinois farms grow a lot of corn and soybeans.
eco|3|l5-6|k-2|You Can Be Both — You are a consumer every day and a producer sometimes.
eco|3|l6-1|k-2|Buyers and Sellers Meet — A market is any place where buyers and sellers meet.
eco|3|l6-2|k-2|Setting a Price — A seller sets a price, and a buyer decides to buy or not.
eco|3|l6-3|k-2|When Nobody Buys — If nobody buys, the seller may lower the price.
eco|3|l6-4|k-2|When Everyone Wants It — When many buyers want something, it can sell out.
eco|3|l6-5|k-2|Making Things to Sell — To sell at a market, you first make a product.
eco|3|l6-6|k-2|Counting and Fair Rules — Fair markets have honest signs, one price for all, and turns.
eco|4|l7-1|3-5|What Scarcity Means — Scarcity means our wants are bigger than the things we have to meet them.
eco|4|l7-2|3-5|Needs and Wants — Needs keep us alive and safe; wants make life nicer, and both must fit what we have.
eco|4|l7-3|3-5|Scarcity Everywhere — Families, schools and towns all face scarcity, so they all have to make choices.
eco|4|l7-4|3-5|Natural Resources — Natural resources are gifts of nature, like soil, water and trees, that we use to make things.
eco|4|l7-5|3-5|Human Resources — Human resources are the work and skills people bring to making goods and services.
eco|4|l7-6|3-5|Capital Resources — Capital resources are tools, machines and buildings made by people to make other things.
eco|4|l7-7|3-5|The Next-Best Thing — The opportunity cost of a choice is the next-best thing you give up.
eco|4|l7-8|3-5|Using a Decision Grid — A decision grid helps you compare choices by what matters to you.
eco|4|l7-9|3-5|Incentives Change Choices — Incentives are rewards or costs that make people more or less likely to do something.
eco|4|l8-1|3-5|Producers and Consumers — Producers make goods and services; consumers buy and use them.
eco|4|l8-2|3-5|Inside a Pizza Shop — A pizza shop uses natural, human and capital resources to make one pizza.
eco|4|l8-3|3-5|Starting a Business — An entrepreneur starts a business, takes a risk and hopes to earn a profit.
eco|4|l8-4|3-5|Specialization — Specialization means doing one kind of job and getting very good at it.
eco|4|l8-5|3-5|The Airplane Assembly Line — Dividing work into steps, like an assembly line, often makes more goods in the same time.
eco|4|l8-6|3-5|When Splitting Work Is Hard — The division of labor helps, but jobs can get boring and one slow step can hold up the line.
eco|4|l8-7|3-5|We Depend on Each Other — Because people specialize, they depend on each other for most of what they need.
eco|4|l8-8|3-5|A Kid Business — A small kid business uses resources, specialization and a plan to earn a profit.
eco|4|l8-9|3-5|Making More With Less — Better tools, skills and teamwork help people make more in the same amount of time.
eco|5|l9-1|3-5|What Is a Market? — A market is any place where buyers and sellers meet to trade goods or services.
eco|5|l9-2|3-5|Demand: What Buyers Want — Demand is how much people want to buy at each price; when the price goes down, people buy more.
eco|5|l9-3|3-5|Supply: What Sellers Offer — Supply is how much sellers will offer at each price; when the price goes up, sellers offer more.
eco|5|l9-4|3-5|When Many Want and Few Are for Sale — When many people want something and only a few are for sale, the price goes up.
eco|5|l9-5|3-5|When There Is Too Much — When there is more for sale than people want, the price goes down.
eco|5|l9-6|3-5|A Hot Day and a Cold Day — When something changes how much people want, like the weather, the price can change too.
eco|5|l9-7|3-5|More Sellers, Better Deals — Competition among sellers leads to lower prices, better quality and better service for buyers.
eco|5|l9-8|3-5|A Price Is a Signal — A price is a signal that tells buyers and sellers what to do.
eco|5|l9-9|3-5|Where the Price Settles — The market price settles where the amount buyers want equals the amount sellers offer.
eco|5|l10-1|3-5|Trading Without Money — Barter is trading one thing for another without money, and it only works when both people want what the other has.
eco|5|l10-2|3-5|Three Jobs of Money — Money does three jobs: it helps us trade, it measures what things are worth, and it lets us save.
eco|5|l10-3|3-5|Coins, Bills and Bank Accounts — Money can be coins, paper bills or numbers in a bank account, and all of them can pay for things.
eco|5|l10-4|3-5|Keeping Money Safe — A bank keeps people's deposits safe, and the government insures bank deposits up to $250,000.
eco|5|l10-5|3-5|Banks Lend Money — Banks lend some of the money people deposit to people and businesses who need to borrow.
eco|5|l10-6|3-5|Getting Paid to Save — Interest is extra money a bank pays you for keeping your savings there.
eco|5|l10-7|3-5|Paying to Borrow — When you borrow money, you pay it back plus interest, so borrowing costs more than paying cash.
eco|5|l10-8|3-5|Income, Spending and Saving — Income is money you get; you can spend it, save it or share it.
eco|5|l10-9|3-5|Making a Budget — A budget is a plan for how to use your income: how much to spend, save and share.
eco|6|l11-1|3-5|Nobody Makes Everything — People trade because nobody can make everything they need by themselves.
eco|6|l11-2|3-5|Doing What You Do Best — When people focus on what they do best and trade, they can both end up with more.
eco|6|l11-3|3-5|Imports and Exports — Exports are things a place sells to other places; imports are things it buys from them.
eco|6|l11-4|3-5|What Illinois Sells — Illinois sells crops like corn and soybeans and machines like tractors to places all over the world.
eco|6|l11-5|3-5|What Illinois Buys — Illinois imports goods it cannot make well or cheaply, like fruit in winter, phones and clothing.
eco|6|l11-6|3-5|Chicago Moves the Goods — Chicago's trains, airport and port help move goods between Illinois and the world.
eco|6|l11-7|3-5|The Backpack Map — Tags and labels show that the things we use come from many places.
eco|6|l11-8|3-5|Depending on Each Other — Trade makes people and countries depend on each other, which brings more goods but also some risk.
eco|6|l11-9|3-5|Fair Rules for Trade — Trade is fair when both sides agree freely and follow honest rules; people disagree about how much to limit it.
eco|6|l12-1|3-5|Money We Pay Together — A tax is money people and businesses must pay to the government to pay for shared services.
eco|6|l12-2|3-5|What Taxes Pay For — Tax money pays for schools, roads, parks, police, firefighters, libraries and more.
eco|6|l12-3|3-5|Who Decides on Taxes — Leaders chosen by voters decide on taxes at the city, state and national levels.
eco|6|l12-4|3-5|Public Goods and Private Goods — Public goods are shared by everyone; private goods are bought and used by one person or family.
eco|6|l12-5|3-5|People Who Work for Us All — Government workers are paid with taxes to provide services the whole community uses.
eco|6|l12-6|3-5|Volunteers and Nonprofits — Volunteers and nonprofit groups help a community without trying to make a profit.
eco|6|l12-7|3-5|Why Pay Is Different — What people earn depends on their skills, their education and how many people want that work done.
eco|6|l12-8|3-5|Learning Pays Off — More education and training often lead to more skills and more job choices.
eco|6|l12-9|3-5|Planning for a Job You Want — You can plan for a career by learning what a job needs and building those skills step by step.
eco|7|l13-1|6-8|Scarcity Starts Everything — Scarcity means our wants are bigger than the time, money and resources we have, so we must choose.
eco|7|l13-2|6-8|The Four Factors of Production — Everything is made with land, labor, capital and entrepreneurship, and all four are scarce.
eco|7|l13-3|6-8|Needs, Wants and Choices — Economists treat needs and wants alike as choices, because even basic needs come in many forms with different costs.
eco|7|l13-4|6-8|Opportunity Cost — The opportunity cost of a choice is the value of the next-best thing you give up.
eco|7|l13-5|6-8|Thinking at the Margin — Most choices are about one more or one less, so compare the extra benefit with the extra cost.
eco|7|l13-6|6-8|Weighing Costs and Benefits — Cost-benefit analysis lists the costs and benefits of each option, puts numbers on them where you can, and compares.
eco|7|l13-7|6-8|How Incentives Change Choices — Incentives are rewards and costs that push people toward or away from a choice.
eco|7|l13-8|6-8|When Incentives Surprise Us — Incentives can lead to results nobody planned, so good rules think about how people will really respond.
eco|7|l13-9|6-8|Families, Schools and Towns Choose — Groups face scarcity too: a family, a school or a town must give up one use of its money to fund another.
eco|7|l13-10|6-8|Choosing Like an Economist — Good choices come from naming the options, finding the opportunity cost, thinking at the margin and checking the incentives.
eco|7|l14-1|6-8|What, How and For Whom — Because of scarcity, every society must decide what to produce, how to produce it and for whom.
eco|7|l14-2|6-8|Traditional Economies — In a traditional economy, custom and the ways of past generations answer the economic questions.
eco|7|l14-3|6-8|Command Economies — In a command economy, the government owns most resources and decides what, how and for whom.
eco|7|l14-4|6-8|Market Economies — In a market economy, individuals and businesses answer the economic questions by buying and selling freely.
eco|7|l14-5|6-8|Mixed Economies — Almost every country today is a mixed economy that combines markets with some government decisions.
eco|7|l14-6|6-8|The U.S. Mixed Market Economy — The United States is a mixed economy that leans toward the market side.
eco|7|l14-7|6-8|What Government Does — In a market economy, government sets rules, provides public goods and runs safety nets.
eco|7|l14-8|6-8|Property Rights — Property rights give people the right to own and use things, which gives them a reason to take care of them and invest.
eco|7|l14-9|6-8|Two Koreas, One Peninsula — North and South Korea began with similar land and people but chose different systems, and their economies grew very differently.
eco|7|l14-10|6-8|How Systems Change — Economic systems change over time, and in recent decades many command economies have moved toward markets.
eco|8|l15-1|6-8|The Law of Demand — When the price of a good goes up, people buy less of it; when the price goes down, they buy more.
eco|8|l15-2|6-8|Reading a Demand Schedule — A demand schedule is a table that shows how many units buyers want at each price.
eco|8|l15-3|6-8|What Shifts Demand — A change in income, tastes, related goods or the number of buyers moves the whole demand curve.
eco|8|l15-4|6-8|Shift or Slide? — A change in the good's own price slides along the curve; a change in anything else shifts the curve.
eco|8|l15-5|6-8|The Law of Supply — When the price of a good goes up, sellers offer more of it; when the price goes down, they offer less.
eco|8|l15-6|6-8|A Supply Schedule — A supply schedule is a table that shows how many units sellers offer at each price.
eco|8|l15-7|6-8|What Shifts Supply — A change in costs, technology or the number of sellers moves the whole supply curve.
eco|8|l15-8|6-8|Finding Equilibrium — The market price settles where the quantity demanded equals the quantity supplied.
eco|8|l15-9|6-8|Surplus and Shortage — A price above equilibrium makes a surplus; a price below equilibrium makes a shortage.
eco|8|l15-10|6-8|Real Prices, Explained — To explain a price change, ask which curve shifted, which way, and what that does to the price.
eco|8|l16-1|6-8|Revenue and Costs — Revenue is the money a business takes in; costs are the money it spends to run.
eco|8|l16-2|6-8|Profit and Loss — Profit is revenue minus costs; if costs are bigger than revenue, the business has a loss.
eco|8|l16-3|6-8|Pricing for Profit — A higher price does not always mean more revenue, because buyers purchase fewer units.
eco|8|l16-4|6-8|One Owner or Partners — A sole proprietorship has one owner; a partnership has two or more who share the business.
eco|8|l16-5|6-8|Corporations — A corporation is a business that is its own legal unit, owned by shareholders who risk only what they invested.
eco|8|l16-6|6-8|Why More Sellers Help Buyers — Competition among many sellers usually pushes prices down and quality up.
eco|8|l16-7|6-8|When There Is Only One Seller — A monopoly is a market with only one seller, which can charge more and has less reason to improve.
eco|8|l16-8|6-8|Keeping Markets Fair — Governments use antitrust laws to stop businesses from blocking competition.
eco|8|l16-9|6-8|Entrepreneurs Take the Risk — An entrepreneur starts a business, takes on risk and earns profit as the reward if it works.
eco|8|l16-10|6-8|A Plan for the School Store — A business plan lists what you will sell, to whom, at what price, and how you expect to earn a profit.
eco|8|l16-11|6-8|Workers, Wages and Unions — Wages are set mostly by the supply of and demand for workers; unions let workers bargain as a group.
eco|9|l17-1|6-8|Ways People Earn Income — Income is money you receive, most often from work, and it comes in several forms.
eco|9|l17-2|6-8|Why Some Jobs Pay More — Jobs that need rare skills or training, or that few people want to do, usually pay more.
eco|9|l17-3|6-8|Reading a Pay Stub — Gross pay is what you earn; net pay is what is left after taxes and deductions.
eco|9|l17-4|6-8|Needs, Wants and Trade-Offs — Every dollar can be spent only once, so a budget means choosing among needs and wants.
eco|9|l17-5|6-8|Building a Monthly Budget — A budget is a plan that lists income, then spending and saving, so money in covers money out.
eco|9|l17-6|6-8|The 50/30/20 Idea — One simple guide splits net income into 50% needs, 30% wants and 20% savings.
eco|9|l17-7|6-8|Saving Goals and Emergency Funds — Saving works best with a clear goal, and an emergency fund protects you from surprise costs.
eco|9|l17-8|6-8|Simple and Compound Interest — Simple interest is paid only on the starting amount; compound interest is also paid on interest already earned.
eco|9|l17-9|6-8|Why Starting Early Matters — Because of compounding, money saved early has more years to grow than money saved later.
eco|9|l17-10|6-8|Where to Keep Your Savings — Banks and credit unions keep savings safe, and federal insurance protects deposits up to $250,000.
eco|9|l18-1|6-8|Credit: Buy Now, Pay Later — Credit lets you use money now and pay it back later, usually with interest.
eco|9|l18-2|6-8|Debit Cards and Credit Cards — A debit card spends your own money; a credit card spends borrowed money that you must repay.
eco|9|l18-3|6-8|APR in Plain Words — APR is the yearly cost of borrowing shown as a percent, and a higher APR means a more expensive loan.
eco|9|l18-4|6-8|What a Credit Score Is — A credit score is a number that shows lenders how likely you are to repay on time.
eco|9|l18-5|6-8|What Changes Your Score — Paying on time and keeping balances low are the biggest ways to build a good credit score.
eco|9|l18-6|6-8|Loans for Big Things — Big purchases like cars, college and homes often use loans that are repaid in monthly payments over years.
eco|9|l18-7|6-8|Spotting Scams and Phishing — Scammers create pressure and ask for private information, so slow down and check before you act.
eco|9|l18-8|6-8|Guarding Against Identity Theft — Identity theft is when someone uses your personal information, and simple habits make it much harder.
eco|9|l18-9|6-8|What Insurance Is For — Insurance lets many people pay small amounts so that no one has to face a huge loss alone.
eco|9|l18-10|6-8|Comparing Prices — Compare the unit price and the total cost, not just the sticker price or the monthly payment.
eco|9|l18-11|6-8|Reading a Contract — A contract is a promise that the law can enforce, so read it and ask questions before you sign.
spa|1|l1-1|k-2|The Sound of A — In Spanish, the letter a always says ah, like in mamá.
spa|1|l1-2|k-2|E, I, O and U — The five vowels say ah, eh, ee, oh, oo, and they never change.
spa|1|l1-3|k-2|Say Every Letter — Spanish words sound the way they are spelled.
spa|1|l1-4|k-2|Watch Out for English — Do not use English vowel sounds when you read Spanish.
spa|1|l1-5|k-2|Clapping Syllables — Clap once for each vowel sound to find the parts of a word.
spa|1|l1-6|k-2|Hola and Adiós — Say hola to say hi and adiós to say goodbye.
spa|1|l2-1|k-2|H Is Quiet — In Spanish, the letter h makes no sound at all.
spa|1|l2-2|k-2|J Is a Strong H — The Spanish j sounds like a strong, breathy h.
spa|1|l2-3|k-2|LL Sounds Like Y — The double l in Spanish sounds like the y in yes.
spa|1|l2-4|k-2|The Letter Ñ — The letter ñ has a squiggle on top and says ny, like in canyon.
spa|1|l2-5|k-2|The Rolled RR — The double rr is a rolled sound, like a little motor.
spa|1|l2-6|k-2|Pero or Perro? — One r is a quick tap, and rr is a long roll.
spa|1|l2-7|k-2|Read a New Word — You can say a Spanish word before you know what it means.
spa|1|l2-8|k-2|The Alphabet Song — The Spanish alphabet has 27 letters, and ñ is one of them.
spa|10|l19-1|6-8|Zero to Fifteen — The numbers cero to quince are single words you learn one by one; they are the base for everything bigger.
spa|10|l19-2|6-8|Sixteen to Twenty-Nine — From dieciséis to veintinueve, Spanish glues ten or twenty to a small number and writes it as one word.
spa|10|l19-3|6-8|The Tens to One Hundred — From treinta on, Spanish says the tens and the ones as separate words joined by y: treinta y uno.
spa|10|l19-4|6-8|Cien or Ciento — Say cien for exactly one hundred and ciento when more follows, as in ciento uno.
spa|10|l19-5|6-8|Two Hundred to Nine Hundred — Most hundreds are a number plus -cientos, but quinientos, setecientos and novecientos change shape.
spa|10|l19-6|6-8|Mil and Big Numbers — Mil means one thousand; say mil, not un mil, and build bigger numbers from left to right.
spa|10|l19-7|6-8|Numbers That Agree — Uno and the hundreds change for feminine nouns: una mesa, doscientas sillas.
spa|10|l19-8|6-8|Asking How Much — Ask ¿Cuánto cuesta? for one thing and ¿Cuánto cuestan? for more than one.
spa|10|l19-9|6-8|Phone Numbers — Spanish speakers often read phone numbers in pairs of digits, so learn to hear numbers like 45 and 87 fast.
spa|10|l19-10|6-8|Saying Your Age — Spanish uses tener for age: Tengo doce años means I am twelve years old.
spa|10|l19-11|6-8|Math Out Loud — Say math in Spanish with más (plus), menos (minus), por (times) and son (equals).
spa|10|l20-1|6-8|What Time Is It? — Ask ¿Qué hora es? and answer Es la una for one o'clock and Son las … for every other hour.
spa|10|l20-2|6-8|Quarter and Half Past — Add minutes after the hour with y: y cuarto is quarter past and y media is half past.
spa|10|l20-3|6-8|Minutes Before the Hour — For times close to the next hour, Spanish often names the next hour and subtracts with menos: las cinco menos cuarto is 4:45.
spa|10|l20-4|6-8|Morning, Afternoon and Night — Spanish marks a.m. and p.m. with de la mañana, de la tarde and de la noche, and asks when with ¿A qué hora…?
spa|10|l20-5|6-8|The Days of the Week — The days are lowercase in Spanish, and el lunes means on Monday.
spa|10|l20-6|6-8|The Months of the Year — The twelve months look like English but are lowercase in Spanish: enero, febrero, marzo…
spa|10|l20-7|6-8|Writing the Date — Spanish says the day before the month: el quince de septiembre, written 15/9.
spa|10|l20-8|6-8|When Is Your Birthday? — Ask ¿Cuándo es tu cumpleaños? and answer Mi cumpleaños es el … de …
spa|10|l20-9|6-8|Día de los Muertos — Día de los Muertos, on November 1 and 2, is a Mexican tradition that honors family members who have died.
spa|10|l20-10|6-8|Independence Days — Mexico celebrates independence on September 16; in Spanish, the date is el dieciséis de septiembre.
spa|11|l21-1|6-8|Masculine and Feminine Nouns — Every Spanish noun is either masculine or feminine, even nouns for objects like tables and books.
spa|11|l21-2|6-8|The Four Words for The — Spanish has four words for the: el, la, los and las, chosen by the noun's gender and number.
spa|11|l21-3|6-8|A, An and Some — Un and una mean a or an, and unos and unas mean some; they match gender and number too.
spa|11|l21-4|6-8|The -o and -a Pattern — Most nouns ending in -o are masculine, and most nouns ending in -a are feminine.
spa|11|l21-5|6-8|Endings in -ción and -dad — Nouns ending in -ción, -sión, -dad and -tad are feminine.
spa|11|l21-6|6-8|El Problema and El Mapa — Many nouns ending in -ma that come from Greek, plus el mapa, are masculine even though they end in -a.
spa|11|l21-7|6-8|El Día and La Mano — Two common nouns break the pattern: el día is masculine, and la mano is feminine.
spa|11|l21-8|6-8|Nouns for People — Nouns for people often change their ending to match the person: el alumno, la alumna.
spa|11|l21-9|6-8|Add -s or -es — Add -s to nouns ending in a vowel and -es to nouns ending in a consonant.
spa|11|l21-10|6-8|When Z Turns to C — A noun ending in -z changes the z to c before adding -es: el lápiz, los lápices.
spa|11|l21-11|6-8|Making Every Word Match — In Spanish, the article, the noun and any describing word all match in gender and number.
spa|11|l22-1|6-8|One Word: Hay — Hay means both there is and there are, and it never changes form.
spa|11|l22-2|6-8|Hay With Un, Una or Numbers — After hay, use un, una, a number or no article, but not el or la.
spa|11|l22-3|6-8|Asking ¿Qué hay en…? — Ask ¿Qué hay en…? to find out what is in a place, and ¿Cuántos hay? to ask how many.
spa|11|l22-4|6-8|Necesito: I Need — Necesito means I need; it can come before a noun or before a verb in its base form.
spa|11|l22-5|6-8|Tengo: I Have — Tengo means I have; tener is irregular: tengo, tienes, tiene.
spa|11|l22-6|6-8|The Supply List — Learn school supplies with their articles so you can ask for, buy and borrow them.
spa|11|l22-7|6-8|School Subjects — School subjects are nouns with articles, and many look like their English names.
spa|11|l22-8|6-8|When Is the Class? — Say a la una or a las plus a number to tell what time a class starts.
spa|11|l22-9|6-8|Commands You Will Hear — Teachers give class commands in the ustedes form, which often ends in -en or -an.
spa|11|l22-10|6-8|Asking for Help — Use short, polite questions like ¿Puede repetir, por favor? to keep learning when you are lost.
spa|12|l23-1|6-8|All the Forms of Ser — Ser is irregular: soy, eres, es, somos, sois, son, and you must memorize these forms.
spa|12|l23-2|6-8|All the Forms of Estar — Estar is also irregular: estoy, estás, está, estamos, estáis, están, with accents on most forms.
spa|12|l23-3|6-8|Why Spanish Has Two — Ser tells what something is; estar tells where it is or how it is right now.
spa|12|l23-4|6-8|Identity and Profession — Use ser to say who someone is, what they are, and what job they have.
spa|12|l23-5|6-8|Origin and Nationality — Use ser de to say where someone or something is from, and ser with nationality words.
spa|12|l23-6|6-8|Traits and Characteristics — Use ser with adjectives that describe what someone or something is like in general.
spa|12|l23-7|6-8|Time, Dates and Events — Use ser to tell time, give dates and days, and say where an event takes place.
spa|12|l23-8|6-8|Location of People and Things — Use estar to say where a person or object is located.
spa|12|l23-9|6-8|Feelings and Moods — Use estar for emotions and moods: how someone feels right now.
spa|12|l23-10|6-8|Conditions and Health — Use estar for physical conditions: sick, tired, broken, open, clean, full.
spa|12|l23-11|6-8|Aburrido and Listo — Some adjectives change meaning with ser or estar: es aburrido is boring, está aburrido is bored.
spa|12|l23-12|6-8|Rico, Malo and Verde — More pairs: ser rico is rich, estar rico is tasty; ser malo is bad, estar malo is sick; ser verde is green, estar verde is unripe.
spa|12|l23-13|6-8|Common Errors and a Final Test — Check ser or estar by asking: is this identity, origin, trait, time or event, or is it location, feeling or condition?
spa|12|l24-1|6-8|Gender Agreement: -o and -a — An adjective ending in -o changes to -a to match a feminine noun: el gato negro, la gata negra.
spa|12|l24-2|6-8|Number Agreement: Plurals — Plural nouns take plural adjectives: add -s after a vowel and -es after a consonant.
spa|12|l24-3|6-8|Adjectives Ending in -e or a Consonant — Adjectives ending in -e or most consonants have one form for both genders: inteligente, fácil.
spa|12|l24-4|6-8|Where Adjectives Go — Most describing adjectives go after the noun; number words and a few like buen and gran go before.
spa|12|l24-5|6-8|Physical Description — Describe looks with ser plus an adjective and tener plus hair or eyes: Es alto. Tiene el pelo negro.
spa|12|l24-6|6-8|Personality — Personality traits use ser and agree with the person: Es generosa, son divertidos.
spa|12|l24-7|6-8|Describing a Family Member — A good description combines who the person is, looks, personality and a detail, each with the right verb and ending.
spa|12|l24-8|6-8|More Than and Less Than — To compare, use más + adjective + que or menos + adjective + que, with the adjective agreeing.
spa|12|l24-9|6-8|Irregular Comparisons — Four common comparisons are irregular: mejor, peor, mayor and menor.
spa|12|l24-10|6-8|Describing a Famous Person — Describe a famous person with facts you know are true: identity, origin, traits and a comparison.
spa|13|l25-1|9-10|What an Infinitive Is — Every Spanish verb has a base form called the infinitive, which ends in -ar, -er or -ir.
spa|13|l25-2|9-10|Finding the Stem — To conjugate a regular verb, drop the -ar, -er or -ir and keep the stem.
spa|13|l25-3|9-10|Subject Pronouns — Spanish has subject pronouns for I, you, he, she, we, you all and they, including both vosotros and ustedes.
spa|13|l25-4|9-10|Conjugating -ar Verbs — Regular -ar verbs take the endings -o, -as, -a, -amos, -áis, -an.
spa|13|l25-5|9-10|Conjugating -er Verbs — Regular -er verbs take the endings -o, -es, -e, -emos, -éis, -en.
spa|13|l25-6|9-10|Conjugating -ir Verbs — Regular -ir verbs share most -er endings but use -imos and -ís for nosotros and vosotros.
spa|13|l25-7|9-10|Choosing the Right Ending — Find the subject, find the verb family, then pick the one ending that fits both.
spa|13|l25-8|9-10|Dropping the Subject Pronoun — Because the ending already shows the subject, Spanish usually leaves out yo, tú and nosotros.
spa|13|l25-9|9-10|Habits and Right Now — The Spanish present tense covers both what you do regularly and what you are doing now.
spa|13|l25-10|9-10|Siempre, a Veces, Nunca — Frequency words like siempre, a veces and nunca tell how often, and nunca goes before the verb.
spa|13|l26-1|9-10|Verbs with -go in the Yo Form — Some common verbs add a g in the yo form, like hago, pongo, salgo and traigo, but stay regular elsewhere.
spa|13|l26-2|9-10|Tener, Venir and Decir — Tener, venir and decir have -go in the yo form and also change their stem in other forms.
spa|13|l26-3|9-10|Conozco, Doy and Veo — A few other verbs are irregular only in the yo form: conozco, sé, doy and veo.
spa|13|l26-4|9-10|Knowing Facts: Saber — Use saber for facts, information and knowing how to do something.
spa|13|l26-5|9-10|Knowing People and Places: Conocer — Use conocer for being familiar with people, places and things, and add a before a person.
spa|13|l26-6|9-10|Choosing Between Them — Ask yourself: is it a fact or skill (saber), or a person, place or first meeting (conocer)?
spa|13|l26-7|9-10|The Verb Ir — Ir (to go) is fully irregular: voy, vas, va, vamos, vais, van.
spa|13|l26-8|9-10|Ir a + Infinitive — To talk about the near future, use a form of ir, then a, then an infinitive: Voy a comer.
spa|13|l26-9|9-10|Planning a Weekend — Combine ir a, days of the week and yo-irregulars to describe weekend plans clearly.
spa|13|l26-10|9-10|Talking About a Trip — A short trip description uses ir, ir a, the yo-go verbs and saber or conocer together.
spa|14|l27-1|9-10|What a Stem Is — A verb's stem is what is left after you drop -ar, -er or -ir, and boot verbs change a vowel in that stem.
spa|14|l27-2|9-10|Drawing the Boot — In a stem-changing verb, yo, tú, él/ella/usted and ellos/ustedes change; nosotros and vosotros keep the original stem.
spa|14|l27-3|9-10|Endings Stay Regular — Boot verbs change only the stem; their endings are the same regular -ar, -er or -ir endings you already know.
spa|14|l27-4|9-10|E Becomes IE — Verbs like querer, pensar, empezar and preferir change the stem vowel e to ie inside the boot.
spa|14|l27-5|9-10|O Becomes UE — Verbs like poder, dormir, volver and almorzar change the stem vowel o to ue inside the boot.
spa|14|l27-6|9-10|Jugar: The Only U to UE — Jugar is the one common verb that changes u to ue: juego, juegas, juega, jugamos, jugáis, juegan.
spa|14|l27-7|9-10|E Becomes I — Some -ir verbs, like pedir, servir and repetir, change the stem vowel e to i inside the boot.
spa|14|l27-8|9-10|Tener: A Boot with a Twist — Tener is an e→ie verb with an irregular yo form: tengo, tienes, tiene, tenemos, tenéis, tienen.
spa|14|l27-9|9-10|Hungry, Thirsty, Cold, Hot — Spanish uses tener plus a noun for many feelings English expresses with to be: tengo hambre means I am hungry.
spa|14|l27-10|9-10|Fear, Being Right, Luck, Sleep — Tener miedo, tener razón, tener sueño, tener suerte and tener prisa also use tener where English uses to be.
spa|14|l28-1|9-10|The Thing Liked Is the Subject — In me gusta el mole, el mole is the subject; the sentence means mole is pleasing to me.
spa|14|l28-2|9-10|Gusta or Gustan — Use gusta with one thing or an infinitive, and gustan with more than one thing.
spa|14|l28-3|9-10|Saying No and Asking — Put no right before the pronoun to make a negative, and turn the sentence into a question with ¿ ? around it.
spa|14|l28-4|9-10|The Six Pronouns — The person who likes something is shown by me, te, le, nos, os or les before the verb.
spa|14|l28-5|9-10|A Mí, A Ti, A Ella — A phrase like a mí, a ti or a Abuela adds emphasis or makes clear who le or les refers to.
spa|14|l28-6|9-10|Me Too, Me Neither — Agree with a positive using a mí también, and with a negative using a mí tampoco.
spa|14|l28-7|9-10|Encantar: To Love Something — Encantar works exactly like gustar and means to love a thing or activity: me encanta el mole.
spa|14|l28-8|9-10|Interesar and Molestar — Interesar (to interest) and molestar (to bother) follow the gustar pattern: me interesa la historia; me molesta el ruido.
spa|14|l28-9|9-10|Faltar: What's Missing — Faltar means to be missing or lacking, and the missing thing is the subject: nos falta el postre.
spa|14|l28-10|9-10|Talking About Hobbies — Combine gustar, encantar and interesar with infinitives to describe hobbies and ask about someone else's.
spa|15|l29-1|9-10|A Finished Action in the Past — The preterite tells about an action that happened and ended at a specific time in the past.
spa|15|l29-2|9-10|Time Words That Signal the Past — Words like ayer, anoche and la semana pasada are clues that a preterite verb is coming.
spa|15|l29-3|9-10|Present or Preterite? — The ending of the verb, not the stem, tells you whether an action is happening now or already happened.
spa|15|l29-4|9-10|The Six -ar Endings — Regular -ar verbs use -é, -aste, -ó, -amos, -asteis and -aron in the preterite.
spa|15|l29-5|9-10|The Accent That Changes Meaning — Hablo means I speak, but habló means he or she spoke, so the written accent matters.
spa|15|l29-6|9-10|Nosotros: Same Form, Two Times — For -ar and -ir verbs, the nosotros form is the same in the present and the preterite, so context decides.
spa|15|l29-7|9-10|One Set of Endings for -er and -ir — Regular -er and -ir verbs share the preterite endings -í, -iste, -ió, -imos, -isteis and -ieron.
spa|15|l29-8|9-10|Salir, Volver and Aprender in Action — The same -er and -ir endings let you tell a whole evening: going out, learning, and coming back.
spa|15|l29-9|9-10|Asking and Answering About the Past — To answer a tú question in the preterite, switch the -aste or -iste ending to the yo ending -é or -í.
spa|15|l29-10|9-10|-car Verbs: Busqué and Toqué — Verbs ending in -car change c to qu in the yo preterite to keep the hard k sound: busqué, toqué.
spa|15|l29-11|9-10|-gar and -zar Verbs: Llegué and Empecé — In the yo preterite, -gar verbs write gué and -zar verbs write cé: llegué, jugué, empecé, almorcé.
spa|15|l30-1|9-10|Fui, Fuiste, Fue: Two Verbs Share — Ser (to be) and ir (to go) have the same preterite forms: fui, fuiste, fue, fuimos, fuisteis, fueron.
spa|15|l30-2|9-10|Telling Ser and Ir Apart — When you see fui or fue, look for a or a place to spot ir, and a description or noun to spot ser.
spa|15|l30-3|9-10|Dar and Ver: Short and Accent-Free — Dar uses -er/-ir endings (di, dio) and ver is regular (vi, vio), and none of their one-syllable forms take an accent.
spa|15|l30-4|9-10|Hacer: Hice and Hizo — Hacer uses the stem hic- and special endings: hice, hiciste, hizo, hicimos, hicisteis, hicieron.
spa|15|l30-5|9-10|The U-Stem Group — Tener, estar, poder and poner take u-stems (tuv-, estuv-, pud-, pus-) and the endings -e, -iste, -o, -imos, -isteis, -ieron.
spa|15|l30-6|9-10|The I-Stem Group: Vine and Quise — Venir and querer take i-stems in the preterite: vine, vino, vinieron and quise, quiso, quisieron.
spa|15|l30-7|9-10|Decir and the J-Stem — Decir and traer take j-stems, and their ellos form drops the i: dijeron and trajeron.
spa|15|l30-8|9-10|I to Y: Leyó and Creyó — In leer, creer and oír, the i of -ió and -ieron becomes y between vowels: leyó, leyeron, creyó, oyó.
spa|15|l30-9|9-10|Stem-Changing -ir Verbs: Durmió and Pidió — Stem-changing -ir verbs change in the él and ellos preterite only: o→u (durmió) and e→i (pidió).
spa|15|l30-10|9-10|Telling the Whole Trip — A good story mixes regular and irregular preterites, so check each verb's family as you write.
spa|16|l31-1|9-10|The -aba Endings — Regular -ar verbs form the imperfect by dropping -ar and adding -aba, -abas, -aba, -ábamos, -abais, -aban.
spa|16|l31-2|9-10|The -ía Endings — Regular -er and -ir verbs share one set of imperfect endings: -ía, -ías, -ía, -íamos, -íais, -ían.
spa|16|l31-3|9-10|Only Three Irregulars — Only ser, ir and ver are irregular in the imperfect: era, iba and veía.
spa|16|l31-4|9-10|Había: There Was, There Were — The imperfect of hay is había, which means both there was and there were.
spa|16|l31-5|9-10|Used To and Would — The imperfect tells what someone used to do or would do again and again in the past.
spa|16|l31-6|9-10|Describing People and Places — Use the imperfect to describe how people, places and things were in the past.
spa|16|l31-7|9-10|Age, Time and Weather — Age, clock time and weather in the past are background facts, so they use the imperfect.
spa|16|l31-8|9-10|Cuando Era Niño o Niña — Cuando era niño or cuando era niña (when I was a child) opens a memory and sets up the imperfect.
spa|16|l31-9|9-10|Soler: Usually Did — Soler in the imperfect plus an infinitive means used to or usually did something.
spa|16|l31-10|9-10|Feelings and States in the Past — How someone felt or what they thought or wanted in the past is usually background, so it goes in the imperfect.
spa|16|l32-1|9-10|The Stage and the Plot — The imperfect sets the scene in the background, and the preterite moves the story forward with completed actions.
spa|16|l32-2|9-10|Interrupted Actions — An action in progress takes the imperfect, and the action that interrupts it takes the preterite.
spa|16|l32-3|9-10|Two Things at Once — Two actions going on at the same time in the past both take the imperfect, often joined by mientras.
spa|16|l32-4|9-10|Clues for the Preterite — Words that point to one moment or a closed period, like ayer or de repente, usually signal the preterite.
spa|16|l32-5|9-10|Clues for the Imperfect — Words for habits, ongoing time and background, like siempre, mientras and de niño, usually signal the imperfect.
spa|16|l32-6|9-10|Same Verb, Two Stories — Switching one verb between preterite and imperfect changes what the sentence tells the listener.
spa|16|l32-7|9-10|Conocer and Saber — In the preterite, conocer means met for the first time and saber means found out.
spa|16|l32-8|9-10|Querer and Poder — In the preterite, quise means I tried, no quise means I refused, pude means I managed, and no pude means I failed.
spa|16|l32-9|9-10|Retelling a Fable — A retold fable opens with imperfect background, moves with preterite events and ends with a lesson.
spa|16|l32-10|9-10|La Llorona in Outline — A legend like La Llorona uses the imperfect for its eerie setting and the preterite for the events that made the legend.
spa|17|l33-1|11-12|Finding the Direct Object — The direct object receives the action, and Spanish replaces it with lo, la, los or las.
spa|17|l33-2|11-12|Where the Pronoun Goes — A direct object pronoun goes right before the conjugated verb, and no before the pronoun.
spa|17|l33-3|11-12|Attached to Infinitives and Gerunds — With an infinitive or a gerund the pronoun may attach to the end, and a gerund then needs a written accent.
spa|17|l33-4|11-12|To Whom and For Whom — The indirect object pronouns le and les mark the person who receives something, and Spanish usually adds them even when the noun is present.
spa|17|l33-5|11-12|Two Pronouns Together — When a sentence has both pronouns, the indirect one comes first, and le or les becomes se before lo, la, los or las.
spa|17|l33-6|11-12|Double Pronouns on the End — Two pronouns can attach together to an infinitive or gerund, and the longer word always takes a written accent.
spa|17|l33-7|11-12|Doing It to Yourself — A reflexive verb sends the action back to the subject, and its pronoun (me, te, se, nos, os, se) must match the subject.
spa|17|l33-8|11-12|The Daily Routine — Spanish tells a routine with a chain of reflexive verbs and uses the definite article, not a possessive, for parts of the body and clothes.
spa|17|l33-9|11-12|Reflexive or Not? — The same verb is reflexive when the action stays with the subject and not reflexive when it goes to someone else.
spa|17|l33-10|11-12|Each Other — With a plural subject, nos, os and se can mean each other, and context or el uno al otro makes it clear.
spa|17|l34-1|11-12|Borrowing the Él Form — An affirmative tú command is the same as the present-tense él/ella form of the verb.
spa|17|l34-2|11-12|The Eight Irregulars — Eight common verbs have short one-syllable tú commands: di, haz, ve, pon, sal, sé, ten, ven.
spa|17|l34-3|11-12|Pronouns on the End — Object and reflexive pronouns attach to the end of an affirmative command, and an accent keeps the stress in place.
spa|17|l34-4|11-12|Flip the Vowel — A negative tú command comes from the yo form: drop the -o, add -es for -ar verbs and -as for -er and -ir verbs.
spa|17|l34-5|11-12|Pronouns Move to the Front — In a negative command every pronoun goes between no and the verb, never on the end.
spa|17|l34-6|11-12|Polite Commands — Usted and ustedes commands use the flipped-vowel form for both affirmative and negative: hable, coma, hablen, coman.
spa|17|l34-7|11-12|Let's Do It — Nosotros commands mean let's and end in -emos or -amos, with vamos as the everyday form for let's go.
spa|17|l34-8|11-12|The Whole Command Map — All commands except affirmative tú use the flipped-vowel form, and pronouns attach only to affirmative commands.
spa|17|l34-9|11-12|Reading a Recipe — Written recipes in Spanish use usted commands or infinitives, while a person coaching you out loud uses tú commands.
spa|17|l34-10|11-12|Giving Directions — Directions to a stranger use usted commands like siga, doble and cruce, plus a few fixed place words.
spa|18|l35-1|11-12|Two Moods for One Verb — Tense tells when something happens; mood tells how the speaker sees it. The indicative reports, and the subjunctive wishes, doubts or reacts.
spa|18|l35-2|11-12|The Two-Clause Pattern — The subjunctive almost always appears in a second clause after que, when the subject of that clause is different from the subject of the first.
spa|18|l35-3|11-12|Start from the Yo Form — To form the present subjunctive, take the present-tense yo form, drop the -o, and add the opposite vowel endings: -e for -ar verbs, -a for -er and -ir verbs.
spa|18|l35-4|11-12|Stem Changes and Spelling Changes — Stem-changing verbs keep their change in the subjunctive wherever the indicative has it, -ir verbs add a second change in nosotros and vosotros, and -car, -gar and -zar verbs adjust their spelling to keep the sound.
spa|18|l35-5|11-12|The Six Irregulars — Six common verbs do not follow the yo-form rule in the present subjunctive: sea, esté, vaya, haya, sepa and dé.
spa|18|l35-6|11-12|Wishes and Wants — Verbs of wanting, hoping and needing, such as querer, esperar, desear and necesitar, trigger the subjunctive when a que clause has a new subject.
spa|18|l35-7|11-12|Emotions and Impersonal Expressions — Reactions such as me alegra que and me molesta que, and judgments such as es importante que and es necesario que, trigger the subjunctive in the que clause.
spa|18|l35-8|11-12|Recommendations and Requests — Verbs of advising, asking and telling, such as recomendar, sugerir, pedir and decir, trigger the subjunctive when someone is told what to do.
spa|18|l35-9|11-12|Doubt and Ojalá — Expressions of doubt or denial, and the word ojalá, trigger the subjunctive; expressions of belief and certainty keep the indicative.
spa|18|l35-10|11-12|When Not to Use It — No change of subject means an infinitive, and a main clause that reports or believes means the indicative; the subjunctive is only for a que clause that wishes, doubts or reacts about a different subject.
spa|18|l35-11|11-12|Writing Back to Lucía — A letter of advice combines wishes, emotions, recommendations and hopes, each one built as main clause, que, new subject, subjunctive.
spa|18|l36-1|11-12|Dudo que and Creo que — Doubt and denial trigger the subjunctive, while belief and certainty keep the indicative, so the same sentence can flip moods when no is added.
spa|18|l36-2|11-12|Questions, Maybe and Perhaps — Questions with creer can take either mood depending on what the speaker expects, and quizás and tal vez take the subjunctive when the speaker is unsure.
spa|18|l36-3|11-12|Reacting to What Happens — Verbs of emotion such as alegrarse de que, sentir que and temer que take the subjunctive in the que clause, even when the event is real.
spa|18|l36-4|11-12|So That, Before, Unless — Some connecting words always take the subjunctive because what follows them has not happened yet: para que, antes de que, sin que, a menos que, con tal de que and en caso de que.
spa|18|l36-5|11-12|When, Until and As Soon As — Time conjunctions such as cuando, hasta que and en cuanto take the subjunctive when the event is still in the future, and the indicative when it is habitual or already happened.
spa|18|l36-6|11-12|Building the -ra Forms — The imperfect subjunctive comes from the preterite ellos form: drop -ron, add -ra, -ras, -ra, -ramos, -rais, -ran, with an accent on the nosotros form.
spa|18|l36-7|11-12|Past Triggers, Past Subjunctive — When the trigger verb is in a past tense, the subjunctive after que shifts to the imperfect subjunctive: quiero que vengas becomes quería que vinieras.
spa|18|l36-8|11-12|The Conditional — The conditional, formed by adding -ía endings to the whole infinitive, means would, and it shares its irregular stems with the future tense.
spa|18|l36-9|11-12|Si Tuviera, Viajaría — A contrary-to-fact if-sentence uses si plus the imperfect subjunctive for the condition and the conditional for the result: Si tuviera dinero, viajaría.
spa|18|l36-10|11-12|Real If and Imagined If — Si with the present indicative describes a real possibility and pairs with the present or future; si with the imperfect subjunctive describes something imagined and pairs with the conditional.
spa|19|l37-1|11-12|Twenty Countries and an Island — Spanish is an official language in twenty countries on three continents, plus Puerto Rico, and each place speaks its own standard form of the language.
spa|19|l37-2|11-12|Spanish in the United States — Spanish has been spoken in what is now the United States since the 1500s, and today tens of millions of Americans speak it at home.
spa|19|l37-3|11-12|Spanish in Illinois and Chicago — More than two million Illinois residents are Hispanic or Latino, and Chicago neighborhoods like Pilsen, Little Village and Humboldt Park carry Spanish in their streets, murals and food.
spa|19|l37-4|11-12|Vos: Another Way to Say You — In Argentina, Uruguay, Paraguay and much of Central America, vos replaces tú, and it has its own present-tense endings: hablás, comés, vivís, sos.
spa|19|l37-5|11-12|Vosotros in Spain — Spain uses vosotros and vosotras for an informal group of people you would each call tú; everywhere else, ustedes covers every group.
spa|19|l37-6|11-12|The Sounds of s, c and z — In most of Spain, c before e or i and z sound like the th in think; in Latin America, the Canary Islands and parts of Andalusia they sound like s, and neither way is wrong.
spa|19|l37-7|11-12|How Do You Say Bus? — Everyday objects often have a different word in each country: guagua, camión, colectivo and autobús are all one bus, and none of them is wrong.
spa|19|l37-8|11-12|Food and Everyday Things — Food words vary the most from country to country: jugo or zumo, aguacate or palta, frijoles or porotos, papa or patata.
spa|19|l37-9|11-12|Understanding Across Regions — When a word is new, use context, ask ¿Cómo se dice…?, and remember that speakers adjust toward a shared, neutral Spanish when they talk across borders.
spa|19|l37-10|11-12|Arabic in Spanish — Nearly eight centuries of Arabic-speaking rule in Spain left Spanish with thousands of words, many beginning with al-: almohada, alfombra, azúcar, aceite, and even ojalá.
spa|19|l37-11|11-12|Nahuatl Words — Nahuatl, the language of the Aztecs and still spoken in Mexico today, gave Spanish and then English words like chocolate, tomate, aguacate and chile.
spa|19|l37-12|11-12|Taíno Words from the Caribbean — Taíno, the language of the Caribbean islands Columbus reached first, gave Spanish and English words like huracán, canoa, hamaca, maíz and barbacoa.
spa|19|l38-1|11-12|Tú, Usted and Vos — Spanish has more than one word for you; tú and vos are for people you are close to, and usted is for people you show respect or distance to.
spa|19|l38-2|11-12|Titles and Names — Señor, señora, don and doña, and professional titles like licenciado and doctora go with usted, and they are written in lowercase except when abbreviated.
spa|19|l38-3|11-12|Switching Register Mid-Conversation — When someone says puedes tutearme, every usted form in your speech shifts to tú: está becomes estás, su becomes tu, le becomes te.
spa|19|l38-4|11-12|The Shape of a Formal Letter — A formal Spanish letter opens with Estimado or Estimada plus a title and a colon, states its purpose in the first sentence, and closes with Atentamente.
spa|19|l38-5|11-12|Saying What You Offer — The body of an application uses short, confident sentences about experience, skills and availability, all in the usted register and without slang.
spa|19|l38-6|11-12|Informal Messages and Texting — Informal Spanish messages open with Hola or ¿Qué tal?, close with un abrazo or nos vemos, and in texts shorten words: q, xq, tqm, bn.
spa|19|l38-7|11-12|Softening a Request — Spanish makes a request more polite by moving from puede to podría, from quiero to quisiera, and by adding por favor and ¿sería posible…?
spa|19|l38-8|11-12|Elders, Teachers and Employers — With elders, teachers and employers, usted plus a title, a greeting by time of day, and small courtesies like con permiso and mande show respect.
spa|19|l38-9|11-12|False Friends — False friends look like English words but mean something else: embarazada is pregnant, éxito is success, carpeta is a folder.
spa|19|l38-10|11-12|Signs on the Street — Public signs use short formal Spanish: usted commands, the impersonal se (se vende, se prohíbe), and a few fixed words like salida, empuje and tire.
spa|19|l38-11|11-12|Reading a Menu and Ordering — A Spanish menu is organized into entradas, platos principales, postres and bebidas, and ordering politely uses quisiera, me trae and ¿me podría traer la cuenta?
spa|19|l38-12|11-12|Reading News Headlines — Spanish headlines drop articles, use the present tense for past events, and pack a whole story into a few formal words.
spa|2|l3-1|k-2|Counting One to Five — Uno, dos, tres, cuatro, cinco are one to five in Spanish.
spa|2|l3-2|k-2|Counting Six to Ten — Seis, siete, ocho, nueve, diez are six to ten in Spanish.
spa|2|l3-3|k-2|Once to Quince — Once, doce, trece, catorce, quince are eleven to fifteen.
spa|2|l3-4|k-2|Dieciséis to Veinte — Sixteen to nineteen are built from diez y (ten and) plus a number.
spa|2|l3-5|k-2|Asking ¿Cuántos? — ¿Cuántos? means how many? Answer with a number.
spa|2|l3-6|k-2|Hay: There Is, There Are — Hay means there is or there are. The h is silent.
spa|2|l4-1|k-2|Red, Blue, Yellow — Rojo is red, azul is blue, and amarillo is yellow.
spa|2|l4-2|k-2|Green, Orange, Purple — Verde is green, anaranjado is orange, and morado is purple.
spa|2|l4-3|k-2|Pink, Black, White — Rosado is pink, negro is black, and blanco is white.
spa|2|l4-4|k-2|Brown: Café or Marrón — Brown can be café or marrón. Both are right.
spa|2|l4-5|k-2|Asking ¿De Qué Color Es? — ¿De qué color es? means what color is it?
spa|2|l4-6|k-2|The Color Comes After — In Spanish, the color word comes after the thing: la manzana roja.
spa|20|l39-1|11-12|Present and Present Perfect — The present tense states what is happening or what is usually true, while the present perfect (he hablado) reports what has happened up to now.
spa|20|l39-2|11-12|Preterite Beside Imperfect — The preterite reports a completed event, the imperfect paints background and habit, and a longer passage needs both working together.
spa|20|l39-3|11-12|Future and Conditional — The future (hablaré) and the conditional (hablaría) share one stem and one set of irregulars; the future says what will happen, the conditional what would happen.
spa|20|l39-4|11-12|Triggers and the Present Subjunctive — A trigger phrase plus que plus a change of subject sends the second verb into the present subjunctive, built from the yo form with the opposite vowel.
spa|20|l39-5|11-12|Past Subjunctive and If — The imperfect subjunctive comes from the ellos preterite with -ra endings, follows past triggers, and pairs with the conditional in imagined if-sentences.
spa|20|l39-6|11-12|Choosing the Form from Context — Before picking a verb form, ask three questions in order: when, is it finished or background, and is it a fact or a wish, doubt or judgment.
spa|20|l39-7|11-12|Contrast Connectors — Sin embargo, en cambio and aunque all signal contrast, but each one joins ideas in a different shape and aunque can change the mood of the verb after it.
spa|20|l39-8|11-12|Adding and Concluding — Además adds a point, por lo tanto draws a conclusion, and a connector should only appear where the logic between two ideas truly exists.
spa|20|l39-9|11-12|The Error Checklist — Most essay errors fall into five families, and checking for one family at a time catches more than reading for everything at once.
spa|20|l39-10|11-12|Reading a Longer Passage — In a real passage, the verb forms and connectors do work you can trace; reading for them is how you learn to write them.
spa|20|l39-11|11-12|A Routine for Self-Correction — Editing your own writing works when it is a fixed routine: one pass per error family, read aloud, then check the connectors last.
spa|20|l40-1|11-12|A Topic You Can Stand Behind — A good presentation topic about the Spanish-speaking world is narrow enough to cover in five minutes and personal enough that you have something to say.
spa|20|l40-2|11-12|Writing the Thesis — A thesis is one sentence that takes a position, opened with a phrase like En mi opinión or Sostengo que, and everything else in the talk exists to support it.
spa|20|l40-3|11-12|Three Supports with Evidence — Each of three supporting reasons needs its own piece of evidence, a story, a number or an example, and the number must come from a source you can name.
spa|20|l40-4|11-12|Transitions Between Parts — Transitions such as en primer lugar, en segundo lugar and por último tell the audience where they are in the talk, so they can follow without a page in front of them.
spa|20|l40-5|11-12|The Conclusion — A conclusion restates the thesis in new words, gathers the three reasons in one breath, and ends with a line the audience will carry out of the room.
spa|20|l40-6|11-12|Notes, Not a Script — Index cards with a few key words per part let you speak to your audience; a full script makes you read to a page.
spa|20|l40-7|11-12|Voice, Pace and Pauses — Speak more slowly than feels natural, pause after each transition, and replace English fillers with Spanish ones or with silence.
spa|20|l40-8|11-12|Answering Questions — A good answer repeats the question, replies in two or three sentences, and links back to the thesis; not knowing is allowed if you say so honestly.
spa|20|l40-9|11-12|Disagreeing Politely — Defending a thesis means acknowledging the other view first, then holding your ground with a reason, using phrases like Entiendo su punto, pero…
spa|20|l40-10|11-12|Reading the Rubric — A rubric names what the grader is listening for, so reading it before you build the talk tells you where to spend your time.
spa|20|l40-11|11-12|Scoring a Classmate — Scoring a classmate's rehearsal against the rubric, with one comment per row in Spanish, sharpens your ear for your own talk.
spa|3|l5-1|k-2|Mom, Dad and the Baby — La mamá is mom, el papá is dad, and el bebé is the baby.
spa|3|l5-2|k-2|Brothers, Sisters, Grandparents — A boy is el hermano, a girl is la hermana.
spa|3|l5-3|k-2|The Dog and the Cat — El perro is the dog, and el gato is the cat.
spa|3|l5-4|k-2|The Fish and the Bird — El pez is the fish, and el pájaro is the bird.
spa|3|l5-5|k-2|El and La Mean The — El and la both mean the. Each name has its own one.
spa|3|l5-6|k-2|Un, Una and Mi — Un and una mean a. Mi means my.
spa|3|l6-1|k-2|Book, Table, Chair — El libro is the book. La mesa is the table. La silla is the chair.
spa|3|l6-2|k-2|The Pencil — El lápiz is the pencil. It ends in z, not -o.
spa|3|l6-3|k-2|Door and Window — La puerta is the door. La ventana is the window.
spa|3|l6-4|k-2|The Bed — La cama is the bed. You sleep in la cama.
spa|3|l6-5|k-2|The -O and -A Clue — Most -o words go with el. Most -a words go with la.
spa|3|l6-6|k-2|Two Rule-Breakers — Día ends in -a but is el día. Mano ends in -o but is la mano.
spa|4|l7-1|k-2|One Cat, Two Cats — When a word ends in a vowel, add -s to mean more than one.
spa|4|l7-2|k-2|Counting Plural Things — After a number bigger than one, the thing is plural: tres perros.
spa|4|l7-3|k-2|One Flower, Many Flowers — When a word ends in a consonant, add -es: flor becomes flores.
spa|4|l7-4|k-2|Z Changes to C — When a word ends in z, change z to c and add -es: lápiz becomes lápices.
spa|4|l7-5|k-2|El Becomes Los — El means the for one thing. Los means the for more than one.
spa|4|l7-6|k-2|La Becomes Las — La means the for one thing. Las means the for more than one.
spa|4|l8-1|k-2|Grande and Pequeño — Grande means big. Pequeño means little.
spa|4|l8-2|k-2|Alto and Bajo — Alto means tall. Bajo means short.
spa|4|l8-3|k-2|Feliz and Triste — Feliz means happy. Triste means sad.
spa|4|l8-4|k-2|The Describing Word Goes After — In Spanish, the describing word comes after the thing: el perro grande.
spa|4|l8-5|k-2|Matching -o and -a — Describing words ending in -o change to -a to match la words.
spa|4|l8-6|k-2|Many Big Dogs — When the thing is plural, the describing word gets -s or -es too.
spa|5|l9-1|3-5|Every Sentence Has Two Parts — A sentence needs someone (the subject) and something that person does (the verb).
spa|5|l9-2|3-5|Yo, Tú, Él, Ella — Yo means I, tú means you (a friend), él means he and ella means she.
spa|5|l9-3|3-5|Nosotros and Ellos — Nosotros means we, and ellos means they when you talk about a group.
spa|5|l9-4|3-5|Hablar Step by Step — To use an -ar verb, drop -ar and add an ending: -o for yo, -as for tú, -a for él or ella.
spa|5|l9-5|3-5|Endings for Groups — For groups, -ar verbs take -amos for nosotros and -an for ellos or ellas.
spa|5|l9-6|3-5|Dropping Yo — Spanish speakers often leave out yo, tú or nosotros because the verb ending already tells who.
spa|5|l9-7|3-5|Bailar and Cantar — Bailar (to dance) and cantar (to sing) follow the same pattern as hablar.
spa|5|l9-8|3-5|Caminar Around the City — Caminar (to walk) is one more regular -ar verb you can use to tell where people go.
spa|5|l9-9|3-5|Where the Words Go — Spanish usually puts the subject, then the verb, then the rest, but it can move words more freely than English.
spa|5|l9-10|3-5|Telling a Game in Sentences — Short sentences with a subject and a conjugated verb can tell a whole story.
spa|5|l10-1|3-5|No Goes Before the Verb — To make a Spanish sentence negative, put no right in front of the verb.
spa|5|l10-2|3-5|No, No: The Double No — In an answer, the first no answers the question and the second no makes the verb negative.
spa|5|l10-3|3-5|Sí and Si — Sí with an accent mark means yes; si without it means if.
spa|5|l10-4|3-5|Let Your Voice Go Up — You can turn a Spanish sentence into a yes-or-no question by raising your voice at the end.
spa|5|l10-5|3-5|The Upside-Down Question Mark — Written Spanish questions start with ¿ and end with ?, and exclamations start with ¡ and end with !.
spa|5|l10-6|3-5|Answer in a Full Sentence — A full answer repeats the verb with the ending for the person who answers.
spa|5|l10-7|3-5|Me Gusta: I Like — Me gusta plus a thing or an action means I like it: me gusta el fútbol, me gusta bailar.
spa|5|l10-8|3-5|No Me Gusta — To say you don't like something, put no in front: no me gusta.
spa|5|l10-9|3-5|¿Te Gusta? Asking a Friend — To ask a friend if they like something, say ¿Te gusta…? and they answer with me gusta.
spa|5|l10-10|3-5|Playing Twenty Questions — With sí, no, rising questions and me gusta, you can play a whole guessing game in Spanish.
spa|6|l11-1|3-5|Soy: Saying Who I Am — Soy means I am, and you use it to say your name and who you are.
spa|6|l11-2|3-5|Eres and Es: You, He, She — Use eres for you (a friend) and es for he, she or it.
spa|6|l11-3|3-5|Somos and Son: Groups — Use somos for we are and son for they are or you all are.
spa|6|l11-4|3-5|Soy de Chicago — Ser plus de tells where someone is from: Soy de Chicago.
spa|6|l11-5|3-5|Nationality Words — Nationality words are lowercase in Spanish and change -o to -a for a girl.
spa|6|l11-6|3-5|Asking ¿De dónde eres? — Ask ¿De dónde eres? and answer with Soy de and a place.
spa|6|l11-7|3-5|Describing with Ser — Ser plus a describing word tells what someone is like: Soy alto.
spa|6|l11-8|3-5|Jobs with Ser — Use ser to say someone's job, and leave out un or una: Es maestra.
spa|6|l11-9|3-5|Describing a Friend — Put ser, where someone is from and what they are like together to describe a friend.
spa|6|l12-1|3-5|Estoy: How I Am Right Now — Estoy means I am, and you use it for how you feel right now.
spa|6|l12-2|3-5|The Five Forms of Estar — Estar has five forms here: estoy, estás, está, estamos, están.
spa|6|l12-3|3-5|¿Cómo estás? — ¿Cómo estás? asks how someone feels, and you answer with estoy.
spa|6|l12-4|3-5|Words for Feelings — Feeling words like contento, triste, cansado and nervioso go with estar.
spa|6|l12-5|3-5|Matching -o and -a — Feeling words end in -o for a boy, -a for a girl, and add -s for a group.
spa|6|l12-6|3-5|Estoy en la escuela — Estar plus en tells where someone or something is right now.
spa|6|l12-7|3-5|¿Dónde está? — Ask ¿Dónde está? to find where someone or something is.
spa|6|l12-8|3-5|The Rule of Thumb — How you feel and where you are use estar; who you are and what you are like use ser.
spa|6|l12-9|3-5|Choosing Ser or Estar — Ask who or what like for ser, and how or where for estar.
spa|7|l13-1|3-5|¿Quién? Asks Who — ¿Quién? means who, and the answer is always a person.
spa|7|l13-2|3-5|¿Qué? Asks What — ¿Qué? means what, and it asks about a thing or an action.
spa|7|l13-3|3-5|¿Cómo? Asks How — ¿Cómo? means how, and it asks what something is like or how someone is.
spa|7|l13-4|3-5|¿Dónde? Asks Where — ¿Dónde? means where, and the answer is a place.
spa|7|l13-5|3-5|¿Cuándo? Asks When — ¿Cuándo? means when, and the answer is a time, a day or a date.
spa|7|l13-6|3-5|¿Cuánto? Asks How Much — ¿Cuánto? asks how much, and ¿cuántos? or ¿cuántas? asks how many.
spa|7|l13-7|3-5|¿Por qué? and Porque — ¿Por qué? (two words, accent) asks why, and porque (one word, no accent) answers because.
spa|7|l13-8|3-5|The Accent That Marks a Question — Every question word carries a written accent, and the accent helps you spot a question.
spa|7|l13-9|3-5|Ask About Any Part — You can ask about any piece of a sentence by swapping that piece for a question word.
spa|7|l14-1|3-5|¿Cómo te llamas? — ¿Cómo te llamas? asks a friend's name, and you answer Me llamo and your name.
spa|7|l14-2|3-5|¿Cuántos años tienes? — Spanish uses tener (to have) for age: Tengo nueve años means I am nine.
spa|7|l14-3|3-5|¿Dónde vives? — ¿Dónde vives? asks where you live, and you answer Vivo en and a place.
spa|7|l14-4|3-5|Listen for the Question Word — The question word, heard first, tells you what kind of answer to give.
spa|7|l14-5|3-5|Answer in a Full Sentence — A full-sentence answer repeats the verb from the question with a new ending.
spa|7|l14-6|3-5|Asking Back: ¿Y tú? — After you answer, ask ¿Y tú? (And you?) to keep the conversation going.
spa|7|l14-7|3-5|Por favor and Gracias — Por favor means please and gracias means thank you; they make any request polite.
spa|7|l14-8|3-5|De nada and Perdón — De nada answers gracias, and perdón says sorry or excuse me.
spa|7|l14-9|3-5|Putting an Interview Together — A good interview has a greeting, a few clear questions, full answers and a polite thank-you.
spa|8|l15-1|3-5|Primero and Luego — Primero means first and luego means then; they start the first and next steps of a day.
spa|8|l15-2|3-5|Después and Finalmente — Después means after that and finalmente means finally; finalmente marks the last step.
spa|8|l15-3|3-5|Putting Steps in Order — Use primero, luego, después and finalmente in that order to tell four steps clearly.
spa|8|l15-4|3-5|Morning, Afternoon and Night — La mañana is the morning, la tarde is the afternoon and la noche is the night.
spa|8|l15-5|3-5|The Seven Days — The days are lunes, martes, miércoles, jueves, viernes, sábado and domingo, written with small letters.
spa|8|l15-6|3-5|My Week, Day by Day — Put a day at the start of a sentence to tell what you do on that day.
spa|8|l15-7|3-5|Verbs for My Day — Como, voy, juego and leo tell what I do; the -o ending means I.
spa|8|l15-8|3-5|It Is One O'Clock — Ask ¿Qué hora es? and answer Es la una for one o'clock.
spa|8|l15-9|3-5|Two, Three and More O'Clock — For two o'clock and later, say Son las and the number: Son las tres.
spa|8|l16-1|3-5|Joining with Y — Y means and; it joins two words or two short sentences into one.
spa|8|l16-2|3-5|Changing Direction with Pero — Pero means but; it joins two ideas that go in different directions.
spa|8|l16-3|3-5|Adding More with También — También means also or too; it adds one more matching idea.
spa|8|l16-4|3-5|Beginning, Middle and End — A good short story has a beginning that sets the scene, a middle with a problem and an end.
spa|8|l16-5|3-5|Four to Six Sentences — A short story in Spanish can be four to six sentences, each one a clear step.
spa|8|l16-6|3-5|A Comic Strip in Spanish — A comic strip tells a story in boxes; each box can hold one short Spanish sentence.
spa|8|l16-7|3-5|Check Every Verb Ending — The verb ending must match who does the action: -o for yo, -as or -es for tú, -a or -e for él and ella.
spa|8|l16-8|3-5|Check Nouns and Describing Words — A describing word matches its noun: la mochila roja, el libro rojo, los libros rojos.
spa|8|l16-9|3-5|Read It Aloud — Reading your story aloud helps you hear missing words, wrong endings and choppy sentences.
spa|9|l17-1|6-8|Buenos Días — Say buenos días (good morning) from when you wake up until about noon.
spa|9|l17-2|6-8|Buenas Tardes and Buenas Noches — Say buenas tardes in the afternoon and buenas noches at night, both as hello and as goodbye.
spa|9|l17-3|6-8|Hola and ¿Qué Tal? — Hola is a friendly hi for any time of day, and ¿Qué tal? asks how things are going.
spa|9|l17-4|6-8|Answering ¿Cómo Estás? — Answer ¿Cómo estás? with bien, muy bien, más o menos or mal, and ask back with ¿y tú?
spa|9|l17-5|6-8|Adiós and Hasta Luego — Adiós means goodbye, and hasta luego means see you later.
spa|9|l17-6|6-8|Hasta Mañana and Chao — Hasta mañana means see you tomorrow, and chao is a casual bye among friends.
spa|9|l17-7|6-8|Choosing the Right Goodbye — Pick a goodbye by asking when you will meet again and how close you are to the person.
spa|9|l17-8|6-8|Tú Versus Usted — Use tú with friends, family and kids, and usted with adults you should show respect to.
spa|9|l17-9|6-8|Handshakes and Cheek Kisses — Many Spanish speakers greet with a touch: a handshake, a hug or a light kiss on the cheek.
spa|9|l17-10|6-8|Te Presento a… and Mucho Gusto — Introduce someone with te presento a (informal) or le presento a (formal), and answer mucho gusto.
spa|9|l18-1|6-8|Twenty-Seven Letters — The Spanish alphabet has 27 letters: the 26 English letters plus ñ.
spa|9|l18-2|6-8|The Names of the Letters — Each Spanish letter has a name, like be, ce, hache and jota, that you use to spell aloud.
spa|9|l18-3|6-8|¿Cómo Se Escribe? — Ask ¿Cómo se escribe? to learn how a word is spelled, and say con acento for an accent mark.
spa|9|l18-4|6-8|Me Llamo and ¿Cómo Te Llamas? — Ask ¿Cómo te llamas? (tú) or ¿Cómo se llama usted? and answer Me llamo plus your name.
spa|9|l18-5|6-8|Two Last Names — Many Spanish speakers traditionally carry two last names: first the father's, then the mother's.
spa|9|l18-6|6-8|Señor, Señora and Señorita — Use señor, señora or señorita with a last name to address adults with respect.
spa|9|l18-7|6-8|¿De Dónde Eres? — Ask ¿De dónde eres? and answer Soy de plus a city or country.
spa|9|l18-8|6-8|Twenty Spanish-Speaking Countries — Spanish is an official language in 20 countries across the Americas, Europe and Africa.
spa|9|l18-9|6-8|Nationalities and Agreement — Nationality words change for gender: mexicano, mexicana; español, española.
spa|9|l18-10|6-8|Accent Marks Change Meaning — A written accent can change a word's meaning: papá is dad, but papa is potato.
